Furthermore,
X
also carries a filtration,
f
and
g
are filtration preserving and the induced maps

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\frac{F_{p}DCI(Y,E)}{F_{p-1}DCI(Y,E)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\frac{F_{p}X}{F_{p-1}X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\frac{F_{p}\widetilde{CI}(Y,E)}{F_{p-1}\widetilde{CI}(Y,E)}}

are quasi-isomorphisms for all
p
.

Remark 2.10
.

The statement in the cited result is slightly weaker. The fact that
the intermediate objects carry a type of index filtration
and that all the quasi-isomorphisms preserve this filtration,
inducing quasi-isomorphisms on the minimal filtration quotients,
is obtained by a close inspection of the quite involved proof.

3.
Binary Polyhedral Spaces and Flat Connections

The purpose of this section is to fix our conventions concerning the
binary polyhedral spaces and to recall the basic representation theory
needed to effectively classify the gauge equivalence classes of flat

\operatorname{SU}(2)
-connections. We also recall the McKay correspondence
between the finite subgroups of
\operatorname{SU}(2)
and the simply laced
extended Dynkin diagrams, that is, the diagrams of type

\widetilde{A}_{n}
,
\widetilde{D_{n}}
,
\widetilde{E}_{6}
,
\widetilde{E}_{7}
or
\widetilde{E}_{8}
. This
surprising relation is vital for our work in the next section and
is essential in the definition of the graphs
\mathcal{S}_{\Gamma}
, given in
the end of the section, mentioned in the introduction.

To avoid making this rather basic section too long we have moved a number
of facts concerning the finite subgroups of
\operatorname{SU}(2)
into Appendix
A
.
This includes their complex representation theory, the corresponding McKay graphs used in the construction of
\mathcal{S}_{\Gamma}
and complete lists of the flat
\operatorname{SU}(2)
-connections over the binary polyhedral spaces.

3.1.
Binary Polyhedral Spaces

We briefly recall the classification of finite subgroups of

\operatorname{SU}(2)
. Let
C_{l}
denote the cyclic
group of order
l
for
l\geq 1
, let
D_{k}
denote
the dihedral group of order
2k
for
k\geq 2
, and let
T
,
O
and
I

denote the subgroups of
\operatorname{SO}(3)
that leave
a regular tetrahedron, octahedron and icosahedron, respectively,
in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
invariant.
T
is called the tetrahedral
group,
O
the octahedral group and
I
the icosahedral
group. Define
D_{k}^{*}
,
T^{*}
,
O^{*}
and
I^{*}
in
\operatorname{SU}(2)
by
pulling back
D_{k}
,
T
,
O
and
I
along the standard double covering
homomorphism
\operatorname{SU}(2)\rightarrow\operatorname{SO}(3)
.
Every cyclic group
C_{l}
may also be realized as
a subgroup of
\operatorname{SU}(2)
by embedding them
in a copy of
U(1)\subset\operatorname{SU}(2)
. We have
thus constructed two infinite families
C_{l}
,
D_{k}^{*}
of subgroups in
\operatorname{SU}(2)
, as well as the three exceptional ones
T^{*}
,
O^{*}
and
I^{*}
.
It is a classical fact that up to conjugacy these
exhaust all the finite subgroups of
\operatorname{SU}(2)
. A proof may be found
in
[
Wol11
, p. 83]
.

Theorem 3.1
.

Let
\Gamma\subset\operatorname{SU}(2)
be
a finite subgroup. Then
\Gamma
is isomorphic to precisely
one of the groups
C_{l}
for
l\geq 1
,
D_{k}^{*}
for
k\geq 2
,
T^{*}
,
O^{*}
or

I^{*}
. Moreover, if two finite subgroups
\Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime}\subset\operatorname{SU}(2)

are isomorphic, then they are conjugate in
\operatorname{SU}(2)
.

Let
\mathbb{C}^{2}
carry the canonical complex orientation and orient

S^{3}\subset\mathbb{C}^{2}
by the outward pointing normal first convention;
that is, an ordered basis
(v_{1},v_{2},v_{3})\in T_{x}S^{3}\subset T_{x}\mathbb{C}^{2}\cong\mathbb{C}^{2}
is positive if and only if
(x,v_{1},v_{2},v_{3})
is a positive basis for
\mathbb{C}^{2}
.
The standard left action of
\operatorname{SU}(2)
on
\mathbb{C}^{2}
restricts to a
transitive and free action by orientation preserving isometries
on the unit sphere
S^{3}\subset\mathbb{C}^{2}
.

Definition 3.2
.

For any finite subgroup

\Gamma\subset\operatorname{SU}(2)
let
\Gamma
act on
S^{3}\subset\mathbb{C}^{2}
by restricting
the standard free action of
\operatorname{SU}(2)
.
Define
Y_{\Gamma}
to be the quotient manifold
S^{3}/\Gamma
equipped with the
Riemannian metric and orientation induced from the standard round metric and
orientation of
S^{3}
. Define
\overline{Y}_{\Gamma}
to be the
same Riemannian manifold equipped with the opposite orientation.
The spaces
Y_{\Gamma}
and
\overline{Y}_{\Gamma}
are called binary polyhedral
spaces.

Note that by the above theorem

Y_{\Gamma}
is determined up to
\operatorname{SU}(2)
-equivariant
isometry by the isomorphism class of
\Gamma
.

Lemma 3.3
.

For
\Gamma\subset\operatorname{SU}(2)
we have

H_{i}(Y_{\Gamma};\mathbb{Z})\cong\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}\mathbb{Z}&\mbox{ for }i=0,3\\
\Gamma^{ab}&\mbox{ for }i=1\\
0&\mbox{ otherwise}\end{array}\right.\;\;\mbox{ and }\;\;H^{i}(Y_{\Gamma};\mathbb{Z})\cong\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}\mathbb{Z}&\mbox{ for }i=0,3\\
\Gamma^{ab}&\mbox{ for }i=2\\
0&\mbox{ otherwise, }\end{array}\right.

where
\Gamma^{ab}=\Gamma/[\Gamma,\Gamma]
is the
abelianization of
\Gamma
. In particular,

H_{*}(Y_{\Gamma};\mathbb{Q})\cong H_{*}(S^{3};\mathbb{Q})
, so the

Y_{\Gamma}
are rational homology
3
-spheres.

Proof.

As
Y_{\Gamma}
is a connected, closed
and orientable the (co)homology is concentrated in degrees
0\leq i\leq 3
and

H_{i}(Y_{\Gamma})\cong H^{i}(Y_{\Gamma})\cong\mathbb{Z}
for

i=0,3
. By construction,
S^{3}
is a universal cover
of
Y_{\Gamma}
so that
\pi_{1}(Y_{\Gamma})\cong\Gamma
and hence
H_{1}(Y_{\Gamma})\cong\Gamma^{ab}
. Since this group is finite it follows by the universal coefficient theorem that
H^{1}(Y_{\Gamma})=0
.
By Poincaré duality we conclude that

H_{2}(Y_{\Gamma})=0
and
H^{2}(Y_{\Gamma})\cong\Gamma^{ab}
.
The final statement follows from the universal coefficient theorem as
H_{1}(Y_{\Gamma};\mathbb{Q})\cong\mathbb{Q}\otimes\Gamma^{ab}=0
.
∎

3.2.
A Few Results from Representation Theory

The framed Floer complex
\widetilde{CI}(Y,E)
associated with the trivial

\operatorname{SU}(2)
-bundle
E\rightarrow Y_{\Gamma}
is generated, in the sense of
Definition
2.4
, by the gauge equivalence classes of the flat connections in
E
. By a well-known result (see for instance
[
Tau11
]
for a proof)
these gauge equivalence classes are in natural bijection with
the equivalence classes of representations

\Gamma=\pi_{1}(Y_{\Gamma})\rightarrow\operatorname{SU}(2)

or in other words, as
\operatorname{SU}(2)\cong\operatorname{Sp}(1)
, the isomorphism classes of

1
-dimensional quaternionic representations of
\Gamma
. It is convenient to express this set in terms of the complex representation theory of
\Gamma
, since in this setting we have the calculational power of
character theory at our disposal. The following
three results may be extracted from
[
BD85
, II.6]
.

Lemma 3.4
.

Let
G
be a finite group. The
set
\operatorname{Irr}(G,\mathbb{C})
of isomorphism classes of
irreducible complex representations admits a decomposition into disjoint subsets

\operatorname{Irr}(G,\mathbb{C})=\operatorname{Irr}(G,\mathbb{C})_{\mathbb{R}}\cup\operatorname{Irr}(G,\mathbb{C})_{\mathbb{C}}\cup\operatorname{Irr}(G,\mathbb{C})_{\mathbb{H}}.

The set
\operatorname{Irr}(G,\mathbb{C})_{\mathbb{C}}
consists of the classes

[V]
where
V\not\cong V^{*}
, while
\operatorname{Irr}(G,\mathbb{C})_{\mathbb{R}}

and
\operatorname{Irr}(G,\mathbb{C})_{\mathbb{H}}
consists of the classes

[W]
admitting a conjugate linear equivariant map

s:W\rightarrow W
satisfying
s^{2}=1
or
s^{2}=-1
, respectively.

An irreducible representation
V
is said to be
of real, complex or quaternionic type according to
whether
[V]\in\operatorname{Irr}(G,\mathbb{C})_{K}
for
K=\mathbb{R},\mathbb{C},\mathbb{H}
,
respectively. There is a simple test for determining
the type of an irreducible complex representation.

Lemma 3.5
.

Let
V
be an irreducible complex representation of the finite group
G
with character

\chi_{V}
. Then

\frac{1}{|G|}\sum_{g\in G}\chi_{V}(g^{2})=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lr}1&\mbox{ if }[V]\in\operatorname{Irr}(G,\mathbb{C})_{\mathbb{R}}\\
0&\mbox{ if }[V]\in\operatorname{Irr}(G,\mathbb{C})_{\mathbb{C}}\\
-1&\mbox{ if }[V]\in\operatorname{Irr}(G,\mathbb{C})_{\mathbb{H}}\end{array}\right.

Finally we recall how the set
\operatorname{Irr}(G,\mathbb{H})
of isomorphism classes of irreducible quaternionic representations may be recovered from
\operatorname{Irr}(G,\mathbb{C})
.
Let
\operatorname{Rep}_{K}(G)
denote the category of finite dimensional
G
-representations over
K=\mathbb{C},\mathbb{H}
. There
is a restriction functor
r\colon\operatorname{Rep}_{\mathbb{H}}(G)\rightarrow\operatorname{Rep}_{\mathbb{C}}(G)
given by pullback along the inclusion
\mathbb{C}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{H}
and an extension functor
e\colon\operatorname{Rep}_{\mathbb{C}}(G)\rightarrow\operatorname{Rep}_{\mathbb{H}}(G)
given by
e(V)=\mathbb{H}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}V
.
Then we have the following result.

Proposition 3.6
.

There is a decomposition into disjoint subsets

\operatorname{Irr}(G,\mathbb{H})=\operatorname{Irr}(G,\mathbb{H})_{\mathbb{R}}\cup\operatorname{Irr}(G,\mathbb{H})_{\mathbb{C}}\cup\operatorname{Irr}(G,\mathbb{H})_{\mathbb{H}}.

Furthermore, the following maps are bijections

\displaystyle r:

\displaystyle\operatorname{Irr}(G,\mathbb{H})_{\mathbb{H}}\rightarrow\operatorname{Irr}(G,\mathbb{C})_{\mathbb{H}}

\displaystyle e:

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{Irr}(G,\mathbb{C})_{\mathbb{C}}\rightarrow\operatorname{Irr}(G,\mathbb{H})_{\mathbb{C}}

\displaystyle e:

\displaystyle\operatorname{Irr}(G,\mathbb{C})_{\mathbb{R}}\rightarrow\operatorname{Irr}(G,\mathbb{H})_{\mathbb{R}},

where
\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{Irr}(G,\mathbb{C})_{\mathbb{C}}
denotes the set
of unordered pairs
\{[V],[V^{*}]\}
for
[V]\in\operatorname{Irr}(G,\mathbb{C})_{\mathbb{C}}
.

Let
\operatorname{Irr}^{n}(G,\mathbb{C})_{K}
denote the set of irreducible

G
-representations of dimension
n
of type
K=\mathbb{R},\mathbb{C},\mathbb{H}
.

Corollary 3.7
.

The set of isomorphism classes of

1
-dimensional quaternionic representations of the
finite group
G
are in bijection with

\operatorname{Irr}^{2}(G,\mathbb{C})_{\mathbb{H}}\cup\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{Irr}^{1}(G,\mathbb{C})_{\mathbb{C}}\cup\operatorname{Irr}^{1}(G,\mathbb{C})_{\mathbb{R}}.

In particular, if
G=\Gamma
is a finite subgroup of

\operatorname{SU}(2)
then the above set is in bijection with
the set of gauge equivalence classes of flat
connections in the trivial
\operatorname{SU}(2)
-bundle
over
Y_{\Gamma}
.

Note that a flat connection is irreducible, reducible or fully reducible
if and only if the associated representation is of quaternionic, complex
or real type, respectively.

3.3.
Arrangement of the Flat Connections

The McKay correspondence
[
McK80
]
sets up a bijection
between the isomorphism classes of the nontrivial finite subgroups of

\operatorname{Sp}(1)\cong\operatorname{SU}(2)
and the simply-laced extended Dynkin diagrams.
The extended Dynkin graph is constructed from the group

\Gamma
as follows. Let
R_{0},R_{1},\cdots,R_{n}
be
a complete set of representatives for the elements of
\operatorname{Irr}(\Gamma,\mathbb{C})
, where we take
R_{0}
to be the trivial
representation. Let
Q
denote the two dimensional representation associated with the inclusion
\Gamma\subset\operatorname{SU}(2)
and define
a matrix
A=(a_{ij})_{ij}
by

Q\otimes R_{i}=\bigoplus_{j=0}^{n}a_{ij}R_{j}\;\;\mbox{ for }\;\;0\leq i\leq n.

One may show that
A
is symmetric with
a_{ii}=0
and

a_{ij}\in\{0,1\}
. Define an unoriented graph
\overline{\Delta}_{\Gamma}

by taking
I=\{0,1,\cdots,n\}
as the set of vertices and
an edge connecting
i
to
j
if and only if

a_{ij}=a_{ji}=1
. This graph will then be an extended Dynkin diagram
of type
\widetilde{A}_{n}
,
\widetilde{D}_{n}
,
\widetilde{E}_{6}
,
\widetilde{E}_{7}
or
\widetilde{E}_{8}
. Furthermore, if we let
\Delta_{\Gamma}
be the graph obtained from
\overline{\Delta}_{\Gamma}

by deleting the vertex
0
corresponding to the trivial representation
we obtain the underlying Dynkin diagram of type
A_{n}
,
D_{n}
,
E_{6}
,
E_{7}
or
E_{8}
. The precise correspondence is given in the following table where

l,k\geq 2
.

\begin{array}[]{|c|c|c|c|c|c|}\hline\cr\Gamma&C_{l}&D^{*}_{k}&T^{*}&O^{*}&I^{*}\\
\hline\cr\Delta_{\Gamma}&A_{l-1}&D_{k+2}&E_{6}&E_{7}&E_{8}\\
\hline\cr\end{array}

The set of vertices, say
\{1,2,\cdots,n\}
, in a Dynkin diagram corresponds
to a set of simple roots
\{r_{i}:1\leq i\leq n\}
in the associated root system. There is then a unique
maximal positive root
r_{\max}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}d_{i}r_{i}
where
d_{i}\in\mathbb{N}

are positive integers. The final fact we wish to bring out is that these integers are determined by
d_{i}=\operatorname{dim}_{\mathbb{C}}R_{i}
.

By mapping an irreducible representation
R_{i}
to its dual representation

R_{i}^{*}
we obtain an involution
\iota\colon\{0,1,\cdots,n\}\rightarrow\{0,1,\cdots,n\}
,
i.e.,
R_{\iota(i)}\cong R_{i}^{*}
. This map extends to a graph involution of

\overline{\Delta}_{\Gamma}
as

a_{\iota(i)\iota(j)}=\operatorname{dim}_{\mathbb{C}}\operatorname{Hom}_{\Gamma}(Q\otimes R_{i}^{*},R_{j}^{*})=\operatorname{dim}_{\mathbb{C}}\operatorname{Hom}_{\Gamma}(Q\otimes R_{j},R_{i})=a_{ji}=a_{ij}

for all
0\leq i,j\leq n
. In view of Lemma
3.4
we see that
the fixed points of
\iota
correspond to the vertices of quaternionic or real type, while the nontrivial orbits are pairs
\{i,j\}
for which
i\neq j
and
R_{i}
,
R_{j}\cong R_{i}^{*}
are of complex type. The set of vertices in the quotient graph

\overline{\Delta}_{\Gamma}/(\iota)
may therefore by
Proposition
3.6
be identified with

\operatorname{Irr}(\Gamma,\mathbb{H})
. In particular, we may identify our set

\mathcal{C}
of flat connections with a subset of the vertices in this quotient
graph. The following may be observed from the quotient graphs
given in Appendix
A
.

Lemma 3.8
.

For each finite subgroup
\Gamma\subset\operatorname{SU}(2)
the
quotient graph
\overline{\Delta}_{\Gamma}/(\iota)
is a connected tree, except
for
\Gamma=C_{l}
with
l
odd in which case it has the form

From the above lemma it follows that for each pair of vertices there
is a unique minimal edge path connecting them.

Definition 3.9
.

[
Aus95
, p. 297]

Let
\Gamma\subset\operatorname{SU}(2)
be a finite subgroup.
Define a graph
\mathcal{S}_{\Gamma}
by letting the vertices be the set
of
1
-dimensional quaternionic representations of
\Gamma
and an
(unoriented) edge connecting a pair of distinct vertices
\alpha
and
\beta

if and only if the minimal edge path connecting
\alpha
to
\beta

in
\overline{\Delta}_{\Gamma}/(\iota)
does not pass through a vertex
corresponding to a
1
-dimensional quaternionic representation
different from
\alpha
and
\beta
.

Two vertices in
\mathcal{S}_{\Gamma}
are said to be adjacent if there is an edge connecting them.

At the end of the next section we will attach symbols to the edges of the
graph
\mathcal{S}_{\Gamma}
such that the resulting labeled graph contains all
the necessary information needed to set up the complex

DCI(\overline{Y}_{\Gamma})
. All of the graphs
\mathcal{S}_{\Gamma}
will be given
in Proposition
4.25
.

4.
Determination of Moduli Spaces and Differentials

In this section we review the key results that allow us to determine
the Donaldson models
DCI(\overline{Y}_{\Gamma})
, for the finite subgroups

\Gamma\subset\operatorname{SU}(2)
, explicitly.
The main result of
[
Aus95
]
necessary for the calculations, originally due to Kronheimer, is the following.

Theorem 4.1
.

Let
\Gamma\subset\operatorname{SU}(2)
be a finite subgroup and let

\alpha,\beta
be flat connections in the
trivial
\operatorname{SU}(2)
-bundle over
\overline{Y}_{\Gamma}
. Let
z
be the unique
homotopy class from
\alpha
to
\beta
with

-2\leq\operatorname{gr}_{z}(\alpha,\beta)\leq 5
. Then if
\alpha
and
\beta

are adjacent in
\mathcal{S}_{\Gamma}
(see Def.
3.9
) then in
the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{z}(\alpha,\beta)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e_{-}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e_{+}}

there is an identification
e_{-}^{-1}(*)\cong Y_{\Gamma^{\prime}}
for some
other finite subgroup
\Gamma^{\prime}\subset\operatorname{SU}(2)
.
If
\alpha
and
\beta
are not adjacent in
\mathcal{S}_{\Gamma}
, then

\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{z}(\alpha,\beta)
is empty.

There is also fairly simple graphical procedure to determine the subgroup

\Gamma^{\prime}
from the positions of
\alpha
and
\beta
in the extended
Dynkin diagram
\overline{\Delta}_{\Gamma}
associated with
\Gamma
under
the McKay correspondence (see Proposition
4.12
).

The main idea of the proof is to translate the problem of determining
instantons over
\mathbb{R}\times Y_{\Gamma}
with
L^{2}
curvature modulo gauge transformations into the problem of determining

\Gamma
-invariant instantons in
\Gamma
-equivariant
\operatorname{SU}(2)
-bundles
over
S^{4}
modulo
\Gamma
-invariant gauge transformations, and then
to solve the latter problem with the use of the equivariant ADHM correspondence.

We begin by setting up the first correspondence. Recall that
the instantons on the trivial
\operatorname{SU}(2)
-bundle over the cylinder

\mathbb{R}\times Y_{\Gamma}
with
L^{2}
-curvature are naturally partitioned
into subsets by three pieces of data: the limiting flat connections

\alpha_{\pm}\in\mathcal{C}\cong\operatorname{Irr}^{1}(\Gamma,\mathbb{H})
and the relative homotopy class
z
traced out by the connection. For our purpose it is more convenient to replace this latter invariant by the relative Chern number
(see
[
Don02
, p. 48]
)

\hat{c}_{2}(A)\coloneqq\frac{1}{8\pi^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}\times Y_{\Gamma}}\operatorname{tr}F_{A}^{2}=\frac{1}{8\pi^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}\times Y_{\Gamma}}|F_{A}|^{2}\in\mathbb{R}.

Thus to any instanton
A
with
L^{2}
-curvature over
\mathbb{R}\times Y_{\Gamma}
we may associate a unique triple

(\alpha_{+},\alpha_{-},\hat{c}_{2}(A))\in\operatorname{Irr}^{1}(\Gamma,\mathbb{H})\times\operatorname{Irr}^{1}(\Gamma,\mathbb{H})\times\mathbb{R}.

Let
\Gamma
act on
\mathbb{R}\times S^{3}
by
\gamma\cdot(t,x)\mapsto(t,\gamma x)
such that
q=(1\times p)\colon\mathbb{R}\times S^{3}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}\times Y_{\Gamma}
is an orientation preserving covering map.
Regard
S^{4}\subset\mathbb{C}^{2}\oplus\mathbb{R}
such that the suspended action
\Gamma\times S^{4}\rightarrow S^{4}
takes the form
\gamma\cdot(x,t)=(\gamma x,t)
. By composing
the conformal equivalence

\mathbb{R}\times S^{3}\cong\mathbb{C}^{2}-\{0\}
,
(t,x)\mapsto e^{t}x
, with
the inverse of stereographic projection from the north pole we obtain
an equivariant conformal equivalence
\tau\colon\mathbb{R}\times S^{3}\cong S^{4}-\{N,S\}
,
where
N,S=(0,\pm 1)\in S^{4}
are the poles. Explicitly,

\tau(t,x)=(\cosh(t)x,\tanh(t)).

Let
S^{4}
carry the orientation that makes this map orientation preserving,
that is, the opposite of the standard orientation.

Recall that a
\Gamma
-equivariant vector-bundle over a
\Gamma
-space

X
is a vector bundle
E\rightarrow X
with a
\Gamma
-action on
the total space making the projection equivariant and such that

\gamma:E_{x}\rightarrow E_{\gamma x}
is linear for each
x\in X
and
\gamma\in\Gamma
. If
E

carries an
\operatorname{SU}(2)
-structure, that is, a Hermitian metric and a
fixed unitary trivialization of
\Lambda^{2}_{\mathbb{C}}E
, then we call
E

a
\Gamma
-equivariant
\operatorname{SU}(2)
-bundle provided the action
preserves this additional structure. In that case, there is an induced action
of
\Gamma
on the space of connections
\mathcal{A}_{E}
. A connection is said to
be invariant if it is fixed under this action. The subspace of invariant
connections is denoted by
\mathcal{A}_{E}^{\Gamma}
. The action of the full gauge
group does not preserve this subspace, but the subgroup of equivariant gauge
transformations
\mathcal{G}^{\Gamma}_{E}\subset\mathcal{G}_{E}
does. Therefore, the natural configuration space in this equivariant setting is

\mathcal{B}_{E,\Gamma}\coloneqq\mathcal{A}^{\Gamma}_{E}/\mathcal{G}_{E}^{\Gamma}
. We may also include
a framing coordinate to obtain the framed configuration space

\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}_{E,\Gamma}
. All of the above may, of course, be spelled out
in terms of principal bundles as well.

Let
F\rightarrow\mathbb{R}\times Y_{\Gamma}
be the trivial
\operatorname{SU}(2)
-bundle. Then

E\coloneqq q^{*}F\rightarrow\mathbb{R}\times S^{3}
obtains the structure of a
\Gamma
-equivariant vector bundle in a natural way. Moreover, since
q
is a finite covering map, pullback induces
a bijection
\mathcal{A}_{F}\cong\mathcal{A}_{E}^{\Gamma}
that matches the subsets
of anti-self-dual connections with curvature in
L^{2}
.
Let
B
be an ASD connection in
F
with curvature in
L^{2}
and let

A=q^{*}(B)
be the pullback connection in
E
. According to
Uhlenbeck’s removable singularities theorem there exists a bundle

\widetilde{E}\rightarrow S^{4}
with connection
\widetilde{A}
and a bundle map

\rho:E\rightarrow\widetilde{E}
covering
\tau\colon\mathbb{R}\times S^{3}\rightarrow S^{4}
such that

\rho^{*}(\widetilde{A})=A
. The following diagram summarizes the situation

(4.1)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(F,B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(E,A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\widetilde{E},\widetilde{A})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{R}\times Y_{\Gamma}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{R}\times S^{3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S^{4}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau}

According to Austin
[
Aus95
]

the
\Gamma
-action extends over
\widetilde{E}
making
\widetilde{A}
invariant and
the flat limiting connections of the connection
B
are encoded in the isomorphism classes of the representations

\widetilde{E}_{N}
and
\widetilde{E}_{S}
over the fixed points. Furthermore,
the following calculation shows that
c_{2}(\widetilde{E})
is uniquely determined
by
\hat{c}_{2}(B)

(4.2)

\displaystyle 8\pi^{2}c_{2}(\widetilde{E})[S^{4}]=

\displaystyle\int_{S^{4}}\operatorname{tr}(F_{\widetilde{A}}^{2})=\int_{S^{4}}|F_{\widetilde{A}}|^{2}=\int_{\mathbb{R}\times S^{3}}|F_{A}|^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle|\Gamma|\int_{\mathbb{R}\times Y_{\Gamma}}|F_{B}|^{2}=8\pi^{2}|\Gamma|\hat{c}_{2}(B).

Here we have used the fact that the Chern-Weil integrand

\operatorname{tr}(F_{\widetilde{A}}^{2})
coincides pointwise with the norm

|F_{\widetilde{A}}|^{2}
for ASD connections, the conformal invariance of the integral of
2
-forms in dimension four and that
\mathbb{R}\times S^{3}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}\times Y_{\Gamma}
is a finite Riemannian covering map with fibers of cardinality

|\Gamma|
.

The following theorem classifies the
\Gamma
-equivariant
\operatorname{SU}(2)
-bundles
over
S^{4}
. The proof is given in Appendix
B
(see Theorem
B.6
).

Theorem 4.2
.

A
\Gamma
-equivariant
\operatorname{SU}(2)
-bundle
\widetilde{E}\rightarrow S^{4}

is determined up to isomorphism by the ordered triple

([\widetilde{E}_{N}],[\widetilde{E}_{S}],k)
, where
k=c_{2}(\tilde{E})[S^{4}]
is the
second Chern number of the underlying
\operatorname{SU}(2)
-bundle and
[\widetilde{E}_{N}],[\widetilde{E}_{S}]
denote the isomorphism classes of the
\Gamma
-representations
over the fixed points. Moreover,
for each pair
\alpha,\beta\in\operatorname{Irr}^{1}(\Gamma,\mathbb{H})
there is a constant
c\in\mathbb{Z}
such that for each
k\equiv c\ (\mathrm{mod}\ |\Gamma|)
there exists
a
\Gamma
-equivariant
\operatorname{SU}(2)
-bundle with invariants
(\alpha,\beta,k)
.

Let
\widetilde{E}\rightarrow S^{4}
be a
\Gamma
-equivariant
\operatorname{SU}(2)
-bundle
with invariants
(\alpha_{+},\alpha_{-},k)
. Let
E=\tau^{*}(E)\rightarrow\mathbb{R}\times S^{3}

and
F=E/\Gamma\rightarrow\mathbb{R}\times Y_{\Gamma}
. Then we may reverse the above
procedure to produce a map from the invariant instantons in
\widetilde{E}

to the instantons over
\mathbb{R}\times Y_{\Gamma}
with limiting flat connection

\alpha_{\pm}
and relative Chern number
k/|\Gamma|
. More is true,
the
\Gamma
-equivariant gauge transformations in
\widetilde{E}
correspond
to gauge transformations in
F
that approach
\alpha_{\pm}
-harmonic
gauge transformations on the ends. Let
b_{0}\in Y_{\Gamma}
denote a basepoint
and let
\tilde{b}\in S^{3}
be a lift.
Let

\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{\Gamma,\infty}(\widetilde{E})\subset\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}_{\widetilde{E},\Gamma}

be the moduli space of invariant instantons in
\widetilde{E}
framed at

(0,\widetilde{b})
.
Then the above discussion yields a bijective correspondence

\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{\Gamma}(\widetilde{E})\cong\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{z}(\alpha_{-},\alpha_{+}),

where
z
is the relative homotopy class corresponding to the relative
Chern number
k/|\Gamma|
and the latter space is framed
at
(0,b)\in\mathbb{R}\times Y_{\Gamma}
. Recall that the end-point maps

e_{\pm}:\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{z}(\alpha_{-},\alpha_{+})\rightarrow\alpha_{\pm}
were defined
by parallel transport of the framing to
\pm\infty
. Under the above
bijection this corresponds to parallel transport of the framing
along the great circle through the basepoint
(\tilde{b},0)\in S^{4}
and
the poles
N,S=(0,\pm 1)
. The equivariant ADHM correspondence, which we turn
to next, describes the instanton moduli space with fixed framing at

N
and we then see that this corresponds to the fiber

e_{+}^{-1}(*)
. If we change the orientation in
Y_{\Gamma}
we may precompose
with the map
(t,x)\mapsto(-t,x)
in
\mathbb{R}\times Y_{\Gamma}
to see that
the ADHM correspondence will describe the fiber
e_{-}^{-1}(*)
instead.

4.1.
The ADHM Description of Instantons

We first recall the non-equivariant ADHM classification of

\operatorname{SU}(2)
-instantons over
S^{4}

[
DK90
, Sec. 3.3]
.
This construction describes the various moduli spaces of
instantons over
S^{4}
in terms of linear algebraic data.
In the following we regard
\mathbb{C}^{2}\cup\{\infty\}=S^{4}
where the orientation
is determined by the complex orientation on
\mathbb{C}^{2}
.
Recall that an
\operatorname{SU}(2)
-bundle over
S^{4}
, or any other closed, oriented

4
-manifold, is determined up to isomorphim by the second
Chern number
k=c_{2}(E)[S^{4}]\in\mathbb{Z}
. The bundle can only support an
ASD connection if
k\geq 0
and in case
k=0
every ASD connection
is flat.

For a fixed integer
k\geq 1
let
\mathscr{H}
be a Hermitian
vector space of dimension
k
and let
E_{\infty}
be a
2
-dimensional complex vector space with symmetry group
\operatorname{SU}(2)
.
Define the complex vector space

M\coloneqq\operatorname{Hom}(\mathbb{C}^{2}\otimes\mathscr{H},\mathscr{H})\oplus\operatorname{Hom}(\mathscr{H},E_{\infty})\oplus\operatorname{Hom}(E_{\infty},\mathscr{H}).

By identifying
\mathbb{C}^{2}\otimes\mathscr{H}=\mathscr{H}\oplus\mathscr{H}
we may write an element
m\in M
in terms of its components
m=(\tau_{1},\tau_{2},\pi,\sigma)
where

\tau_{1},\tau_{2}\in\operatorname{End}(\mathscr{H})
,
\pi\colon\mathscr{H}\rightarrow E_{\infty}
and
\sigma\colon E_{\infty}\rightarrow\mathscr{H}
.
Let
U(\mathscr{H})
, the group of unitary automorphisms of
\mathscr{H}
, act on
M

by

g\cdot(\tau_{1},\tau_{2},\pi,\sigma)=(g\tau_{1}g^{-1},g\tau_{2}g^{-1},\pi g^{-1},g\sigma).

Set
\mathfrak{u}(\mathscr{H})\coloneqq\operatorname{Lie}(U(\mathscr{H}))
and define

\mu=(\mu_{\mathbb{C}},\mu_{\mathbb{R}}):M\rightarrow\operatorname{End}(\mathscr{H})\oplus\mathfrak{u}(\mathscr{H})
by

(4.3)

\displaystyle\mu_{\mathbb{C}}(\tau_{1},\tau_{2},\pi,\sigma)=

\displaystyle[\tau_{1},\tau_{2}]+\sigma\pi

\displaystyle\mu_{\mathbb{R}}(\tau_{1},\tau_{2},\pi,\sigma)=

\displaystyle[\tau_{1},\tau_{1}^{*}]+[\tau_{2},\tau_{2}^{*}]+\sigma\sigma^{*}-\pi^{*}\pi.

These maps are equivariant when
U(\mathscr{H})
acts by conjugation on

\operatorname{End}(\mathscr{H})
and
\mathfrak{u}(\mathscr{H})
.

Given
(\tau_{1},\tau_{2},\pi,\sigma)\in M

we may for each
z=(z_{1},z_{2})\in\mathbb{C}^{2}
form the sequence

(4.4)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{H}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{C}^{2}\otimes\mathscr{H}\oplus E_{\infty}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{H}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{z}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{z}}

where

A_{z}=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\tau_{1}-z_{1}\\
\tau_{2}-z_{2}\\
\pi\end{array}\right)\;\mbox{ and }\;B_{z}=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}-(\tau_{2}-z_{2})&(\tau_{1}-z_{1})&\sigma\end{array}\right).

The condition
\mu_{\mathbb{C}}(\tau_{1},\tau_{2},\pi,\sigma)=0
is equivalent to

B_{z}\circ A_{z}=0
for all
z\in\mathbb{C}^{2}
.
Define
U_{reg}\subset M
to be the open set consisting of the

(\tau_{1},\tau_{2},\pi,\sigma)
for which
A_{z}
is injective and
B_{z}
is surjective for all
z\in\mathbb{C}^{2}
.
From the sequence (
4.4
) we may form a bundle homomorphism

R
between the trivial bundles over
\mathbb{C}^{2}
with fibers
\mathbb{C}^{2}\otimes\mathscr{H}
and

\mathbb{C}^{2}\otimes\mathscr{H}\oplus E_{\infty}
, respectively, by
R_{z}=(A_{z},B_{z}^{*})
.
This map extends naturally to a bundle homomorphism

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma\oplus\gamma\oplus\cdots\oplus\gamma}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\mathbb{C}^{2}\otimes\mathscr{H}\oplus E_{\infty}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{C}^{2}\cup\{\infty\}=S^{4},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R}

where
\gamma
is the
\operatorname{SU}(2)
-bundle with
c_{2}(\gamma)[S^{4}]=-1

and we take
k
copies of
\gamma
on the left hand side. This fact is
most easily understood using quaternions and the model
\mathbb{H}P^{1}\cong S^{4}

(see
[
Ati79
]
).
If
m=(\tau_{1},\tau_{2},\pi,\sigma)\in U_{reg}\cap\mu_{\mathbb{C}}^{-1}(0)
it follows that
R
is everywhere injective and we obtain an
\operatorname{SU}(2)
-bundle

E\coloneqq\operatorname{Im}(R)^{\perp}\cong\operatorname{Coker}(R)

with
c_{2}(E)[S^{4}]=k
, and the fiber over the point at
\infty
is the space

E_{\infty}
we started with. This bundle carries a natural connection
A
obtained by orthogonal projection from the product connection in the trivial bundle. This connection is ASD provided
\mu_{\mathbb{R}}(m)=0
as well.

The above construction associates a pair
(E,A)
consisting of
an
\operatorname{SU}(2)
-bundle
E\rightarrow S^{4}
with
c_{2}(E)=k
and an ASD connection

A
in
E
to each element
m\in U_{reg}\cap\mu^{-1}(0)
. If
m
is replaced
by
g\cdot m
for
g\in U(\mathscr{H})
the resulting pair
(E^{\prime},A^{\prime})
is equivalent
to
(E,A)
by a bundle isomorphism
\rho^{g}:E\rightarrow E^{\prime}
fixing the
fiber at
\infty
. The content of the ADHM classification is that this
construction produces all instantons in the
\operatorname{SU}(2)
-bundle over

S^{4}
with Chern number
k
.

Theorem 4.3
.

(The ADHM-Correspondence)
The assignment
(\tau_{1},\tau_{2},\pi,\sigma)\mapsto(E,A)
descends to an equivalence

(U_{reg}\cap\mu^{-1}(0))/U(\mathscr{H})\cong\mathcal{M}_{\infty}(k)

where
\mathcal{M}_{\infty}(k)
denotes the moduli space of gauge equivalences
classes of instantons framed at
\infty
in the bundle of
Chern number
k
over
S^{4}=\mathbb{C}^{2}\cup\{\infty\}
.

For the proof of the theorem and additional details we refer to
[
DK90
, Section 3.3]
. A part of the proof involves giving an inverse procedure to the construction sketched above.
We will need one fact from this inverse procedure. Given an

\operatorname{SU}(2)
-bundle
E\rightarrow S^{4}
with
c_{2}(E)[S^{4}]=k
and ASD connection

A
, the space
\mathscr{H}
is recovered by

(4.5)

\operatorname{Ker}[D_{A}^{*}\colon\Gamma(S^{-}\otimes E)\rightarrow\Gamma(S^{+}\otimes E)],

where
S^{\pm}
are the complex spinor bundles associated with the
unique spin structure on
S^{4}
and
D_{A}^{*}
is the formal adjoint
of the Dirac operator twisted by the connection
A
.

Let
\Gamma\subset\operatorname{SU}(2)
be a finite subgroup. Then
according to
[
Aus95
]
the ADHM correspondence is natural with respect
to the standard linear action
\Gamma\times\mathbb{C}^{2}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}^{2}
and
the corresponding suspended action on
\mathbb{C}^{2}\cup\{\infty\}=S^{4}
. This means
that if
E\rightarrow S^{4}
is a
\Gamma
-equivariant bundle with a
\Gamma
-invariant
ASD connection
A
, then the spaces
E_{\infty}
,
\mathscr{H}
are
complex
\Gamma
-representations and
m=(\tau_{1},\tau_{2},\pi,\sigma)\in U_{reg}\cap\mu^{-1}(0)
associated with
(E,A)
belongs to the subspace
M^{\Gamma}

of invariants. Conversely, if
\mathscr{H}
and
E_{\infty}
are

\Gamma
-representations and
m\in U_{reg}\cap\mu^{-1}(0)\cap M^{\Gamma}

then the associated bundle
E
naturally obtains the structure of
a
\Gamma
-equivariant bundle such that the connection
A
is invariant.

As we have seen the
\Gamma
-equivariant
\operatorname{SU}(2)
-bundles over
S^{4}

are determined up to isomorphism by the triple of invariants
(\alpha,\beta,k)

as in Theorem
4.2
. We need to know which representation

\mathscr{H}
that produces invariant instantons in the bundle with data

(\alpha,\beta,k)
. The relevant formula is obtained by an application
of the equivariant index theorem to the twisted Dirac operator in (
4.5
). Let
R(\Gamma)
denote the complex representation ring of
\Gamma
and let
\epsilon:R(\Gamma)\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
denote the augmentation. Furthermore, let as before
Q
denote the
2
-dimensional
representation associated with the inclusion
\Gamma\subset\operatorname{SU}(2)
.

Lemma 4.4
.

Let
E\rightarrow S^{4}
be a
\Gamma
-equivariant
\operatorname{SU}(2)
-bundle
with invariants
([E_{N}],[E_{S}],c_{2}(E)[S^{4}])=(\alpha,\beta,k)
and set

\mathscr{H}=\operatorname{Ind}[D_{A}^{*}\colon\Gamma(S^{-}\otimes E)\rightarrow\Gamma(S^{+}\otimes E)]\in R(\Gamma)

where
A
is a
\Gamma
-invariant connection in
E
.
Then we have the following relations in the representation ring
R(\Gamma)
:

\displaystyle(2-Q)\mathscr{H}

\displaystyle=\alpha-\beta

\displaystyle\epsilon(\mathscr{H})

\displaystyle=k.

These formulas are given in
[
Aus95
, Sec. 4.3]
. We have included
a proof in Appendix
B.11
. The following lemma ensures that
the above two equations determine the virtual representation
\mathscr{H}

uniquely.

Lemma 4.5
.

Let
\Gamma\subset\operatorname{SU}(2)
be a finite subgroup and let
Q
be the
canonical
2
-dimensional representation of
\Gamma
. Then the kernel
of the map
R(\Gamma)\rightarrow R(\Gamma)
given by
V\mapsto(2-Q)V
is given by

\{nR:n\in\mathbb{Z}\}
where
R\cong\mathbb{C}[\Gamma]
denotes the regular representation.

Proof.

Assume that
(2-Q)V=0
in
R(\Gamma)
. In terms of the
associated characters this means that
(2-\chi_{Q}(g))\chi_{V}(g)=0

for all
g\in\Gamma
. Since
Q
is a faithful representation of

\Gamma
it follows that
\chi_{Q}(g)\neq\chi_{Q}(1)=2
for all
g\neq 1
,
from which we deduce that
\chi_{V}(g)=0
for all
g\neq 1
. There exist
representations
U,W
of
\Gamma
such that
V=U-W
in
R(\Gamma)
.
Therefore,

\chi_{V}(1)/|\Gamma|=(\chi_{V},1_{\Gamma})=(\chi_{U},1_{\Gamma})-(\chi_{W},1_{\Gamma})=\operatorname{dim}_{\mathbb{C}}U^{\Gamma}-\operatorname{dim}_{\mathbb{C}}W^{\Gamma}\in\mathbb{Z},

where
1_{\Gamma}
is the character of the trivial representation and

(\cdot,\cdot)
is the Hermitian inner product on characters.
As the character of the regular representation
R
is given by

\chi_{R}(1)=|\Gamma|
and
\chi_{R}(g)=0
for
g\neq 1
, we conclude
that for
m=\chi_{V}(1)/|\Gamma|
we have

\chi_{V}=m\chi_{R}
, and hence
V=mR
in
R(\Gamma)
.
∎

Recall that
R(\Gamma)
is, as an abelian group, freely generated by the isomorphism classes of the irreducible representations of
\Gamma
.
Therefore, if we label the irreducible
representations by
R_{0},\cdots,R_{m}
, then every element in
R(\Gamma)
may
be expressed uniquely as a linear combination
\sum_{i=0}^{m}n_{i}[R_{i}]
for

n_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}
. Such a sum is called a virtual representation and in the
case all
n_{i}\geq 0
we call it an actual representation since
it determines the representation
\bigoplus_{i}n_{i}R_{i}
up to
isomorphism.

We are now in a position to state the equivariant ADHM correspondence.
This is a more precise version of
[
Aus95
, Lemma 4.3]
.

Theorem 4.6
.

(The Equivariant ADHM Correspondence) Let
E
be a
\Gamma
-equivariant

\operatorname{SU}(2)
-bundle over
S^{4}
with
([E_{N}],[E_{S}],c_{2}(E)[S^{4}])=(\alpha,\beta,k)
. Let
\mathscr{H}\in R(\Gamma)
be the unique element satisfying

(2-Q)\mathscr{H}=\alpha-\beta
and
\epsilon(\mathscr{H})=k
. Then if
\mathscr{H}
is
an actual representations the moduli space
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{\Gamma,\infty}(E)
of

\Gamma
-invariant instantons modulo
\Gamma
-equivariant gauge transformations
framed at
\infty=N
is given by

(U_{reg}\cap\mu^{-1}(0)\cap M^{\Gamma})/U(\mathscr{H})^{\Gamma},

where
U(\mathscr{H})^{\Gamma}
is the group of unitary
\Gamma
-equivariant
automorphisms of
\mathscr{H}
and

M^{\Gamma}=\operatorname{Hom}_{\Gamma}(Q\otimes\mathscr{H},\mathscr{H})\oplus\operatorname{Hom}_{\Gamma}(\mathscr{H},E_{\infty})\oplus\operatorname{Hom}_{\Gamma}(E_{\infty},\mathscr{H}).

If
\mathscr{H}
is not an actual representation, the moduli space is
empty.

Note that in the above statement
E_{\infty}=E_{N}
.

4.2.
Determination of the Low-Dimensional Moduli Spaces

The ADHM equations (
4.3
) are a particularly simple example of the hyper-Kähler moment map equations associated with an action of a Lie group on a hyper-Kähler manifold (see
[
Hit+87
]
). We briefly review the
set up in the linear case relevant for our purpose.

Let
V
be a (left) quaternionic vector space equipped with a compatible
real inner product
g\colon V\times V\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
, i.e., multiplication by unit
quaternions are orthogonal. Let
\operatorname{Sp}(V)
denote the group of

\mathbb{H}
-linear orthogonal automorphisms of
V
.
Define an
\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{H}\coloneqq\operatorname{Span}_{\mathbb{R}}\{i,j,k\}
valued

2
-form
\omega\colon V\times V\rightarrow\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{H}
by the formula

\omega(v,w)=ig(iv,w)+jg(jv,w)+kg(kv,w).

Note that the unique symplectic inner product
s:V\times V\rightarrow\mathbb{H}
determined
by
g
is given by
s=g-\omega
.

Suppose that
G
is a compact Lie group acting on
V
through
a homomorphism
\rho:G\rightarrow\operatorname{Sp}(V)
. The Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
of
G

acts on
V
through the derivative
d\rho_{1}:\mathfrak{g}\rightarrow\operatorname{sp}(V)=\operatorname{Lie}(\operatorname{Sp}(V))
.
A hyper-Kähler moment map for the action
G\times V\rightarrow V
is a
G
-equivariant map
\mu\colon V\rightarrow\operatorname{Hom}(\mathfrak{g},\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{H})
, where
G
acts on the target through the adjoint representation, satisfying

d\mu_{x}(v)=[\xi\mapsto\omega(\xi\cdot x,v)]\in\operatorname{Hom}(\mathfrak{g},\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{H})

for all
x\in V
and
v\in V\cong T_{x}V
. A moment map is clearly unique up to
a constant and one may check that the unique moment map vanishing at

0\in V
is given by

(4.6)

\mu(x)=[\xi\in\mathfrak{g}\mapsto\frac{1}{2}\omega(\xi\cdot x,x)\in\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{H}].

Definition 4.7
.

For a pair
(V,\rho\colon G\rightarrow\operatorname{Sp}(V,g))
as above we
define the hyper-Kähler quotient of
V
by
G
to be the space

\mu^{-1}(0)/G.

In general neither
\mu^{-1}(0)
nor
\mu^{-1}(0)/G
will be manifolds
due to singularities. However, if
U\subset V
is an open
G
-invariant
subset on which
G
acts freely, then
\mu^{-1}(0)\cap U
and the quotient
by
G
will be manifolds. Moreover, in this case the quotient inherits the structure of a hyper-Kähler manifold.

Example 4.8
.

(The ADHM equations)
Let
\mathscr{H}
and
E_{\infty}
be a pair
of Hermitian vector space and define

V=\operatorname{Hom}(\mathbb{C}^{2}\otimes\mathscr{H},\mathscr{H})\oplus\operatorname{Hom}(\mathscr{H},E_{\infty})\oplus\operatorname{Hom}(E_{\infty},\mathscr{H})

equipped with the Hermitian metric induced from
\mathscr{H}
and
E_{\infty}
.
Using the standard basis of
\mathbb{C}^{2}
an element of
V
is written

(\tau_{1},\tau_{2},\pi,\sigma)
. Let
G=U(\mathscr{H})
act on
V
by

g\cdot(\tau_{1},\tau_{2},\pi,\sigma)=(g\tau_{1}g^{-1},g\tau_{2}g^{-1},\pi g^{-1},g\sigma)
. Define a quaternionic structure map
J\colon V\rightarrow V
by

J(\tau_{1},\tau_{2},\pi,\sigma)=(-\tau_{2}^{*},\tau_{1}^{*},-\sigma^{*},\pi^{*}).

Then the action of
G
commutes with

J
and therefore defines a homomorphism
\rho\colon G\rightarrow\operatorname{Sp}(V)
.
If we use the trace inner product in
\mathfrak{u}(\mathscr{H})
and the decomposition

\operatorname{Im}(\mathbb{H})\cong\mathbb{R}\oplus\mathbb{C}
to identify

\operatorname{Hom}(\mathfrak{u}(\mathscr{H}),\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{H})\cong\mathfrak{u}(\mathscr{H})\otimes\operatorname{Im}(\mathbb{H})\cong\mathfrak{u}(\mathscr{H})\oplus\mathfrak{u}(\mathscr{H})\otimes\mathbb{C}\cong\mathfrak{u}(\mathscr{H})\oplus\operatorname{End}_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathscr{H}),

the resulting moment map
\mu\colon V\rightarrow\mathfrak{u}(\mathscr{H})\oplus\operatorname{End}_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathscr{H})
vanishing at
0
is given by the ADHM equations in (
4.3
).

Kronheimer
[
Kro89
]
used this construction in the following
situation. Let
\Gamma\subset\operatorname{SU}(2)
be a finite subgroup and
let
Q
and
R
denote the canonical representation and the
regular representation, respectively. Assume that
Q
and
R
are equipped with

\Gamma
-invariant Hermitian metrics and fix a
\Gamma
-invariant
quaternionic structure
j:Q\rightarrow Q
compatible with the metric. Let

P=Q\otimes\operatorname{End}(R)\cong\operatorname{Hom}(Q\otimes R,R)

equipped with the induced Hermitian metric. By combining the
quaternionic structure on
Q
with the real structure
f\mapsto f^{*}

on
\operatorname{End}(R)
one obtains a
\Gamma
-equivariant quaternionic structure

J\colon P\rightarrow P
compatible with the Hermitian metric. As this structure
is preserved by the action of
\Gamma
, the subspace

K=P^{\Gamma}
carries the same structure.

The group
U(R)
acts on
P
by conjugation in the second factor. This action preserves the quaternionic structure and the Hermitian metric. The subgroup
U(R)^{\Gamma}
of
\Gamma
-equivariant unitary automorphisms preserves the subspace
K
and therefore defines a homomorphism
U(R)^{\Gamma}\rightarrow\operatorname{Sp}(K)
. Here, the subgroup
U(1)
of scalars is contained in the kernel so the homomorphism descends to a homomorphism
F\coloneqq U(R)^{\Gamma}/U(1)\rightarrow\operatorname{Sp}(K)
.
Let
\mu:K\rightarrow\operatorname{Hom}(\mathfrak{f},\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{H})
be the unique hyper-Kähler moment vanishing at
0\in K
. In this situation Kronheimer
identified the hyper-Kähler quotient.

Lemma 4.9
.

[
Kro89
]

There is a homeomorphism

\mu^{-1}(0)/F\cong\mathbb{C}^{2}/\Gamma

that restricts to an isometry

\frac{\mu^{-1}(0)-\{0\}}{F}\cong\frac{\mathbb{C}^{2}-\{0\}}{\Gamma}.

Remark 4.10
.

A consequence of this is that

F
acts freely on
\mu^{-1}(0)-\{0\}
.

The space
K
and the group
F
admit simple descriptions in terms of
the McKay graph
\overline{\Delta}_{\Gamma}
associated with
\Gamma
. Let

R_{0},\cdots,R_{m}
be a complete set of representatives for

\operatorname{Irr}(\Gamma,\mathbb{C})
, where we take
R_{0}=\mathbb{C}
to be the trivial
representation. The regular representation
R=\mathbb{C}[\Gamma]
decomposes as

R\cong\bigoplus_{i=0}^{m}n_{i}R_{i},

where
n_{i}=\operatorname{dim}_{\mathbb{C}}R_{i}
. Recall from subsection
3.3

that a matrix

A
was defined by
Q\otimes R_{i}=\bigoplus_{j}a_{ij}R_{j}
, or equivalently

a_{ij}=\operatorname{dim}_{\mathbb{C}}\operatorname{Hom}_{\Gamma}(Q\otimes R_{i},R_{j})
. Furthermore,
the McKay graph
\overline{\Delta}_{\Gamma}
was defined by taking the
irreducible representations as vertices and an (unoriented) edge connecting

R_{i}
to
R_{j}
precisely when
a_{ij}=a_{ji}=1
. Using this

\displaystyle K=\operatorname{Hom}_{\Gamma}(Q\otimes R,R)

\displaystyle=\bigoplus_{i,j}\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathbb{C}^{n_{i}},\mathbb{C}^{n_{j}})\otimes\operatorname{Hom}_{\Gamma}(Q\otimes R_{i},R_{j})

\displaystyle=\bigoplus_{i\to j\mbox{ in }\overline{\Delta}_{\Gamma}}\operatorname{Hom}(\mathbb{C}^{n_{i}},\mathbb{C}^{n_{j}})

where each edge is repeated twice, once with each orientation,
and

F=U(R)^{\Gamma}/U(1)=U\left(\bigoplus_{i}n_{i}R_{i}\right)^{\Gamma}/U(1)=\left(\prod_{i}U(n_{i})\right)/U(1).

This has a simple graphical interpretation on the Dynkin graph
\overline{\Delta}_{\Gamma}
corresponding to
\Gamma
. Attach the vector space

\mathbb{C}^{n_{i}}
to the vertex corresponding to
R_{i}
. Then an element of
K
is an assignment of a linear map
f_{ji}:\mathbb{C}^{n_{i}}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}^{n_{j}}
whenever
a_{ij}=1
.
An element
g=(g_{i})_{i}\in\prod_{i}U(n_{i})
acts on
f=(f_{ji})_{i,j}\in K

by
(g\cdot f)_{ji}=g_{j}f_{ji}g_{i}^{-1}
.

Example 4.11
.

For
\Gamma=O^{*}
an element of
K=\operatorname{Hom}_{O^{*}}(Q\otimes R,R)
is given by assigning a linear map to each arrow in the following diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{C}^{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{C}^{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{C}^{3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{C}^{4}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{C}^{3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{C}^{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{C}}

The next result is the key step in relating Kronheimer’s hyper-Kähler
quotient to the space occuring in the equivariant ADHM correspondence.
Recall that an
\operatorname{SU}(2)
-representation
\alpha
of
\Gamma
is
either

(1)

of quaternionic type and irreducible as a complex
representation,

(2)

of complex type and splits
\alpha=\lambda\oplus\lambda^{*}

with
\lambda\not\cong\lambda^{*}
or

(3)

of real type and splits
\alpha=2\eta
for some
\eta
with
\eta\cong\eta^{*}
.

By the vertex or vertices corresponding to
\alpha
in
\overline{\Delta}_{\Gamma}
we will mean the vertex corresponding to
\alpha
in case
(1)
, the vertices corresponding to
\lambda
and
\lambda^{*}
in case
(2)
and the vertex corresponding to
\eta
in case
(3)
. The following is a more refined
version of
[
Aus95
, Lemma 4.6]
. Austin gives no explicit proof,
but states that one may verify it by a case by case analysis.

Proposition 4.12
.

Let
\Gamma\subset\operatorname{SU}(2)
be a finite subgroup
and let
\alpha
and
\beta
be
\operatorname{SU}(2)
-representations that are adjacent
in the graph
\mathcal{S}_{\Gamma}
(Definition
3.9
).
Then the solution

\mathscr{H}
of the equation
(2-Q)\mathscr{H}=\alpha-\beta
in
R(\Gamma)
with
minimal
\epsilon(\mathscr{H})=k>0
is given in the following way.

(i)

Delete the vertex or vertices corresponding to
\beta
in
\overline{\Delta}_{\Gamma}
. The resulting graph has one or two components.
Let
\Delta_{\mathscr{H}}
be the component containing
\alpha
.

(ii)

Up to isomorphism there is a unique nontrivial finite subgroup
\Gamma^{\prime}\subset\operatorname{SU}(2)
and an isomorphism of graphs

\phi\colon\Delta_{\Gamma^{\prime}}\rightarrow\Delta_{\mathscr{H}}
.

(iii)

Let
S_{0},S_{1},\cdots,S_{r}
be the irreducible representations of

\Gamma^{\prime}
, where
S_{0}
is the trivial representation. Then

\mathscr{H}\coloneqq\bigoplus_{j=1}^{r}(\operatorname{dim}_{\mathbb{C}}S_{j})R_{\phi(j)}.

Furthermore, if
Q^{\prime}=m_{1}S_{i_{1}}\oplus m_{2}S_{i_{2}}
is the isotypical decomposition of the canonical representation of
\Gamma^{\prime}
, then

\alpha=m_{1}R_{\phi(i_{1})}\oplus m_{2}R_{\phi(i_{2})}

is the isotypical decomposition of
\alpha
. In particular,
\phi
takes
the vertex or vertices associated with
Q^{\prime}
to the vertex or vertices
corresponding to
\alpha
.

Remark 4.13
.

The isotypical decomposition of the canonical representation

Q
of a finite subgroup
\Gamma\subset\operatorname{SU}(2)
is
Q=Q
for

\Gamma
non-cyclic and
Q=\rho\oplus\rho^{*}
for some
1
-dimensional
representation
\rho
for
\Gamma
cyclic.

Corollary 4.14
.

Let
\Gamma\subset\operatorname{SU}(2)
be a finite subgroup
and let
\alpha\neq\beta
be
\operatorname{SU}(2)
-representations that
are not adjacent in
\mathcal{S}_{\Gamma}
. Let
\alpha=\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{s}=\beta
be the vertices of the minimal edge path connecting

\alpha
to
\beta
in
\mathcal{S}_{\Gamma}
and let

\mathscr{H}_{i}\in R(\Gamma)
be the minimal positive solution of the equation

(2-Q)\mathscr{H}_{i}=\alpha_{i}-\alpha_{i+1}
for
0\leq i\leq s-1
. Then

\mathscr{H}\coloneqq\bigoplus_{i=0}^{s-1}\mathscr{H}_{i}
is the minimal positive
solution
(2-Q)\mathscr{H}=\alpha-\beta
in
R(\Gamma)
.

Proof.

The calculation

(2-Q)\mathscr{H}=\sum_{i=0}^{s-1}(2-Q)\mathscr{H}_{i}=\sum_{i=0}^{s-1}\alpha_{i}-\alpha_{i+1}=\alpha_{0}-\alpha_{s}=\alpha-\beta

shows that
\mathscr{H}
solves the relevant equation.
By Lemma
4.5
every other solution is given by

\mathscr{H}+nR
for some
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, where
R
is the regular representation.
To see that
\mathscr{H}
is the minimal positive solution, it therefore suffices to show that
\mathscr{H}-R
is not positive, i.e., not an actual representation.
Let
\Delta_{i}=\Delta_{\mathscr{H}_{i}}
be
the subgraph of
\overline{\Delta}_{\Gamma}
described in the above proposition.
By the construction of these graphs and Lemma
3.8
we
conclude that
\Delta_{0}\subset\Delta_{1}\subset\cdots\subset\Delta_{s-1}
.
This implies that the representation
\mathscr{H}
is only supported on the
vertices of
\Delta_{s-1}
, which by construction does not contain the
vertices corresponding to
\beta=\alpha_{s}
. Hence, in the isotypical
decomposition
\mathscr{H}=\bigoplus_{i=0}^{n}k_{i}R_{i}
there exists some

i
with
k_{i}=0
. We therefore conclude that
\mathscr{H}-R
is not an actual
representation. ∎

Let
\Gamma
,
\alpha
,
\beta
,
\mathscr{H}
,
\Gamma^{\prime}
and
\phi\colon\Delta_{\Gamma^{\prime}}\rightarrow\Delta_{\mathscr{H}}
be defined as in Proposition

4.12
and set

N=M^{\Gamma}=\operatorname{Hom}_{\Gamma}(\mathscr{H}\otimes Q,\mathscr{H})\oplus\operatorname{Hom}_{\Gamma}(\mathscr{H},E_{\infty})\oplus\operatorname{Hom}_{\Gamma}(E_{\infty},\mathscr{H}),

as in Theorem
4.6
. This space is endowed with
a quaternionic structure and compatible metric preserved by the action
of
G\coloneqq U(\mathscr{H})^{\Gamma}
as in Example
4.8
. Note that

E_{\infty}=\alpha
.
Let
R^{\prime}
and
Q^{\prime}
denote the regular and canonical representation
of
\Gamma^{\prime}
respectively and set

K=\operatorname{Hom}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}(Q^{\prime}\otimes R^{\prime},R^{\prime})\;\mbox{ and }\;\;F=U(R^{\prime})^{\Gamma^{\prime}}/U(1)

as in Kronheimer’s construction. The following is a more precise version
of
[
Aus95
, Lemma 4.7]
.

Proposition 4.15
.

In the above situation there is an isometric isomorphism

f:N\rightarrow K
of quaternionic vector spaces and an isomorphism of groups

\tau:G\rightarrow F
such that
f(g\cdot n)=\tau(g)f(n)
for all

n\in N
and
g\in G
.

Proof.

Let
S
be the regular representation of
\Gamma^{\prime}
and
write
S=S_{0}\oplus S^{\prime}
, where
S_{0}
denotes the trivial representation. Then

K=\operatorname{Hom}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}(Q^{\prime}\otimes S,S)=\operatorname{Hom}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}(Q^{\prime}\otimes S^{\prime},S^{\prime})\oplus\operatorname{Hom}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}(Q^{\prime},S^{\prime})\oplus\operatorname{Hom}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}(S^{\prime},Q^{\prime})

since
\operatorname{Hom}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}(Q^{\prime},S_{0})=0
and
\operatorname{Hom}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}(Q^{\prime}\otimes S^{\prime},S_{0})\cong\operatorname{Hom}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}(S^{\prime},Q^{\prime})
as
Q^{\prime}
is self-dual.
We will now match these components with the components of
N
. Let

n_{i}=\operatorname{dim}_{\mathbb{C}}S_{i}
. Recall that the graph
\Delta_{\Gamma^{\prime}}
is the
graph obtained from
\overline{\Delta}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}
by deleting the
vertex corresponding to
S_{0}
. Using part
(iii)
of the above proposition
we can write
\mathscr{H}=\oplus_{i=1}^{r}n_{i}R_{\phi(i)}
where

\phi:\Delta_{\Gamma^{\prime}}\rightarrow\Delta_{\mathscr{H}}
is the isomorphism of graphs.
Then

(4.7)

\displaystyle\operatorname{Hom}_{\Gamma}(Q\otimes\mathscr{H},\mathscr{H})=\bigoplus_{i\to j\mbox{ in }\Delta_{\Gamma^{\prime}}}\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathbb{C}^{n_{i}},\mathbb{C}^{n_{j}})\otimes\operatorname{Hom}_{\Gamma}(Q\otimes R_{\phi(i)},R_{\phi(j)})

\displaystyle\cong\bigoplus_{i\to j\mbox{ in }\Delta_{\Gamma^{\prime}}}\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathbb{C}^{n_{i}},\mathbb{C}^{n_{j}})\otimes\operatorname{Hom}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}(Q^{\prime}\otimes S_{i},S_{j})=\operatorname{Hom}_{\Gamma}(Q^{\prime}\otimes S^{\prime},S^{\prime}).

To make this an
\mathbb{H}
-linear isometric isomorphism we specify the
isomorphisms

(4.8)

\operatorname{Hom}_{\Gamma}(Q\otimes R_{\phi(i)},R_{\phi(j)})\cong\mathbb{C}\cong\operatorname{Hom}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}(Q^{\prime}\otimes S_{i},S_{j})

for
i
adjacent to
j
in
\Delta_{\Gamma^{\prime}}
as follows.
Choose an arbitrary orientation on the edges in
\Delta_{\Gamma^{\prime}}
. Then
for each positive arrow
i\to j
in
\Delta_{\Gamma^{\prime}}
we pick basis vectors

u_{ij}\in\operatorname{Hom}_{\Gamma}(Q\otimes R_{\phi(i)},R_{\phi(j)})

and
v_{ij}\in\operatorname{Hom}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}(Q^{\prime}\otimes S_{i},S_{j})
of unit length.
Then
u_{ji}=Ju_{ij}\in\operatorname{Hom}_{\Gamma}(Q\otimes R_{\phi(j)},R_{\phi(i)})

and
v_{ji}=Jv_{ij}\in\operatorname{Hom}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}(Q^{\prime}\otimes S_{j},S_{i})

are also unit basis vectors. We then define the isomorphisms in
(
4.8
) by sending
u_{ij}
to
v_{ij}

for each
i\to j
in
\Delta_{\Gamma^{\prime}}
.

Next, by the final part of the above proposition we know that if

Q^{\prime}=m_{1}S_{i_{1}}\oplus m_{2}S_{i_{2}}
is the isotypical decomposition, then

\alpha=E_{\infty}=m_{1}R_{\phi(i_{1})}\oplus m_{2}R_{\phi(i_{2})}
is the
isotypical decomposition of
\alpha
. We may therefore identify

(4.9)

\displaystyle\operatorname{Hom}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}(S^{\prime},Q^{\prime})=\bigoplus_{j=1}^{2}\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathbb{C}^{n_{i_{j}}},\mathbb{C}^{m_{j}})\otimes\operatorname{Hom}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}(S_{i_{j}},S_{i_{j}})

\displaystyle\cong\bigoplus_{j=1}^{2}\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathbb{C}^{n_{i_{j}}},\mathbb{C}^{m_{j}})\otimes\operatorname{Hom}_{\Gamma}(R_{\phi(i_{j})},R_{\phi(i_{j})})\cong\operatorname{Hom}_{\Gamma}(\mathscr{H},E_{\infty})

and similarly
\operatorname{Hom}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}(Q^{\prime},S^{\prime})\cong\operatorname{Hom}_{\Gamma}(E_{\infty},\mathscr{H})
.
Once again one should match unit vectors carefully to ensure that
the direct sum of these isomorphisms is an
\mathbb{H}
-linear isometry.
The direct sum of these isomorphisms gives the required
\mathbb{H}
-linear
isometry
N\cong K
.

The (orthogonal) decomposition
\mathscr{H}=\bigoplus_{i=1}^{r}n_{i}R_{\phi(i)}
induces an isomorphism
G=U(\mathscr{H})^{\Gamma}\cong\prod_{i=1}^{r}U(n_{i})

and this group only acts on the matrix components in the decompositions
(
4.7
) and (
4.9
). In the latter case it only acts
on factors coming from
\mathscr{H}
. Moreover,

F\cong\left(\prod_{i=0}^{r}U(n_{i})\right)/U(1)\cong\prod_{i=1}^{r}U(n_{i})

as
n_{0}=1
, and the action can again be seen to be on the matrix component
in (
4.7
) and (
4.9
), and in the latter case only on
the factors coming from
S^{\prime}
. Therefore, the isomorphism

F\cong\prod_{i=1}^{r}U(n_{i})\cong G

does the trick and the proof is complete.
∎

Proposition 4.16
.

Let
\Gamma\subset\operatorname{SU}(2)
be a finite subgroup
and let
\alpha
and
\beta
be
\operatorname{SU}(2)
-representations
adjacent in the graph
\mathcal{S}_{\Gamma}
. Let
E\rightarrow S^{4}

be the
\Gamma
-equivariant
\operatorname{SU}(2)
-bundle with

[E_{N}]=\alpha
,
[E_{S}]=\beta
and minimal
k=c_{2}(E)[S^{4}]>0
. Then the moduli
space
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{\Gamma,\infty}(E)
of
\Gamma
-invariant instantons in
E
framed at
N=\infty
is given by

(\mathbb{C}^{2}-\{0\})/\Gamma^{\prime}\cong\mathbb{R}\times S^{3}/\Gamma^{\prime}

where the finite subgroup
\Gamma^{\prime}\subset\operatorname{SU}(2)
is determined as in Proposition
4.12
.

Proof.

We continue to use the notation of the two preceding
propositions. According to the equivariant ADHM correspondence the
moduli space
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{\Gamma,\infty}(E)
is given by

(4.10)

\frac{\mu^{-1}(0)\cap U_{reg}\cap M^{\Gamma}}{U(\mathscr{H})^{\Gamma}},

where in the above notation
N=M^{\Gamma}
and
G=U(\mathscr{H})^{\Gamma}
.
Let
\mu_{N}\colon N\rightarrow\mathfrak{g}\otimes\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{H}
and
\mu_{K}\colon K\rightarrow\mathfrak{f}\otimes\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{H}
be the unique hyper-Kähler
moment maps vanishing at
0
associated with the action of

G
on
N
and
F
on
K
. Due to the uniqueness of the moment map it
follows that the isomorphisms
f\colon N\rightarrow K
and
\psi\colon G\rightarrow F
of the previous proposition induce a homeomorphism
\mu_{N}^{-1}(0)\cong\mu_{K}^{-1}(0)

equivariant along the map
\psi
. The group
F
acts freely on

\mu_{K}^{-1}(0)-\{0\}
(see Remark
4.10
), so by Lemma

4.9
we obtain isometries of hyper-Kähler
4
-manifolds

\frac{\mu_{N}^{-1}(0)-\{0\}}{G}\cong\frac{\mu_{K}^{-1}(0)-\{0\}}{F}\cong\frac{\mathbb{C}^{2}-\{0\}}{\Gamma^{\prime}}.

To complete the proof we have to show that the left hand side
coincides with the space in (
4.10
). This will be achieved by
proving that

(1)

\mu_{N}
is the restriction of
\mu
to
N=M^{\Gamma}\subset M
,
so that
\mu_{N}^{-1}(0)=\mu^{-1}(0)\cap N
and

(2)

U_{reg}\cap\mu_{N}^{-1}(0)=\mu_{N}^{-1}(0)-\{0\}
.

We may decompose an element
m\in M^{\Gamma}
as

m=(\tau_{1},\tau_{2},\pi,\sigma)\in\operatorname{Hom}_{\Gamma}(Q\otimes\mathscr{H},\mathscr{H})\oplus\operatorname{Hom}_{\Gamma}(\mathscr{H},E_{\infty})\oplus\operatorname{Hom}_{\Gamma}(E_{\infty},\mathscr{H}),

where we regard
Q=\mathbb{C}^{2}
, as earlier. Here
\pi
and
\sigma
are
\Gamma
-equivariant, while
\tau_{1},\tau_{2}
satisfy the properties
(see
[
Kro89
, Eq. 2.2]
)

(4.11)

\begin{array}[]{c}\gamma^{-1}\tau_{1}\gamma=a\tau_{1}+b\tau_{2}\\
\gamma^{-1}\tau_{2}\gamma=-\overline{b}\tau_{1}+\overline{a}\tau_{2}\end{array}\;\;\mbox{ for all }\;\;\gamma=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}a&b\\
-\overline{b}&\overline{a}\end{array}\right)\in\Gamma\subset\operatorname{SU}(2),

where on the left hand side
\gamma
is regarded as an isometry
\mathscr{H}\rightarrow\mathscr{H}
.
Using the above description and the moment map equations (
4.3
)

\displaystyle\mu_{\mathbb{C}}(\tau_{1},\tau_{2},\pi,\sigma)=

\displaystyle[\tau_{1},\tau_{2}]+\sigma\pi

\displaystyle\mu_{\mathbb{R}}(\tau_{1},\tau_{2},\pi,\sigma)=

\displaystyle[\tau_{1},\tau_{1}^{*}]+[\tau_{2},\tau_{2}^{*}]+\sigma\sigma^{*}-\pi^{*}\pi

one easily verifies that
\mu_{\mathbb{R}}
and
\mu_{\mathbb{C}}
map
N=M^{\Gamma}
into

\mathfrak{u}(\mathscr{H})^{\Gamma}
and
\operatorname{End}_{\Gamma}(\mathscr{H})
, respectively. By the
uniqueness of the moment map it follows that
\mu_{N}
is the restriction
of
\mu
to
N=M^{\Gamma}
. This proves the first point.

Before we proceed to the second point recall that
U_{reg}\subset M
was defined to be the set of
m=(\tau_{1},\tau_{2},\pi,\sigma)
for which

\displaystyle A_{z}

\displaystyle=(\tau_{1}-z_{1},\tau_{2}-z_{2},\pi)^{t}\colon\mathscr{H}\oplus\mathscr{H}\oplus E_{\infty}\rightarrow\mathscr{H}

\displaystyle B_{z}

\displaystyle=(-(\tau_{2}-z_{2}),\tau_{1}-z_{1},\sigma)\colon\mathscr{H}\oplus\mathscr{H}\oplus E_{\infty}\rightarrow\mathscr{H}

were injective and surjective, respectively, for all
z=(z_{1},z_{2})\in\mathbb{C}^{2}

(see (
4.4
)). Observe that the equation
\mu_{\mathbb{R}}(m)=0
may be rewritten as

\tau_{1}\tau_{1}^{*}+\tau_{2}\tau_{2}^{*}+\sigma\sigma^{*}=\tau_{1}^{*}\tau_{1}+\tau_{2}^{*}\tau_{2}+\pi^{*}\pi,

or equivalently
B_{0}B_{0}^{*}=A_{0}^{*}A_{0}
. From this it follows that

\operatorname{Ker}(A_{0})=\operatorname{Ker}(A_{0}^{*}A_{0})=\operatorname{Ker}(B_{0}B_{0}^{*})=\operatorname{Ker}(B_{0}^{*}).

Hence,
A_{0}
is injective if and only if
B_{0}
is surjective.
In view of the easily verified fact that for any
(z_{1},z_{2})\in\mathbb{C}^{2}
we have

\mu(\tau_{1}-z_{1},\tau_{2}-z_{2},\pi,\sigma)=\mu(\tau_{1},\tau_{2},\pi,\sigma)
, the
above argument applies to show that if
m\in\mu^{-1}(0)
, then for
any
z\in\mathbb{C}^{2}
,
A_{z}
is injective if and only if
B_{z}
is surjective.

We may now verify the second claim as follows. As
U_{reg}\cap\mu_{N}^{-1}(0)\subset\mu_{N}^{-1}(0)-\{0\}
, it suffices to show that
if
m=(\tau_{1},\tau_{2},\pi,\sigma)\in\mu_{N}^{-1}(0)-U_{reg}
then

m=0
. To this end assume that
m\in\mu_{N}^{-1}(0)-U_{reg}
. In view of the
above considerations this means that there exists
z=(z_{1},z_{2})\in\mathbb{C}^{2}

and
h\neq 0\in\mathscr{H}
such that
A_{z}(h)=0
. Then
h
belongs to
\operatorname{Ker}\pi
and is a common eigenvector for

\tau_{1},\tau_{2}
with eigenvalues
z_{1},z_{2}
respectively. Using the
equations in (
4.11
) we deduce that
\gamma\cdot h
is a common eigenvector for
\tau_{1},\tau_{2}
with eigenvalues

\left(\begin{array}[]{c}z_{1}^{\gamma}\\
z_{2}^{\gamma}\end{array}\right)\coloneqq\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}a&b\\
-\overline{b}&\overline{a}\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{c}z_{1}\\
z_{2}\end{array}\right)\;\;\mbox{ for all }\;\;\gamma=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}a&b\\
-\overline{b}&\overline{a}\end{array}\right)\in\Gamma\subset\operatorname{SU}(2).

Let
L\coloneqq\operatorname{Span}_{\mathbb{C}}\{\gamma h:\gamma\in\Gamma\}
. This is a nontrivial
\Gamma
-invariant subspace of
\mathscr{H}
satisfying

\tau_{i}(L)\subset L
for
i=1,2
and
\pi(L)=0
. Moreover, since

\gamma\cdot h\in\operatorname{Ker}(A_{(z_{1}^{\gamma},z_{2}^{\gamma})})=\operatorname{Ker}(B_{(z_{1}^{\gamma},z_{2}^{\gamma})}^{*})
, it follows that

\tau_{i}^{*}(\gamma h)=\overline{z_{i}^{\gamma}}h
for
i=1,2
and

\sigma^{*}(\gamma h)=0
. Therefore,
\tau_{i}^{*}(L)\subset L
for
i=1,2

and
\sigma^{*}(L)=0
as well. Hence,

\tau_{i}(L^{\perp})\subset L^{\perp}
for
i=1,2
and
\sigma(E_{\infty})\subset L^{\perp}
.

To summarize, we have proved that there is an orthogonal splitting of

\Gamma
-modules
\mathscr{H}=L\oplus L^{\perp}
such that
\tau_{i}=\tau_{i}^{\prime}\oplus\tau_{i}^{\prime\prime}
preserves the splitting for
i=1,2,
,
\pi(L)=0
and
\operatorname{Im}(\sigma)\subset L^{\perp}
. Pick
\lambda\neq 1\in U(1)
and define

g\in U(\mathscr{H})^{\Gamma}
by setting
g=\lambda\operatorname{id}
on
L
and

g=\operatorname{id}
on
L^{\perp}
. Then
(g\tau_{1}g^{-1},g\tau_{2}g^{-1},\pi g^{-1},g\sigma)=(\tau_{1},\tau_{2},\pi,\sigma)
, so as
g\neq 1
and

U(\mathscr{H})^{\Gamma}
acts freely on
\mu_{N}^{-1}(0)-\{0\}
we conclude that

m=0
as desired.
∎

Proof of Proposition
4.1
.

Given an
adjacent pair
\alpha,\beta\in\mathcal{C}
let
E\rightarrow S^{4}
be the unique

\Gamma
-equivariant
\operatorname{SU}(2)
-bundle with
[E_{N}]=\alpha
and
[E_{S}]=\beta

and minimal
c_{2}(E)[S^{4}]>0
. Then by the above proposition
the moduli space
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{\Gamma,\infty}(E)
of
\Gamma
-invariant instantons framed at
\infty=N
may be identified with
\mathbb{R}\times Y_{\Gamma^{\prime}}
for
some other finite subgroup
\Gamma^{\prime}\subset\operatorname{SU}(2)
. Using the correspondence between invariant instantons in
E\rightarrow S^{4}
and instantons
over
\mathbb{R}\times\overline{Y}_{\Gamma}
, taking the framing and orientation
reversal into account, one obtains

\mathbb{R}\times Y_{\Gamma^{\prime}}\cong e_{-}^{-1}(*)\subset\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{z}(\alpha,\beta),

where
e_{-}\colon\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{z}(\alpha,\beta)\rightarrow\alpha
is the end-point map and
z
is the homotopy class from
\alpha
to
\beta
corresponding to the relative Chern number
\hat{c}_{2}=c_{2}(E)/|\Gamma|
. According to

[
Aus95
]
the natural translation action on the left hand side corresponds to the translation action on the right hand side. Hence,

Y_{\Gamma^{\prime}}\cong e_{-}^{-1}(*)\subset\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{z}^{0}(\alpha,\beta).

Since the end-point map is
\operatorname{SO}(3)
-equivariant it follows that

\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{z}^{0}(\alpha,\beta)=e_{-}^{-1}(*)\cdot\operatorname{SO}(3)
is compact.
Therefore,
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{z}^{0}(\alpha,\beta)=\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{z}(\alpha,\beta)
,
and as
\operatorname{gr}_{z}(\alpha,\beta)=\operatorname{dim}(e_{-}^{-1}(*))+1=4
, we conclude
that
z
is indeed the unique homotopy class with
-2\leq\operatorname{gr}_{z}(\alpha,\beta)\leq 5
.

To complete the proof we have to verify that
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{z}(\alpha,\beta)

is empty when
\alpha
and
\beta
are not adjacent in
\mathcal{S}_{\Gamma}
. Let

\alpha=\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{s}=\beta
be the vertices of
the minimal edge path connecting
\alpha
to
\beta
in
\mathcal{S}_{\Gamma}
.
Let
z_{i}
denote the unique homotopy class connecting
\alpha_{i}
to

\alpha_{i+1}
with
\operatorname{gr}_{z_{i}}(\alpha_{i},\alpha_{i+1})=4
for
each
0\leq i<s
. Let
\mathscr{H}_{i}
be the minimal positive solution of

(2-Q)\mathscr{H}_{i}=\alpha_{i}-\alpha_{i+1}
for
0\leq i<s
. By Corollary

4.14
the minimal positive solution of

(2-Q)\mathscr{H}=\alpha-\beta
is given by
\mathscr{H}=\bigoplus_{i=0}^{s-1}\mathscr{H}_{i}
.
Put
k=\epsilon(\mathscr{H})
and let
E^{l}\rightarrow S^{4}
denote the
\Gamma
-equivariant

\operatorname{SU}(2)
-bundle with invariants
([E^{l}_{N}],[E^{l}_{S}],c_{2}(E^{l})[S^{4}])=(\alpha,\beta,k+|\Gamma|l)
for
l\in\mathbb{Z}
. The representation
\mathscr{H}_{l}
used in
the description of the moduli space
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{\Gamma,\infty}(E^{l})

in the equivariant ADHM correspondence is then given by

\mathscr{H}_{l}=\mathscr{H}+lR
, where
R
is the regular representation. The dimension
of the moduli space increases linearly with
l
so, as
\mathscr{H}_{0}=\mathscr{H}
is the
minimal positive solution of
(2-Q)\mathscr{H}=\alpha-\beta
, we conclude that

\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{\Gamma,\infty}(E^{l})
is empty for
l<0
. This space
corresponds to the fiber
e_{-}^{-1}(*)\subset\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{w}(\alpha,\beta)
where

w=z_{0}*z_{1}*\cdots*z_{s-1}
. By Theorem
2.1
part
(a)
we
have

\operatorname{gr}_{w}(\alpha,\beta)=\sum_{i=0}^{s-1}\operatorname{gr}_{z_{i}}(\alpha_{i},\alpha_{i+1})=4s>4,

since by assumption
s>1
. Consequently, the fiber
e_{-}^{-1}(*)\subset\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{z}(\alpha,\beta)
where
z
is the unique homotopy class with
\operatorname{gr}_{z}(\alpha,\beta)=0,4
, corresponds to
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{\Gamma,\infty}(E^{l})

for some
l<0
and must therefore be empty. This implies that the whole
moduli space must be empty and the proof is complete.
∎

4.3.
The Structure of

DCI(\overline{Y}_{\Gamma})

In this section we determine the complexes
DCI(\overline{Y}_{\Gamma})

explicitly. Let
\Gamma\subset\operatorname{SU}(2)
be a finite subgroup and
let
\mathcal{C}
denote the set of gauge equivalence classes of flat connections in the trivial
\operatorname{SU}(2)
-bundle over
\overline{Y}_{\Gamma}
. In this section
we make the convention that the relative and absolute gradings

\operatorname{gr}\colon\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}/8\;\;\mbox{ and }\;\;j\colon\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}/8,

introduced after Theorem
2.1
, are always defined with respect to the orientation
\overline{Y}_{\Gamma}
of
S^{3}/\Gamma
. Furthermore,
the vertices of the graph
\mathcal{S}_{\Gamma}
, introduced in Definition
3.9
, i.e., the set of isomorphism classes of
\operatorname{SU}(2)
-representations of
\Gamma
, will be identified freely with the set

\mathcal{C}
.

Lemma 4.17
.

For any pair
\alpha,\beta\in\mathcal{C}
that are adjacent in
\mathcal{S}_{\Gamma}

it holds true that
\operatorname{gr}(\alpha,\beta)=4
. For each
\alpha\in\mathcal{C}
let

p(\alpha)
denote the number of edges in the minimal edge path connecting the
trivial representation
\theta
to
\alpha
. Then
j(\alpha)\equiv 4p(\alpha)\ (\mathrm{mod}\ 8)
for all
\alpha\in\mathcal{C}
.

Proof.

Suppose that
\alpha
and
\beta
are adjacent in
\mathcal{S}_{\Gamma}
and let
z
be the unique homotopy class with
-2\leq\operatorname{gr}_{z}(\alpha,\beta)\leq 5
. Then according to Theorem
4.1
the fiber of
e_{-}\colon\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{z}(\alpha,\beta)\rightarrow\alpha
has dimension
3
.
Using the dimension formula (
2.1
) we deduce that

\operatorname{gr}(\alpha,\beta)=\operatorname{dim}\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{z}(\alpha,\beta)-\operatorname{dim}(\alpha)+1=\operatorname{dim}e_{-}^{-1}(*)+1=4

proving the first assertion. For the second let
\alpha\in\mathcal{C}

be arbitrary and let
\theta=\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{s}=\alpha

denote the vertices in the minimal edge path connecting
\theta
to

\alpha
in
\mathcal{S}_{\Gamma}
. Then
p(\alpha)=s
and by the additivity of the grading

j(\alpha)=\operatorname{gr}(\alpha,\theta)=\sum_{i=0}^{s-1}\operatorname{gr}(\alpha_{i+1},\alpha_{i})=4s\in 4\mathbb{Z}/8.

Consequently,
j(\alpha)\equiv 4p(\alpha)\ (\mathrm{mod}\ 8)
as required. ∎

To determine the differentials in
DCI(\overline{Y}_{\Gamma})
we need the following result, which is contained in the proof of
[
Aus95
, Lemma 5.1]
.

Lemma 4.18
.

Let
\alpha,\beta\in\mathcal{C}
be adjacent in
\mathcal{S}_{\Gamma}
with

\beta
irreducible, let
\mathscr{H}\in R(\Gamma)
be the solution of

(2-Q)\mathscr{H}=\alpha-\beta

given by Proposition
4.12
and let
\Gamma^{\prime}\subset\operatorname{SU}(2)
be the corresponding finite subgroup.
Let
z
be the unique homotopy class with

\operatorname{gr}_{z}(\alpha,\beta)=4
. Then the degree of the map

e_{-}^{-1}(*)=Y_{\Gamma^{\prime}}\rightarrow\beta=\operatorname{SO}(3)

is up to a sign given by
2\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\mathscr{H}/|\Gamma^{\prime}|
.

Remark 4.19
.

We are not aware of a procedure to pin down the orientation
of
Y_{\Gamma^{\prime}}
in the above statement. For this reason we are only
able to determine the degree up to a sign. As we will see, this inaccuracy
will not affect our calculations in any relevant way.

For
i=0,1
let
\mathcal{C}^{i}=\{\alpha\in\mathcal{C}:j(\alpha)\equiv 4i\mod{8}\}

such that
\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{C}^{0}\cup\mathcal{C}^{i}
is a disjoint union. Let
us fix generators
b_{\alpha}\in H_{0}(\alpha)
for all
\alpha\in\mathcal{C}
,
and
t_{\alpha}\in H_{2}(\alpha)
for all reducible
\alpha
and

t_{\alpha}:=b_{\alpha}u\in H_{3}(\alpha)
for all irreducible
\alpha
.

Definition 4.20
.

Let
\mathcal{C}^{irr}\subset\mathcal{C}
be the subset of
irreducibles. For each adjacent pair
\alpha,\beta\in\mathcal{C}
with

\beta\in\mathcal{C}^{irr}
define
n_{\beta\alpha}
to be the integer

2\operatorname{dim}_{C}\mathscr{H}/|\Gamma^{\prime}|
where
\mathscr{H}\in R(\Gamma)
is the minimal
positive solution of the equation
(2-Q)\mathscr{H}=\alpha-\beta
. If

\alpha
and
\beta
are not adjacent in

\mathcal{S}_{\Gamma}
we define
n_{\beta\alpha}=0
.

Theorem 4.21
.

Let
\Gamma\subset\operatorname{SU}(2)
be a finite subgroup.
Then the multicomplex
(DCI(\overline{Y}_{\Gamma})_{*,*},\{\partial^{r}\}_{r\geq 0})

is given by

DCI_{8s,*}=\bigoplus_{\alpha\in\mathcal{C}^{0}}H_{*}(\alpha)\mbox{ and }DCI_{8s+4,*}=\bigoplus_{\beta\in\mathcal{C}^{1}}H_{*}(\beta)

for all
s\in\mathbb{Z}
and
DCI_{s,*}=0
otherwise. The differentials
are given by
\partial^{r}=0
for
r\neq 4
and

\partial^{4}\colon DCI_{4s,0}\rightarrow DCI_{4(s-1),3}
is determined on generators by

\partial^{4}(b_{\beta})=\sum_{\alpha\in\mathcal{C}^{irr}}n_{\alpha\beta}t_{\alpha}

where the integers
n_{\alpha\beta}
are defined above.

Proof.

The description of
DCI(Y,E)
as a graded module is a simple
consequence of the definition and Lemma
4.17
. By the final
part of Theorem
4.1
the moduli space

\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{z}(\alpha,\beta)
, where
z
is the homotopy class with

-2\leq\operatorname{gr}_{z}(\alpha,\beta)\leq 5
, is empty unless
\alpha
and

\beta
are adjacent in
\mathcal{S}_{\Gamma}
. The
statement concerning the differentials therefore follows from
Lemma
2.7
and Lemma
4.18
. ∎

We will end this section by giving a few applications of Proposition

4.12
and show how we can easily extract both
the grading and the integers
n_{\alpha\beta}
provided we know where
the one dimensional quaternionic representations are placed in the
graph
\overline{\Delta}_{\Gamma}
. All the necessary information is
contained in Appendix
A
.

Example 4.22
.

(
\Gamma=O^{*}
) There are four
1
-dimensional quaternionic
representations; two of real type
\theta,\eta
and two of quaternionic
type
\alpha
,

. The graph
\overline{\Delta}_{O^{*}}
is shown
below.

The integers are the dimensions of the corresponding irreducible complex representations and we have labeled the vertices corresponding to the

of complex type, in particular
\overline{\Delta}_{\Gamma}=\overline{\Delta}_{\Gamma}/(\iota)
. One may immediately conclude that the
indexing is given by
j(\theta)=j(\beta)=0
and
j(\alpha)=j(\eta)=4
.
To determine the integer
n_{\beta\eta}
we need to solve the equation

(2-Q)\mathscr{H}=\eta-\beta
in
R(O^{*})
. According to the recipe given
in Proposition
4.12
we first delete the vertex
corresponding to
\beta
and let
\Delta_{\mathscr{H}}
be the component containing

\eta
; that is,
\Delta_{\mathscr{H}}
is just the single vertex corresponding to

. We then recognize this graph as
\Delta_{C_{2}}
, from which we deduce that
\Gamma^{\prime}=C_{2}
and
\mathscr{H}=\frac{1}{2}\eta
. Therefore,
n_{\beta\eta}=2\operatorname{dim}_{\mathbb{C}}\mathscr{H}/|\Gamma^{\prime}|=2/2=1
. The situation with

and

as well.

The case of

is more interesting.
If we delete
\alpha
, the component
\Delta_{\mathscr{H}}
containing
\beta
is given by
1
2
2
2
1
1

We recognize this as the Dynkin graph
D_{6}
which corresponds to the group

D_{4}^{*}
of order
16
. We find the integral weights of
\mathscr{H}
, as given in the above graph, by knowledge of the dimensions of the irreducible representations of
D_{4}^{*}
(see Appendix
A.2
). From this we calculate

\operatorname{dim}_{\mathbb{C}}\mathscr{H}=3\cdot 1+4\cdot 2+2\cdot 1+3\cdot 2+2\cdot 2+1\cdot 1=24

and hence

. By symmetry

as well.

The calculation of
n_{\alpha\theta}
and
n_{\beta\eta}
in the above example
generalizes.

Lemma 4.23
.

Let
\Gamma\subset\operatorname{SU}(2)
be a finite subgroup and let

Q
be the canonical representation. Suppose
\rho
is a
1
-dimensional
complex representation of real type and that
\rho\otimes Q
is irreducible
so that
\eta=2\rho
and
\beta=\rho\otimes Q

correspond to a pair of distinct vertices in
\mathcal{S}_{\Gamma}
. Then

n_{\beta\eta}=1
.

Proof.

We trivially have
(2-Q)\rho=2\rho-Q\otimes\rho=\eta-\beta

so that
\mathscr{H}=\rho
. This means that
\Delta_{\mathscr{H}}
consists of a single
vertex and that the associated subgroup is
\Gamma^{\prime}=C_{2}
. It follows that

n_{\beta\eta}=2\operatorname{dim}_{\mathbb{C}}\mathscr{H}/|\Gamma^{\prime}|=2/2=1
. ∎

We also consider the most involved case
\Gamma=D_{n}^{*}
. The
1
-dimensional
quaternionic representations and the graph
\overline{\Delta}_{D_{n}^{*}}

are given in Appendix
A.2
. For
n
even we label the quaternionic representations by

\theta,\eta_{1},\eta_{2},\eta_{3},\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\cdots,\alpha_{n/2}

and for
n
odd by

\theta,\eta,\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\cdots,\alpha_{(n-1)/2},\lambda.

Here
\theta,\eta
and the
\eta_{i}
are fully reducible, the
\alpha_{i}

are irreducible and
\lambda
is reducible.

Lemma 4.24
.

In the above situation we have
n_{\alpha_{i},\alpha_{i+1}}=n_{\alpha_{i+1},\alpha_{i}}=2
for all
i
.

Proof.

Due to the symmetry in the graph
\overline{\Delta}_{D_{n}^{*}}
it suffices to
show that
n_{\alpha_{i+1},\alpha_{i}}=2
for all
i
. We therefore have
to solve
(2-Q)\mathscr{H}=\alpha_{i}-\alpha_{i+1}
for
\mathscr{H}
. The relevant
portion of the graph
\overline{\Delta}_{D_{n}^{*}}
is shown below
1
1
2
2
2
2
2
\alpha_{i}
\alpha_{i+1}

Note that
\alpha_{i}
is the
2i-1
’th vertex labeled with

from left
to right. Following the description in Proposition
4.12

we delete the vertex corresponding to
\alpha_{i+1}
and let

\Delta_{\mathscr{H}}
be the component containing
\alpha_{i}
. This graph
has a total of
2(i+1)
vertices so we recognize
\Delta_{\mathscr{H}}=D_{2(i+1)}=\Delta_{D^{*}_{2i}}
. Thus
\Gamma^{\prime}=D_{2i}^{*}
of order
8i
. The weights of
\mathscr{H}
are therefore given by attaching the integer
2
to each internal
vertex (of degree
\geq 2
) and
1
to the rest. This yields

n_{\alpha_{i+1}\alpha_{i}}=2\operatorname{dim}_{\mathbb{C}}\mathscr{H}/|D_{2i}^{*}|=2(1+1+4(2i-1)+2)/8i=2

as required.
∎

We note that for cyclic groups there are no irreducible

\operatorname{SU}(2)
-representations, from which it follows that

DCI(\overline{Y}_{C_{m}})
has trivial differential for each
m
.
There are now only a finite number of
n_{\alpha\beta}
left to calculate. We state the calculations
of these below without proof and trust that the
interested reader will verify these for themselves.

For a finite subgroup
\Gamma\subset\operatorname{SU}(2)
we attach labels
to the edges in the graph

to express all the information
needed to set up

. If
\alpha
and
\beta

are adjacent and both irreducible we attach the symbol
(n_{\alpha\beta}|n_{\beta\alpha})
to the edge connecting
\alpha
to
\beta
such that

n_{\alpha\beta}
is closest to
\alpha
. If
\alpha

and
\beta
are adjacent and only
\alpha
is irreducible we attach

n_{\alpha\beta}
to the edge.
