5.
The Algebraic Construction of Equivariant Floer Homology

The equivariant instanton Floer homology associated with the
trivial
\operatorname{SU}(2)
-bundle over a closed oriented
3
-manifold
Y

comes in three flavors:
I^{+}(Y)
,
I^{-}(Y)
,
I^{\infty}(Y)
, the positive, negative and Tate homology, respectively. These are all constructed algebraically
from the complex
\widetilde{CI}(Y)
, the associated action of
C_{*}^{gm}(\operatorname{SO}(3);R)
and the index filtration
\{F_{p}\widetilde{CI}(Y)\}_{p\in\mathbb{Z}}
.

The construction proceeds as follows. First, following
[
Eis19
, Appendix A]
, we review the construction of four functors

C^{+}_{A}
,
C^{-}_{A}
,
C^{(+,tw)}
and
C^{\infty}_{A}
from the category of right modules over a differential graded
R
-algebra
A
to the category of differential
graded
R
-modules. In fact,
C^{-}_{A}(R)
will carry the structure of
a differential graded algebra and the three functors take values in the
subcategory of left
C^{-}_{A}(R)
-modules. Under the assumptions that the
ground ring
R
is a principal ideal domain and the algebra
A
is
degreewise free over
R
, the four functors will be
exact and preserve quasi-isomorphisms.

Next, we promote the functors to the category of filtered right
A
-modules.
If
M
is a right
A
-module equipped with an increasing filtration

\cdots\subset F_{p}M\subset F_{p+1}M\subset\cdots\subset M

of
A
-submodules, the exactness of the functors ensure that
C^{\bullet}_{A}(M)
is naturally filtered by

F_{p}C^{\bullet}_{A}(M):=C^{\bullet}_{A}(F_{p}M)
for each
\bullet\in\{+,-,(+,tw),\infty\}
. However, this filtration may not be very well behaved. To remedy this issue we pass to the so-called full completion,
thereby obtaining four new functors
\hat{C}^{\bullet}_{A}
,
\bullet\in\{(+,-,(+,tw),\infty\}
from the category of filtered right
A
-modules to the category of filtered left

C^{-}_{A}(R)
-modules; to be specific,

\hat{C}^{\bullet}_{A}(M)=\operatorname{lim}_{q}\operatorname{colim}_{p}C^{\bullet}_{A}(F_{p}M/F_{q}M).

For
A=C^{gm}_{*}(\operatorname{SO}(3);R)
the groups
I^{\bullet}(Y)
for
\bullet\in\{+,-,\infty\}
are obtained
by applying
\hat{C}^{\bullet}_{A}
to
M=\widetilde{CI}(Y)
equipped with
the index filtration and then passing to homology. These groups will then be modules over
H(C^{-}_{A}(R))
, which, provided
\frac{1}{2}\in R
, is
isomorphic to the polynomial algebra
R[U]
with a single generator

U
of degree
-4
. The mod
8
periodicity of
M
will be preserved so that
I^{\bullet}_{n}\cong I^{\bullet}_{n+8}
for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
.

The above constructions are also functorial in a suitable sense depending on

\bullet\in\{+,-,\infty\}
, in the differential graded algebra
A
.
Moreover, this functoriality will preserve quasi-isomorphisms in the
situation of interest; that is, we may equally well calculate

I^{\bullet}(Y)
using
A=\Lambda_{R}[u]
and
M=DCI(Y)
. When we proceed
to the calculations for binary polyhedral spaces in the next section, we
will indeed work in this context.

5.1.
Conventions

Throughout
R
will be a fixed principal ideal domain.
A module or algebra will always mean an
R
-module or
R
-algebra, and
the same applies in the graded or differential graded setting. We will
work with homological gradings and differentials
\partial
of degree
-1
.
We follow the conventions on graded and differential graded (DG) modules given in
[
Mac95
, Chapter VI]
. We briefly recall the most central
concepts. If
M
and
N
are DG modules then
M\otimes N
and
\operatorname{Hom}(M,N)
are the DG modules given by

(M\otimes N)_{n}=\bigoplus_{p+q=n}M_{n}\otimes N_{q}\;\mbox{ and }\;\operatorname{Hom}(M,N)_{n}=\prod_{q-p=n}\operatorname{Hom}(M_{p},N_{q})

with differentials
\partial_{M\otimes N}=\partial_{M}\otimes 1+1\otimes\partial_{N}

and
\partial_{\operatorname{Hom}(M,N)}=\operatorname{Hom}(1,\partial_{N})-\operatorname{Hom}(\partial_{M},1)
.
This means that
\partial_{M\otimes N}(a\otimes b)=\partial_{M}a\otimes b+(-1)^{|a|}a\otimes\partial_{N}b
and
\partial_{\operatorname{Hom}(M,N)}(f)=\partial_{N}\circ f-(-1)^{|f|}f\circ\partial_{M}
, where we write
|x|
for the degree of an element in a graded module.
There is a natural interchange isomorphism
\tau:M\otimes N\cong N\otimes M

given by
m\otimes n\mapsto(-1)^{|m||n|}n\otimes m
.

For each integer
p\in\mathbb{Z}
let
R[p]
denote the complex with a single
copy of
R
concentrated in degree
p
. For a DG module
M
the shifted
complex
M[p]
is defined to be
R[p]\otimes M
. Therefore,
M[p]_{n+p}=M_{n}

for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
with differential
\partial_{M[p]}=(-1)^{p}\partial_{M}
. It is also
possible to define
M[p]=M\otimes R[p]
, but this will result in different
sign conventions.

A differential graded algebra is a graded algebra
A
equipped with a
differential satisfying the Leibniz rule
\partial_{A}(ab)=\partial_{A}(a)b+(-1)^{|a|}a\partial_{A}b
for all
a,b\in A
. We will always assume that
A
is unital, i.e., there
is a unit
1\in A_{0}
for the multiplication. A left or right
A
-module is a graded left or right
A
-module equipped with a differential satisfying a corresponding Leibniz rule. This ensures that
H(M)
is a graded

H(A)
-module.
If
M
is a left or right
A
-module then so is
M[p]
. For
a\in A
and

x\in M[p]_{n}=M_{n-p}
the induced product
is given by
a\cdot x=(-1)^{|a|p}ax
in the left module context and by
x\cdot a=xa
in the right module context. This is a consequence of our left shifting (suspension) convention.

We also mention that there is a natural adjunction isomorphism of DG modules

\operatorname{Hom}(M\otimes N,P)\cong\operatorname{Hom}(M,\operatorname{Hom}(N,P))

given by
(f\colon M\otimes N\rightarrow P)\mapsto(g\colon M\rightarrow\operatorname{Hom}(N,P))
where

f(m\otimes n)=g(m)(n)
. This isomorphism admits various natural generalizations to the case where
M
,
N
and
P
carry additional module
structures over various DG algebras, see
[
Mac95
, Chapter VI.8]
.
All the signs occurring in the upcoming theory can be deduced from
these conventions: the interchange morphism, the natural adjunction and
the left shift convention.

Finally, a homomorphism
f\colon M\rightarrow N
of DG modules will mean a degree
0

chain map unless otherwise stated. We say
that
f
is a quasi-isomorphism if the induced map in homology,

H(f)\colon H(M)\rightarrow H(N)
, is an isomorphism.

5.2.
The Bar Construction and the Functors
C^{\pm}_{A}

Let
A
be a unital DG algebra and assume that
A
is equipped with an augmentation
\epsilon\colon A\rightarrow R
, that is,
\epsilon
is a map of DG algebras
where
R
is regarded as a DG algebra concentrated in degree
0
.
The augmentation gives
R
the structure of an
A
-bimodule.
We define
\bar{A}=A/R\cdot 1
and note that there is a canonical
identification of
\bar{A}
with the augmentation ideal
\operatorname{Ker}(\epsilon)
.
Given a right
A
-module
M
and a left
A
-module
N
define
the bigraded module
B_{*,*}(M,A,N)
by

B_{p,q}(M,A,N)=(M\otimes\bar{A}^{\otimes p}\otimes N)_{q}.

We adopt the standard notation and write

m[a_{1}|a_{2}|\cdots|a_{p}]n\coloneqq m\otimes a_{1}\otimes a_{2}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{p}\otimes n\in B_{p,q}(M,A,N).

Here,
p
is the simplicial degree,
q=|m|+\left(\sum_{i=1}^{p}|a_{i}|\right)+|n|
is the internal degree and
p+q
is the total degree. Let

\partial^{s}\colon B_{p,q}(M,A,N)\rightarrow B_{p-1,q}(M,A,N)
and
\partial^{i}\colon B_{p,q}(M,A,N)\rightarrow B_{p,q-1}(M,A,N)
act on
x=m[a_{1}|\cdots|a_{p}]n
by

\displaystyle\partial^{s}(x)=

\displaystyle\;ma_{1}[a_{2}|\cdots|a_{p}]n+\sum_{i=1}^{p-1}(-1)^{i}m[a_{1}|\cdots|a_{i}a_{i+1}|\cdots|a_{p}]n

\displaystyle\;+

\displaystyle(-1)^{p}m[a_{1}|\cdots|a_{p-1}]a_{p}n

\displaystyle\partial^{i}(x)=

\displaystyle\;(-1)^{p}(\partial_{M}m[a_{1}|\cdots|a_{p}]n+\sum_{i=1}^{p}(-1)^{\epsilon_{i-1}}m[a_{1}|\cdots|\partial a_{i}|\cdots|a_{p}]n

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\;(-1)^{\epsilon_{p}}m[a_{1}|\cdots|a_{p}]\partial_{N}n),

where
\epsilon_{i}=|m|+|a_{1}|+\cdots+|a_{i}|
. Note that
\partial^{i}
is the usual tensor product differential in
M\otimes\bar{A}^{\otimes p}\otimes N
twisted by a sign depending on the simplicial degree. We call
\partial^{s}
the simplicial differential and
\partial^{i}
the internal differential. It holds true that

(\partial^{i})^{2}=0
,
(\partial^{s})^{2}=0
and
\partial^{i}\partial^{s}+\partial^{s}\partial^{i}=0
. This ensures that the triple
(B_{*,*}(M,A,N),\partial^{s},\partial^{i})
is a double complex.

Definition 5.1
.

[
GM74
, Appendix A]

The bar construction
B(M,A,N)
is the total complex associated
with the double complex
(B_{*,*}(M,A,N),\partial^{s},\partial^{i})
introduced above,
that is,

B_{k}(M,A,N)=\bigoplus_{p+q=n}B_{p,q}(M,A,N)\;\mbox{ and }\;\partial=\partial^{s}+\partial^{i}.

The bar construction is functorial in the triple
(M,A,N)
. For a triple

(f,g,h)\colon(M,A,N)\rightarrow(M^{\prime},A^{\prime},N^{\prime})
where
g
is a map of augmented DG algebras
and
f,h
are chain maps linear along
g
, there is an induced map

B(f,g,h)\colon B(M,A,N)\rightarrow B(M^{\prime},A^{\prime},N^{\prime})
of DG modules.
There is also a natural augmentation
B(M,A,N)\rightarrow M\otimes_{A}N
given by

m[a_{1}|\cdots|a_{p}]n\mapsto 0
for
p>0
and
m[]n\mapsto m\otimes n
.

Remark 5.2
.

The DG module
B(M,A,A)
inherits a right
A
-module
structure from the last factor. The augmentation
B(M,A,A)\rightarrow M
is a map of
right
A
-modules and should be regarded as an analogue of a projective
resolution of
M
. Moreover,
B(M,A,N)\cong B(M,A,A)\otimes_{A}N
,
so that
B(M,A,N)
is a derived tensor product
M\otimes^{L}_{A}N

in a certain sense. These statements are made precise in the language
of model categories in
[
BMR14
, Section 10.2]
.

Following
[
Eis19
]
we introduce the notation

BA\coloneqq B(R,A,R)\;\;\mbox{ and }\;\;EA\coloneqq B(R,A,A).

Furthermore, we write

[a_{1}|a_{2}|\cdots|a_{p}]\coloneqq 1_{R}[a_{1}|a_{2}|\cdots|a_{p}]1_{R}\in BA
and similar
conventions apply for
B(R,A,N)
and
B(M,A,R)
.

Lemma 5.3
.

For any right
A
-module
M
and left
A
-module
N
there is a natural map
of DG modules
\psi_{M,N}\colon B(M,A,N)\rightarrow B(M,A,R)\otimes B(R,A,N)
given by

m[a_{1}|\cdots|a_{p}]n\mapsto\sum_{i=0}^{p}(-1)^{(p-i)\epsilon_{i}}m[a_{1}|\cdots|a_{i}]\otimes[a_{i+1}|\cdots|a_{p}]n,

where
\epsilon_{i}=|m|+|a_{1}|+\cdots+|a_{i}|
.
The map
\psi_{R,R}\colon BA\rightarrow BA\otimes BA
along with the augmentation

BA\rightarrow R
give
BA
the structure of a DG coalgebra. The map

\psi_{M,R}
gives
B(M,A,R)
the structure of a right DG
BA
-comodule
and the map
\psi_{R,N}
gives
B(R,A,N)
the structure of a left DG
BA
-comodule.

Definition 5.4
.

[
Eis19
, Definition A.5]
Let
A
be an augmented
DG algebra and let
M
be a right
A
-module. Define the positive and
negative
A
-chains of
M
to be

C^{+}_{A}(M)\coloneqq B(M,A,R)\;\;\mbox{ and }\;\;C^{-}_{A}(M)\coloneqq\operatorname{Hom}_{A}(EA,M).

The homology of
C^{\pm}_{A}(M)
is denoted by
H^{\pm}_{A}(M)
and is called
the positive and negative
A
-homology of
M
, respectively.

The above constructions are functorial in the following sense.
A homomorphism
f\colon A\rightarrow B
of augmented DG algebras induces maps

f_{*}\colon C^{+}_{A}(f^{-1}M)\rightarrow C^{+}_{B}(M)\;\mbox{ and }\;f^{*}\colon C^{-}_{B}(M)\rightarrow C^{-}_{A}(f^{-1}M)

where
M
is a
B
-module and
f^{-1}M
is the
A
-module obtained from
M
by restriction along
f
. Furthermore, a homomorphism
g\colon M\rightarrow N
of
A
-modules induces maps

g^{+}\colon C^{+}_{A}(M)\rightarrow C^{+}_{A}(N)\;\mbox{ and }\;g^{-}\colon C^{-}_{A}(M)\rightarrow C^{-}_{A}(N).

The following invariance result is proven in
[
Eis19
, Theorem A.1,A.2]
;
it relies on the standing assumption that
R
is a PID.

Proposition 5.5
.

Let
f\colon A^{\prime}\rightarrow A
is a homomorphism of augmented
DG algebras and let
g\colon M\rightarrow M^{\prime}
be a homomorphism of
A
-modules.

(i)

If
A
and
A^{\prime}
are degreewise free and
f
is a quasi-isomorphism,
then
f_{*}
and
f^{*}
are quasi-isomorphisms.

(ii)

If
A
is degreewise free and
g
is a quasi-isomorphism, then

g^{+}
and
g^{-}
are quasi-isomorphisms.

The DG modules
C^{\pm}_{A}(M)
carry additional structure. First, we have
the following sequence of isomorphisms of DG modules

C_{A}^{-}(R)=\operatorname{Hom}_{A}(EA,\epsilon^{-1}(R))\cong\operatorname{Hom}_{R}(EA\otimes_{A}R,R)\cong\operatorname{Hom}(BA,R),

which identifies
C_{A}^{-}(R)
with the
R
-dual of
BA
. The DG coalgebra structure of
BA
dualizes to give
C_{A}^{-}(R)
the structure of a DG algebra.
Next, for any right
A
-module
M
the DG modules
C^{\pm}_{A}(M)
obtain
left
C_{A}^{-}(R)
-module structures in the following way. Write

BA^{\vee}=\operatorname{Hom}_{R}(BA,R)=C_{A}^{-}(R)
, then the structure map

C_{A}^{-}(R)\otimes C^{+}_{A}(M)\rightarrow C^{+}_{A}(M)
is given by the following composition

(5.1)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces BA^{\vee}\otimes C^{+}_{A}(M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces BA^{\vee}\otimes C^{+}_{A}(M)\otimes BA}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{+}_{A}(M)\otimes BA^{\vee}\otimes BA}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{+}_{A}(M)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1\otimes\psi_{M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau\otimes 1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1\otimes\operatorname{ev}}

where
\psi_{M}=\psi_{M,R}
is the comodule structure map of

C^{+}_{A}(M)=B(M,A,R)
in Lemma
5.3
,
\tau
is the interchange isomorphism
a\otimes b\mapsto(-1)^{|a||b|}b\otimes a
and
\operatorname{ev}\colon BA^{\vee}\otimes BA\rightarrow R
is the evaluation map. In the other case the structure map
C_{A}^{-}(R)\otimes C^{-}_{A}(M)\rightarrow C_{A}^{-}(M)

is given by the following composition

(5.2)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces BA^{\vee}\otimes\operatorname{Hom}_{A}(EA,M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Hom}_{A}(BA\otimes EA,M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Hom}_{A}(EA,M)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\zeta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi_{EA}^{*}}

where
\zeta(f\otimes g)(a\otimes b)=(-1)^{|a||g|}f(a)\cdot g(b)

and
\psi_{EA}^{*}=\operatorname{Hom}_{A}(\psi_{R,A},1)
is the dual of the
comodule structure map
\psi_{R,A}\colon EA\rightarrow BA\otimes EA
.

The properties of the functors
C^{\pm}_{A}
that will be important for us
are summarized in the following theorem. Here the morphisms in the
category of right or left modules over a DG algebra
A
are taken to
be the
A
-linear chain maps of degree
0
.

Theorem 5.6
.

Let
A
be a degreewise
R
-free augmented DG algebra.
Then the assignments

M\mapsto C^{\pm}_{A}(M)\;\mbox{ and }\;(g\colon M\rightarrow M^{\prime})\mapsto(g^{\pm}\colon C^{\pm}_{A}(M)\rightarrow C^{\pm}_{A}(M^{\prime})

define functors
C^{\pm}_{A}
from the category of right
A
-modules
to the category of left
C_{A}^{-}(R)
-modules. Moreover, these functors
preserve quasi-isomorphisms and short exact sequences.

We will now treat the case
A=\Lambda_{R}[u]
with
|u|=3
in detail. These calculations will be important for later purposes and will serve as an excellent example of how the above constructions work. We should note
that all of these calculations easily generalize to the case where
the degree of
u
is any odd number.

We will write
R[U]
for the graded polynomial algebra on a single generator

U
of even non-zero degree. This is also a coalgebra with counit given
by the identity in degree
0
and diagonal determined by

U^{p}\mapsto\sum_{i=0}^{p}U^{i}\otimes U^{p-i}

where
U^{0}=1
. This structure is compatible with the algebra structure and turns
R[U]
into a Hopf algebra, but this will not be important for us.

Lemma 5.7
.

Let
A=\Lambda_{R}[u]
with
|u|=3
. Then there is an isomorphism
of DG coalgebras
BA\cong R[V]
where
|V|=4
, and an isomorphism of
DG algebras
C_{A}^{-}(R)=BA^{\vee}\cong R[U]
where
|U|=-4
. In particular, these
complexes have trivial differentials so that

H^{+}_{A}(R)=R[V]
and
H^{-}_{A}(R)=R[U]
. The first isomorphism is given by

V^{p}\mapsto(-1)^{p(p+1)/2}[u|\cdots|u]\;\;\mbox{ (p times) }

for
p\geq 0
. The second is obtained by dualization of the first
where
U^{p}
is determined by the evaluation
U^{p}(V^{p})=1
for all
p\geq 0
. Furthermore, the left
BA^{\vee}
-module structure of
BA
is given by

U^{s}\cdot V^{p}=V^{p-s}
, where we interpret
V^{i}=0
for
i<0
.

Proof.

We have
A=\Lambda_{R}[u]
so that
A_{0}=R
,
A_{3}=Ru
and

A_{n}=0
otherwise. The augmentation is necessarily given by the identity
in degree
0
. Hence,
\overline{A}=Ru
concentrated in degree
3
. From the
definition of
B(R,A,R)
we find that
BA_{p,3p}=R\cdot Z^{p}
for
p\geq 0
, where
Z^{p}=[u|\cdots|u]
with
u
repeated
p
times, and
B_{p,q}=0
otherwise. It follows immediately that the differential vanishes identically.
Using the formula for the coalgebra structure map in Lemma
5.3
we find that

\psi_{R,R}(Z^{p})=\sum_{i=0}^{p}(-1)^{(p-i)3i}Z^{i}\otimes Z^{p-i}.

Define
V^{p}=(-1)^{p(p+1)/2}Z^{p}
for
p\geq 0
. Then using the identity

\frac{p(p+1)}{2}=(p-i)i+\frac{i(i+1)}{2}+\frac{(p-i)(p-i+1)}{2}

we deduce that the above formula transforms into

\psi_{R,R}(V^{p})=\sum_{i=0}^{p}V^{i}\otimes V^{p-i}
.
Hence,
BA\cong R[V]
as DG coalgebras with trivial differentials.

Define
U^{p}\in BA^{\vee}
to be the dual of
V^{p}
, i.e.,
U^{p}\colon BA\rightarrow R

has degree
-4p
and acts by
U^{p}(V^{p})=1
and vanishes otherwise. The differential is still of course trivial. The product
U^{s}\cdot U^{t}
has degree

-4(s+t)
and must therefore be an
R
-multiple of
U^{s+t}
. The
calculation

(U^{s}\cdot U^{t})(V^{s+t})=(U^{s}\otimes U^{t})\left(\sum_{i=0}^{s+t}V^{i}\otimes V^{s+t-i}\right)=U^{s}(V^{s})U^{t}(V^{t})=1

shows that
U^{s}\cdot U^{t}=U^{s+t}
. Hence,
C_{A}^{-}(R)\cong BA^{\vee}\cong R[U]

as DG algebras with trivial differentials.
Finally, we need to check that the left module structure
BA^{\vee}\otimes BA\rightarrow BA
is given by
U^{s}\cdot V^{p}=V^{p-s}
. Using the definition we find

U^{s}\cdot V^{p}=\sum_{i=0}^{p}(-1)^{|U^{s}||V^{i}|}V^{i}\cdot U^{s}(V^{p-i})=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}V^{p-s}&\mbox{ if }p\geq s\\
0&\mbox{ otherwise.}\end{array}\right.

as required.
∎

We will also need explicit calculations of
H^{\pm}_{A}(M)
when

M=H_{*}(\alpha;R)
for
\alpha=\operatorname{SO}(3)/\operatorname{SO}(2)
or
\alpha=\operatorname{SO}(3)
,
under the assumption
\frac{1}{2}\in R
. In other words,

M=R\oplus R[2]
with the trivial
A
-module structure or

M=A=\Lambda_{R}[u]
(see (
2.2
)). For this purpose we give concrete models for the complexes
C^{\pm}_{A}(M)
. Note that a right

\Lambda_{R}[u]
-module
M
is simply a DG module equipped with a degree

3
map
u:M\rightarrow M
satisfying
u^{2}=0
and
u\partial_{M}=\partial_{M}u
(because
A
acts from the right).

Proposition 5.8
.

Let
M
be a right
A=\Lambda_{R}[u]
-module. Define
bigraded modules
D^{\pm}_{*,*}
by

D^{+}_{p,q}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}M_{q-3p}&p\geq 0\\
0&p<0\end{array}\right.\;\mbox{ and }\;D^{-}_{p,q}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}M_{q-3p}&p\leq 0\\
0&p>0\end{array}\right..

Define
U\colon D^{\pm}_{p,q}\rightarrow D^{\pm}_{p-1,q-3}
to be the identity
M_{q-3p}\rightarrow M_{q-3p}
when
p\geq 1
and
p\leq 0
, respectively, and otherwise to be
zero. Define

\displaystyle\partial^{\prime}=(-1)^{p+q+1}u\colon D^{\pm}_{p,q}

\displaystyle=M_{q-3p}\rightarrow M_{q-3p+3}=D^{\pm}_{p-1,q}

\displaystyle\partial^{\prime\prime}=\partial_{M}\colon D^{\pm}_{p,q}

\displaystyle=M_{q-3p}\rightarrow M_{q-3p-1}=D^{\pm}_{p,q-1},

in the range it makes sense. Then
(D^{+}_{*,*},\partial^{\prime},\partial^{\prime\prime})
and
(D^{-}_{*,*},\partial^{\prime},\partial^{\prime\prime})
are double complexes and there are natural isomorphisms of
DG
R[U]
-modules

\operatorname{Tot}^{\oplus}(D^{+}_{*,*},\partial^{\prime},\partial^{\prime\prime})\cong C^{+}_{A}(M)\;\;\mbox{ and }\;\;\operatorname{Tot}^{\Pi}(D^{-}_{*,*},\partial^{\prime},\partial^{\prime\prime})\cong C^{-}_{A}(M).

Proof.

We have
\bar{A}=R\cdot u
with
u
in degree
3
. Therefore,

B_{p,q}=(M\otimes\bar{A}^{\otimes p})_{q}\cong M_{q-3p}\otimes\bar{A}^{\otimes p}_{3p}\cong M_{q-3p}
. The isomorphism is given by

m\mapsto m[u|u|\cdots|u]
with
u
repeated
p
times. For simplicity
of notation write this as
m\otimes Z^{p}
. Then, using the formulas for
the differentials, we find
\partial^{s}(m\otimes Z^{p})=mu\otimes Z^{p-1}

and
\partial^{i}(m\otimes Z^{p})=(-1)^{p}\partial_{M}m\otimes Z^{p}
. The action of

U
is given by first applying the comodule structure map to

m\otimes Z^{p}
, and then evaluating
U
on the right factors in the
resulting sum (
U
has even degree so the interchange introduces no sign).
We have

\psi_{M,R}(m\otimes Z^{p})=\sum_{i=0}^{p}(-1)^{(p-i)(|m|+3i)}(m\otimes Z^{i})\otimes Z^{p-i}

so that
U\cdot(m\otimes Z^{p})=(-1)^{|m|+p-1}(m\otimes Z^{p-1})\cdot U(Z^{1})=(-1)^{|m|+p}m\otimes Z^{p-1}
as
U
was defined to be the dual of

V^{1}=-Z^{1}
. It is now easy to check that when we introduce the
sign change
m\in D^{+}_{p,q}=M_{q-3p}\mapsto(-1)^{p(p+1)/2+p|m|}m\otimes Z^{p}

the
U
action and the differentials are given as in the proposition.

In the second case,
EA=B(R,A,A)
has generators over
R
of the form

Z^{p}=[u|u|\cdots|u]
and
Z^{p}\otimes u=[u|u|\cdots|u]u

in degrees
4p
and
4p+3
respectively. It is convenient as earlier
to introduce the sign twist
V^{p}=(-1)^{p(p+1)/2}Z^{p}
, since then the
differential is given by
\partial(V^{p})=V^{p-1}\otimes u
and

\partial(V^{p}\otimes u)=0
and the comodule structure map
\psi_{R,A}\colon EA\rightarrow BA\otimes EA
is given by

\psi_{R,A}(V^{p})=\sum_{i=0}^{p}V^{i}\otimes V^{p-i}.

An element
f\in\operatorname{Hom}_{A}(EA,M)_{n}
is by
A
-linearity uniquely determined
by the elements
f(V^{p})\in M_{n+4p}
for
p\geq 0
. The differential is given
by

(\partial f)(V^{p})=\partial_{M}(f(V^{p}))-(-1)^{|f|}f(\partial V^{p})=\partial_{M}(f(V^{p}))-(-1)^{|f|}f(V^{p-1})u

and the action of
U
is given by

(U\cdot f)(V^{p})=(U\otimes f)(\psi_{R,A}(V^{p}))=(U\otimes f)(V\otimes V^{p-1})=f(V^{p-1}).

It is now easy to check that the complex
C^{-}_{A}(M)=\operatorname{Hom}_{A}(EA,M)

can be described as the product totalization of the double complex
given in the proposition. ∎

For later reference we record the following facts from the above proof.
The isomorphism
\operatorname{Tot}^{\oplus}(D^{+},\partial^{\prime},\partial^{\prime\prime})\cong C^{+}_{A}(M)
is given by sending an element
m\in M_{q-3p}=D^{+}_{p,q}
to

(5.3)

(-1)^{p(q-p)+p(p+1)/2}m\overbrace{[u|u|\cdots|u]}^{p}\in B(M,A,R)_{p,q}

and the isomorphism
C^{-}_{A}(M)\cong\operatorname{Tot}^{\Pi}(D^{-},\partial^{\prime},\partial^{\prime\prime})
is given by sending an element
f\in\operatorname{Hom}_{A}(EA,M)_{n}
to

(5.4)

((-1)^{p(p+1)/2}f(\overbrace{[u|u|\cdots|u]}^{p}))_{p\geq 0}\in\prod_{p\geq 0}M_{n+4p}=\prod_{p\geq 0}D^{-}_{-p,n+p}.

It should be noted that there are other choices of signs possible. We
have chosen the signs that ensure that the action of
U
corresponds to
the identity map.
A part of the double complex
(D^{+}_{*,*},\partial^{\prime},\partial^{\prime\prime})
corresponding
to a right
A
-module
M
is shown below

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\vdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\vdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\vdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{-1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{-4}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{-2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{-5}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{-3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{-6}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{-1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{-4}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{-7}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots.}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\vdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\vdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\vdots}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial_{M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial_{M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial_{M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial_{M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial_{M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial_{M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial_{M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial_{M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial_{M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}

The dotted arrows represent the action of
U
. An element
x
of

C^{+}_{A}(M)_{n}
can be represented by a sequence
(m_{n-4p})_{p\geq 0}
,
where almost all terms vanish. This element
x
is a cycle if and only if

\partial_{M}m_{n-4p}=(-1)^{n}m_{n-4(p-1)}u
for each
p\geq 0
. Each column
is a shifted copy of the original complex
M
. The double complex

(D^{-}_{*,*},\partial^{\prime},\partial^{\prime\prime})
is obtained by shifting the whole diagram from
the right half plane over to the left half plane. A more concise representation
of this double complex is given in the following diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M[-9[}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M[-6]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M[-3]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pm u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mp u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pm u}

Note that in this case the internal differential in
M[-3n]
,
n\geq 0
,
is not twisted by a sign, and that the
\pm u
notation means that
the sign depends on the internal degree.

With these concrete models in hand it is easy to calculate the following.

Corollary 5.9
.

We have
H^{+}_{A}(A)\cong R
,
H^{-}_{A}(A)\cong R[3]
, with
necessarily trivial
R[U]
-module structure, and

H^{+}_{A}(R\oplus R[2])\cong R[W]
with
|W|=2
,
H^{-}_{A}(R\oplus R[2])=R[Z][2]

with
|Z|=-2
. The
R[U]
-module structures are determined by
U\cdot W^{p}=W^{p-2}
and
U\cdot Z^{p}=Z^{p+2}
.

Remark 5.10
.

The notation
R[W]
and
R[Z][2]
in the above result is only used to effectively express the underlying graded modules and the action
of
R[U]
, it should not be interpreted to mean that
H^{\pm}_{A}(R\oplus R[2])

carry any (co)algebra structure.

5.3.
Tate Homology

We will now proceed to the construction of
C^{\infty}_{A}(M)
.

Definition 5.11
.

For an augmented
DG
algebra
A
define the dualizing object by

D_{A}\coloneqq C^{-}_{A}(A)=\operatorname{Hom}_{A}(EA,A).

The dualizing object is a (left-left)
A-C^{-}_{A}(R)
-bimodule. The left

C^{-}_{A}(R)
-module structure is the one already given in (
5.2
),
while the left
A
-module structure is obtained from the second factor
in
D_{A}=\operatorname{Hom}_{A}(EA,A)
, that is,

(a\cdot f)(x):=af(x)
for all
a\in A
,
f\in\operatorname{Hom}_{A}(EA,A)
and
x\in EA
. This justifies the following definition.

Definition 5.12
.

For a right
A
-module
M
define the twisted
positive
A
-chains of
M
to be the
DG
module

C^{+,tw}_{A}(M)\coloneqq B(M,A,D_{A}).

The homology of this complex is denoted by
H^{+,tw}_{A}(M)
and is called
the positive twisted
A
-homology of
M
.

The complex
C^{+,tw}_{A}(M)
carries a left
C^{-}_{A}(R)
-module structure
induced from the left
C^{-}_{A}(R)
-module structure of
D_{A}
.

Definition 5.13
.

The norm map
N_{M}\colon C^{+,tw}_{A}(M)\rightarrow C^{-}_{A}(M)
is
for each right
A
-module
M
defined to be the composite

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B(M,A,D_{A})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M\otimes_{A}D_{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Hom}_{A}(EA,M),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\epsilon}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi}

where
\epsilon
is the augmentation and
\xi
is the adjoint of

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M\otimes_{A}(D_{A}\otimes EA)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M\otimes_{A}A=M.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1\otimes\operatorname{ev}}

Explicitly, for
m[a_{1}|\cdots|a_{p}]v\in B(M,A,D_{A})
and
x\in EA
,

(5.5)

N_{M}(m[a_{1}|\cdots|a_{p}]v)(x)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}m\cdot v(x)&\mbox{ for }p=0\\
0&\mbox{ for }p>0.\end{array}\right.

Lemma 5.14
.

The norm map
N_{M}\colon C^{+,tw}_{A}(M)\rightarrow C^{-}_{A}(M)
is

C^{-}_{A}(R)
-linear and natural in
M
.

Proof.

The naturality in
M
is clear from the definition.
To verify the
C^{-}_{A}(R)
-linearity let
f\in C^{-}_{A}(R)
and

w=m[a_{1}|\cdots|a_{p}]u\in C^{+,tw}_{A}(M)=B(M,A,D_{A})
. Then

f\cdot w=(-1)^{\eta}m[a_{1}|\cdots|a_{p}](f\cdot w)
for

\eta=p+|m|+|a_{1}|+\cdots+|a_{p}|
and therefore

N_{M}(f\cdot w)=0=f\cdot N_{M}(w)
provided
p>0
. We may therefore
assume that
p=0
such that
w=m[]u
. Let
v=N_{M}(w)
and note that

|v|=|m|+|u|
. Let
x\in EA
and write
\psi_{R,A}(x)=\sum_{i}y_{i}\otimes z_{i}\in BA\otimes EA
where
\psi_{R,A}
is the map of Lemma
5.3
. Then by definition
(f\cdot v)(x)=(f\otimes v)(\psi_{R,A}(x))
,
which equals

(5.6)

\sum_{i}(-1)^{|v||y_{i}|}f(y_{i})v(z_{i})=\sum_{i}(-1)^{|m||y_{i}|+|u||y_{i}|}m\cdot f(y_{i})u(z_{i}).

On the other hand,
N_{M}(f\cdot m[]u)=(-1)^{|f||m|}N_{M}(m[](f\cdot u))
, and
by (
5.5
) this coincides with

(-1)^{|f||m|}\sum_{i}m\cdot(f\otimes u)(y_{i}\otimes z_{i})=\sum_{i}(-1)^{|f||m|+|u||y_{i}|}m\cdot f(y_{i})u(z_{i}).

The final expression is equal to the final expression in (
5.6
) because
f\colon BA\rightarrow R
so that
f(y_{i})
can only be nonzero if

|f|=-|y_{i}|
. Hence,
f\cdot N_{M}(w)=N_{M}(f\cdot w)
and the proof is complete.
∎

The Tate complex

C^{\infty}_{A}(M)
will be defined to be the mapping cone complex of the norm map.
At a later stage we will also have to consider the cone of a map
of nonzero degree, so we fix our conventions in the following definition.

Definition 5.15
.

Let
B
be a
DG
algebra and suppose that

f\colon M\rightarrow N
is a degree
d
chain map of left
B
-modules. This means that

(a)

f(M_{n})\subset N_{n+d}
for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
,

(b)

f(bm)=(-1)^{|b|d}bf(m)
for all
b\in B
and
m\in M
and

(c)

f\circ\partial_{M}=(-1)^{d}\partial_{N}\circ f
.

The left
B
-module
\operatorname{Cone}(f)
is defined by
\operatorname{Cone}(f)_{k}=N_{k}\oplus M_{k-d-1}
for all
k\in\mathbb{Z}
, with differential

\partial_{\operatorname{Cone}(f)}\cdot\left(\begin{array}[]{c}n\\
m\end{array}\right)=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\partial_{N}&(-1)^{d}f\\
0&(-1)^{d+1}\partial_{M}\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{c}n\\
m\end{array}\right)=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\partial_{N}n+(-1)^{d}fm\\
(-1)^{d+1}\partial_{M}m\end{array}\right)

and
B
-module structure
b\cdot(n,m)=(bn,(-1)^{|b|(d+1)}bm)
.

Remark 5.16
.

If
B=R
is concentrated in degree
0
and
f
has degree

0
, then this definition agrees with
[
Mac95
, p. 46]
. The above
definition is what we get when we regard
f
as a degree
0
map

f\colon M[d]\rightarrow N
and take into account the sign twist in the
B
-action
upon shifting
M
.

The cone complex sits in a natural short exact sequence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Cone}(f)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M[d+1]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}

of
B
-modules, whose connecting homomorphism
\delta\colon H(M)_{n}\rightarrow H_{n+d}(N)

recovers
H(f)
up to a sign. In particular, there is a natural exact
triangle in homology.

Definition 5.17
.

Let
M
be a right
A
-module. Then the Tate complex
of
M
is defined to be the
C^{-}_{A}(R)
-module

C^{\infty}_{A}(M)\coloneqq\operatorname{Cone}(N_{M}\colon C^{+,tw}_{A}(M)\rightarrow C^{-}_{A}(M)).

The homology of this complex is denoted by
H^{\infty}_{A}(M)
and is called
the Tate homology of
M
.

Given a map
g\colon M\rightarrow M^{\prime}
of a right
A
-modules, there are induced maps

g^{+,tw}\colon C^{+,tw}_{A}(M)\rightarrow C^{+,tw}_{A}(M^{\prime})\;\;\mbox{ and }\;\;g^{\infty}\colon C^{\infty}_{A}(M)\rightarrow C^{\infty}_{A}(M^{\prime})

of
C^{-}_{A}(R)
-modules. The key properties of the functors
C^{+,tw}_{A}
and
C^{\infty}_{A}
are summarized in the following theorem.

Theorem 5.18
.

Let
A
be a degreewise
R
-free augmented DG algebra.
Then the assignments

(M\mapsto C^{\bullet}_{A}(M))\;\mbox{ and }\;(g\colon M\rightarrow M^{\prime})\mapsto(g^{\bullet}\colon C^{\bullet}_{A}(M)\rightarrow C^{\bullet}_{A}(M^{\prime}))

for
\bullet\in\{(+,tw),\infty\}
, define functors
C^{+,tw}_{A}
,
C^{\infty}_{A}

from the category of right
A
-modules to the category of left

C^{-}_{A}(R)
-modules. They are both exact and preserve quasi-isomorphisms.
Moreover, there is a natural exact triangle of
H^{-}_{A}(R)
-modules

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{+,tw}_{A}(M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{-}_{A}(M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{\infty}_{A}(M).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(N_{M})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces[-1]}

Suppose
f\colon A\rightarrow B
is a quasi-isomorphism of augmented DG algebras.
For a given right
B
-module
M
there is no induced map between

C^{\bullet}_{A}(f^{-1}M)
and
C^{\bullet}_{B}(M)
for
\bullet\in\{(+,tw),\infty\}
.
Rather one has to introduce an intermediate object to compare them.

Definition 5.19
.

Let
f\colon A\rightarrow B
be a homomorphism of augmented
DG algebras. Define the relative dualizing object to be the
(left-left)
C^{-}_{A}(R)-B
-bimodule

D_{f}\coloneqq C^{-}_{A}(f^{-1}B)=\operatorname{Hom}_{A}(EA,f^{-1}B),

where
f^{-1}B
is regarded as a (right-left)
A-B
-bimodule.

Given a right
B
-module
M
define the relative positive twisted complex to be

C^{+,tw}_{f}(M)\coloneqq B(M,B,D_{f})
equipped with the left
C^{-}_{A}(R)
-module structure induced from
D_{f}
.

The relative positive twisted complex is a functor from the
category of right
B
-modules to the category of left
C^{-}_{A}(R)
-modules.
Given a map
g\colon M\rightarrow N
of right
B
-modules the induced map
is given by
B(g,1,1)\colon B(M,B,D_{f})\rightarrow B(N,B,D_{f})
.

Lemma 5.20
.

In the situation of the above definition, if
A
and
B

are degreewise free, the relative functor
C^{+,tw}_{f}
preserves
quasi-isomorphisms and short exact sequences.

Proof.

The assumptions that
A
and
B
are degreewise free along
with the fact that
R
is PID ensure that the relative dualizing object

D_{f}
is degreewise flat. This is verified as in
[
Eis19
, Lemma A.10]
.
It then follows from the invariance theorem
[
Eis19
, Theorem A.1]

that
C^{+,tw}_{f}=B(-,B,D_{f})
is an exact functor preserving quasi-isomorphisms.
∎

Proposition 5.21
.

Let
f\colon A\rightarrow B
be a homomorphism of augmented DG algebras
and let
M
be a right
B
-module. There is a relative norm map

N_{M}^{f}\colon C^{+,tw}_{f}(M)\rightarrow C^{-}_{A}(f^{-1}M)
fitting into the following
commutative diagram, natural in
M
,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{+,tw}_{B}(M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{+,tw}_{f}(M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{+,tw}_{A}(f^{-1}M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{-}_{B}(M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{-}_{A}(f^{-1}M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{-}_{A}(f^{-1}M).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta_{M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{M}^{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N^{f}_{M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N^{A}_{M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}

All of the maps in the diagram are
C^{-}_{A}(R)
-linear,
C^{-}_{B}(R)
-linear
or linear along
f^{*}:C^{-}_{B}(R)\rightarrow C^{-}_{A}(R)
, as appropriate.

Moreover, if
f
is a quasi-isomorphism and
A
and
B
are degreewise

R
-free, then all the horizontal maps in the diagram are quasi-isomorphisms.

Proof.

Define
g\colon D_{B}\rightarrow D_{f}
and
h\colon D_{A}\rightarrow D_{f}

to be the maps

g=f^{*}\colon C^{-}_{B}(B)\rightarrow C^{-}_{A}(f^{-1}B)\;\;\mbox{ and }\;\;h=f^{-}\colon C^{-}_{A}(A)\rightarrow C^{-}_{A}(f^{-1}B).

Note that
D_{A}
is a
C^{-}_{A}(R)-A
-bimodule,
D_{f}
is a
C^{-}_{A}(R)-B
-bimodule
and
D_{B}
is a
C^{-}_{B}(R)-B
-bimodule. Moreover, observe that

g
is a homomorphism along
(f^{*},1)\colon(C^{-}_{B}(R),B)\rightarrow(C^{-}_{A}(R),B)
and
that
h
is a homomorphism along
(1,f)\colon(C^{-}_{A}(R),A)\rightarrow(C^{-}_{A}(R),B)
.
We may therefore define the upper horizontal maps in the diagram by

\displaystyle\beta_{M}\coloneqq B(1,1,g)

\displaystyle\colon B(M,B,D_{B})\rightarrow B(M,B,D_{f})

\displaystyle\alpha_{M}\coloneqq B(1,f,h)

\displaystyle\colon B(f^{-1}M,A,D_{A})\rightarrow B(M,B,D_{f}).

It is clear by construction that these maps are natural in
M
. Furthermore, by the above observations
\beta_{M}

is linear along
f^{*}\colon C^{-}_{B}(R)\rightarrow C^{-}_{A}(R)
and
\alpha_{M}
is

C^{-}_{A}(R)
-linear.

We define the relative norm map
N^{f}_{M}\colon B(M,B,D_{f})\rightarrow C^{-}_{A}(f^{-1}M)
to be the composition of the augmentation

B(M,B,D_{f})\rightarrow M\otimes_{B}D_{f}
and the map
\kappa\colon M\otimes_{B}D_{f}\rightarrow C^{-}_{A}(M)
which is given as the adjoint of

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M\otimes_{B}(\operatorname{Hom}_{A}(EA,f^{-1}B)\otimes EA)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M\otimes_{B}f^{-1}B\cong f^{-1}M.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1\otimes\operatorname{ev}}

Then
N^{B}_{M}
is
C^{-}_{B}(R)
-linear while
N_{M}^{f}
and
N_{M}^{A}
are

C^{-}_{A}(R)
-linear. Consider the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B(M,B,D_{B})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B(M,B,D_{f})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B(f^{-1}M,A,D_{A})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M\otimes_{B}D_{B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M\otimes_{B}D_{f}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{-1}M\otimes_{A}D_{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Hom}_{B}(EB,B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Hom}_{A}(EA,f^{-1}M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Hom}_{A}(EA,f^{-1}M),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\epsilon}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B(1,1,g)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\epsilon}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B(1,f,h)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\epsilon}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1\otimes_{B}g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1\otimes_{f}h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Ef^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}

where
\epsilon
denotes the augmentations and
\xi
are the natural
maps in the definition of the norm map (see
5.13
). In particular, the outer compositions
\xi\circ\epsilon
are the respective
norm maps and by definition
\kappa\circ\epsilon=N^{f}_{M}
. We wish
to verify that the diagram commutes. The two upper squares
commute by the naturality of the augmentation in the bar construction.
To verify that the lower squares commute, consider the diagram obtained
by passing to the adjoints of the vertical maps
\xi
,
\kappa
and
\xi
in the bottom half of the diagram. The resulting diagram is then seen to commute using the fact that each of the maps
\xi
,
\kappa
and
\xi
was defined as the adjoint of
1\otimes\operatorname{ev}
for a suitable evaluation.
By omitting the middle row of the above diagram, we obtain the commutative diagram in the statement of the proposition.

Finally, we need to verify that all the horizontal maps in the diagram
are quasi-isomorphisms provided that
f\colon A\rightarrow B
is a quasi-isomorphism of
degreewise
R
-free algebras. We already know that this is true for

f^{*}\colon C^{-}_{B}(M)\rightarrow C^{-}_{A}(f^{-1}M)
by Proposition
5.5
.
For the upper horizontal maps, note first that

g=f^{*}
and
h=f^{-}
are quasi-isomorphisms by the same result. Then, since
D_{A}
,
D_{B}
and
D_{f}
are degreewise flat, one concludes by

[
Eis19
, Theorem A.1]
that
\beta_{M}=B(1,1,g)
and
\alpha_{M}=B(1,f,g)
are quasi-isomorphisms as well. This completes the proof.
∎

From this result one quickly obtains the required invariance result.
Nevertheless, we will need the more refined statement of the above proposition in the next section.

Corollary 5.22
.

Let
f\colon A\rightarrow B
be a quasi-isomorphism of degreewise

R
-free augmented DG algebras. Then for every right
B
-module

M
there are natural isomorphisms of
H^{-}_{A}(R)\cong H^{-}_{B}(R)
modules

H^{+,tw}_{A}(f^{-1}M)\cong H^{+,tw}_{B}(M)\;\mbox{ and }\;H^{\infty}_{A}(f^{-1}M)\cong H^{\infty}_{B}(M).

Moreover, these isomorphisms are compatible with the exact triangles
of Theorem
5.18
.

A DG algebra
A
is said to satisfy Poincaré duality of degree
d\in\mathbb{Z}

provided there is a weak equivalence
A\simeq\operatorname{Hom}(A,R[d])
of

A-A
-bimodules, where the right and left actions on the target are
induced by the left action on
A
and the trivial left
action on
R[d]
, respectively. Here, weak equivalence means that
there exist a finite number
A=X_{0},X_{1},\cdots,X_{r}=\operatorname{Hom}(A,R[d])

of
A
-bimodules and for each
0\leq i<r
there is a quasi-isomorphism

X_{i}\rightarrow X_{i+1}
or
X_{i+1}\rightarrow X_{i}
of
A
-bimodules.
According to
[
Eis19
, Theorem A.19]
there is under this hypothesis,
an isomorphism
H^{+,tw}_{A}(M)\cong H^{+}_{A}(M)[n]
of
H^{-}_{A}(R)
-modules.
We will give a different proof of this result for
A=\Lambda_{R}[u]
with

|u|=3
. This case is sufficient for the application to equivariant instanton Floer homology when
\frac{1}{2}\in R
.

We will now consider these constructions for
A=\Lambda_{R}[u]
with
|u|=3

as we did for
C^{\pm}_{A}
. The Tate complex
C^{\infty}_{A}(M)
, even in this simple case, is big and difficult to handle explicitly. To remedy this we will
construct a much simpler and very explicit complex that also computes
the Tate homology
H^{\infty}_{A}(M)
. Indeed, we will show that if
we take one of the double complexes
(D^{\pm}_{*,*},\partial^{\prime},\partial^{\prime\prime})
of Proposition

5.8
and extend them in the natural way to the
whole plane, then a suitable totalization of this complex will do the
trick.

Lemma 5.23
.

Let
A=\Lambda_{R}[u]
with
|u|=3
. Then there is an isomorphism of left

A
-modules
A\otimes R[\alpha]\cong D_{A}
where
|\alpha|=-4
. Under this
isomorphism the differential on
A\otimes R[\alpha]
is given by

\partial(1\otimes\alpha^{p})=-u\otimes\alpha^{p+1}
and
\partial(u\otimes\alpha^{p})=0

for
p\geq 0
, and the action of
R[U]=C^{-}_{A}(R)
is given by

U\cdot(a\otimes\alpha^{p})=a\otimes\alpha^{p+1}
for
a\in A
and

p\geq 0
.

Furthermore, the map
\rho\colon R\rightarrow A\otimes R[\alpha]
of degree
3

given by
1\mapsto u\otimes\alpha^{0}
is an
A
-linear quasi-isomorphism.

Proof.

Using the explicit model of Theorem
5.8

we see that

(D_{A})_{n}=C^{-}_{A}(A)_{n}\cong\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}A_{0}&\mbox{ for }n=4m,m\leq 0\\
A_{3}&\mbox{for }n=4m+3,m\leq 0\\
0&\mbox{ otherwise}\end{array}\right.

and a quick check shows that these identifications are compatible with
the left
A
-module structure. The required isomorphism is therefore obtained
by letting
1\otimes\alpha^{p}
and
u\otimes\alpha^{p}
correspond to the
generators
1\in A_{0}\cong C^{-}_{A}(A)_{-4p}
and
u\in A_{3}=C^{-}_{A}(A)_{-4p+3}
, respectively, for
p\geq 0
. The same result shows that the differential
and
R[U]
-action are determined by the formulas
\partial(u\otimes\alpha^{p})=0
,
\partial(1\otimes\alpha^{p})=-u\otimes\alpha^{p+1}
and
U\cdot(a\otimes\alpha^{p})=a\otimes\alpha^{p+1}
for
a\in A
and
p\geq 0
.

From this description we find that
\partial\colon(D_{A})_{-4p}\rightarrow(D_{A})_{-4(p+1)-3}

is an isomorphism for each
p\geq 0
. Therefore, the map

\rho\colon R\rightarrow A\otimes R[\alpha]
of degree
3
given by
1\mapsto u\otimes\alpha^{0}
is an
A
-linear quasi-isomorphism.
∎

In the following result we establish the isomorphism
H^{+,tw}_{A}(M)\cong H^{+}_{A}(M)[3]
of
R[U]
-modules for each right
A
-module
M
. It also
contains the key ingredients needed to establish the simplified model for
C^{\infty}_{A}(M)
.

Lemma 5.24
.

Let
A=\Lambda_{R}[u]
with
|u|=3
and let
M
be a right

A
-module. Then there is a quasi-isomorphism
f_{M}\colon C^{+}_{A}(M)\rightarrow C^{+,tw}_{A}(M)
of degree
3
and a chain homotopy
s_{M}\colon C^{+}_{A}(M)\rightarrow C^{+,tw}_{A}(M)
of degree
0
such that

f_{M}\circ U-U\circ f_{M}=\partial\circ s_{M}-s_{M}\circ\partial.

In particular,
H(f_{M})\colon H^{+}_{A}(M)\rightarrow H^{+,tw}_{A}(M)
is a degree
3

isomorphism of
R[U]
-modules. Furthermore,
f_{M}
and
s_{M}
are natural in the module
M
, and in terms
of the explicit models
C^{+}_{A}(M)\cong\operatorname{Tot}^{\oplus}(D^{+}_{*,*},\partial^{\prime},\partial^{\prime\prime})

and
C^{-}_{A}(M)\cong\operatorname{Tot}^{\Pi}(D^{-}_{*,*},\partial^{\prime},\partial^{\prime\prime})
of
Proposition
5.8
the compositions

N_{M}\circ f_{M}
and
N_{M}\circ s_{M}
are given in degree
n
by

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigoplus_{p\geq 0}M_{n-4p}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{n+3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{q\geq 0}M_{n+3+4q}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigoplus_{p\geq 0}M_{n-4p}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{q\geq 0}M_{n+4q},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(-1)^{n}u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}

respectively.

Proof.

Recall that
C^{+}_{A}(M)=B(M,A,R)
and
C^{+,tw}_{A}(M)=B(M,A,D_{A})
.
It is convenient to introduce the notation

\displaystyle m\otimes V^{p}\coloneqq

\displaystyle(-1)^{p(p+1)/2}m[u|u|\cdots|u]\in B(M,A,R)

\displaystyle m\otimes V^{p}\otimes\psi\coloneqq

\displaystyle(-1)^{p(p+1)/2}m[u|u|\cdots|u]\psi\in B(M,A,D_{A})

for
m\in M
and
\psi\in D_{A}
, where
u
is repeated
p
times in both formulas. In terms of this notation the differentials and actions of
U\in R[U]
of
B(M,A,R)
and
B(M,A,D_{A})
are given by the formulas

\displaystyle U(m\otimes V^{p})=

\displaystyle\;(-1)^{|m|}m\otimes V^{p-1}

\displaystyle U(m\otimes V^{p}\otimes\psi)=

\displaystyle\;m\otimes V^{p}\otimes(U\psi)

\displaystyle\partial(m\otimes V^{p})=

\displaystyle\;(-1)^{p}(\partial_{M}m+mu\otimes V^{p-1})

\displaystyle\partial(m\otimes V^{p}\otimes\psi)=

\displaystyle\;(-1)^{p}(\partial_{M}m\otimes V^{p}+mu\otimes V^{p-1})\otimes\psi

\displaystyle\;+m\otimes(V^{p-1}\otimes u\psi+(-1)^{|m|}V^{p}\otimes\partial_{D_{A}}\psi).

By the previous lemma there is an isomorphism
D_{A}\cong A\otimes R[\alpha]

with
|\alpha|=-4
. To simplify the notation write

a\alpha^{p}\coloneqq a\otimes\alpha^{p}
for
a\in A
. There is also an

A
-linear quasi-isomorphism
\rho:R\rightarrow D_{A}
of degree
3
given by
\rho(1)=u\alpha^{0}
. This map induces a quasi-isomorphism

f_{M}\coloneqq B(1,1,\rho)\colon B(M,A,R)\rightarrow B(M,A,D_{A})

of degree
3
, which in terms of the above notation
is given by
f_{M}(m\otimes V^{p})=(-1)^{|m|}m\otimes V^{p}\otimes u\alpha^{0}

(the sign is forced upon us by the fact that we regard
\rho
as a map
of degree
3
). Using the above formulas we find that

(5.7)

(f_{M}\circ U-U\circ f_{M})(m\otimes V^{p})=m\otimes V^{p-1}\otimes u\alpha^{0}-(-1)^{|m|}m\otimes V^{p}\otimes u\alpha^{1}.

Define
s_{M}\colon B(M,A,R)\rightarrow B(M,A,D_{A})
of degree
0
by
s_{M}(m\otimes V^{p})=m\otimes V^{p}\otimes\alpha^{0}
. A straightforward calculation using the above formulas and the formula for the differential in
D_{A}\cong A\otimes R[\alpha]
given in the previous lemma shows that

\displaystyle\partial s_{M}(m\otimes V^{p})=

\displaystyle\;(-1)^{p}(\partial_{M}m\otimes V^{p}\otimes\alpha^{0}+mu\otimes V^{p-1}\otimes\alpha^{0})

\displaystyle\;+m\otimes V^{p-1}\otimes u\alpha^{0}-(-1)^{|m|}m\otimes V^{p}\otimes u\alpha^{1}

\displaystyle s_{M}\partial(m\otimes V^{p})=

\displaystyle(-1)^{p}(\partial_{M}m\otimes V^{p}\otimes\alpha^{0}+mu\otimes V^{p-1}\otimes\alpha^{0}).

Combining this with (
5.7
) we obtain
f_{M}\circ U-U\circ f_{M}=\partial s_{M}-s_{M}\partial
as required.

Finally, we need to consider the compositions of
f_{M}
and
s_{M}
with the
norm map
N_{M}\colon C^{+,tw}_{A}(M)\rightarrow C^{-}_{A}(M)
in terms of the models
given in Proposition
5.8
. By (
5.3
)

m\in M_{q-3p}=D^{+}_{p,q}
corresponds to
(-1)^{|m|p}m\otimes V^{p}
,
which is mapped by
f_{M}
to
(-1)^{|m|(p+1)}m\otimes V^{p}\otimes u\alpha^{0}
.
The norm map kills off all such elements with
p>0
and sends

m\otimes V^{0}\otimes u\alpha^{0}\mapsto mu\cdot\alpha^{0}
. This is the
functional that sends
V^{0}
to
mu\in M_{q+3}
and vanishes on
V^{q}
for

q>0
. By (
5.4
) the identification
C^{-}_{A}(M)_{n}\cong\prod_{q\geq 0}M_{n+4q}
is given by
\psi\mapsto(\psi(V^{q}))_{q\geq 0}
. We conclude that
N_{M}\circ f_{M}
is given by the composition in the statement of the lemma. The
verification for
N_{M}\circ s_{M}
is analogous.
∎

We need two simple but slightly technical results before we can reach
our goal.

Lemma 5.25
.

Let
X
and
Y
be DG
R[U]
-modules and suppose that

f\colon X\rightarrow Y
is a degree
d
map of
R[U]
-modules up to homotopy; that
is,
f
is a chain map of degree
d
and there is a chain homotopy

s\colon X\rightarrow Y
such that
fU-Uf=\partial_{Y}s+(-1)^{d}s\partial_{X}
. Let
\operatorname{Cone}(f)
be given
as in Definition
5.15
, except that we redefine the

R[U]
-module structure by setting

U\cdot\left(\begin{array}[]{c}y\\
x\end{array}\right)=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}U&(-1)^{|s|}s\\
0&U\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{c}y\\
x\end{array}\right)=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}Uy-(-1)^{d}sx\\
Ux\end{array}\right)

for all
(y,x)\in Y_{n}\oplus X_{n-d-1}=\operatorname{Cone}(f)_{n}
.
Then
\operatorname{Cone}(f)
is a DG
R[U]
-module and the maps in the natural
short exact sequence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Cone}(f)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X[d+1]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}

are (strict) maps of
R[U]
-modules.

Proof.

It suffices to check that
\partial_{\operatorname{Cone}(f)}U=U\partial_{\operatorname{Cone}(f)}
. This follows easily from the relations
\partial_{X}U=U\partial_{X}
,
\partial_{Y}U=U\partial_{Y}

and
fU-Uf=\partial_{Y}s+(-1)^{d}s\partial_{X}
. Next, the maps in the natural
exact sequence are given degreewise by the inclusion

Y_{n}\rightarrow Y_{n}\oplus X_{n-d-1}
and the projection
Y_{n}\oplus X_{n-d-1}\rightarrow X_{n-d-1}
and are seen to commute with the action of
U
.
∎

Lemma 5.26
.

Let
f\colon X\rightarrow Y
be a degree
3
map of DG
R[U]
-modules up
to homotopy, and let
g\colon Y\rightarrow Z
be a degree
0

map of DG
R[U]
-modules. Then
g\circ f:X\rightarrow Z
is a degree
3
map of

R[U]
-modules up to homotopy and there is a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Cone}(g\circ f)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X[4]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Cone}(g)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y[1]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Cone}(f,1)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f[1]}

with short exact rows. If
\operatorname{Cone}(g\circ f)
is given the DG
R[U]
-module
structure of the previous lemma, then the maps
\operatorname{Cone}(f,1)
and
f[1]
are maps of DG
R[U]
-modules up to homotopy and all the other maps in the diagram
are strict maps of DG
R[U]
-modules.

Proof.

By assumption there is a chain homotopy
s\colon X\rightarrow Y
of
degree
0
such that
fU-Uf=\partial s-s\partial
. Then for
h\coloneqq g\circ f

and
t\coloneqq g\circ s
we have
hU-Uh=\partial t-t\partial
as well. This shows that
h
is a map of
R[U]
-modules up to homotopy. By functoriality of the cone
construction we obtain a commutative diagram of DG modules with
short exact rows

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Cone}(g\circ f)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X[4]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Cone}(g)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y[1]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Cone}(f,1)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f[1]}

Here,
\operatorname{Cone}(f,1)\colon\operatorname{Cone}(g\circ f)_{n}=Z_{n}\oplus X_{n-4}\rightarrow Z_{n}\oplus Y_{n-1}=\operatorname{Cone}(g)_{n}
is given by
(z,x)\mapsto(z,-fx)
, and

f[1]\colon X[4]_{n}=X_{n-4}\rightarrow Y_{n-1}=Y[1]_{n}
is given by
x\mapsto-fx
.
Equip
\operatorname{Cone}(g\circ f)
with the adjusted
R[U]
-module structure
given in the previous lemma. Then all the horizontal maps in the
diagram are strict maps of
R[U]
-modules. The right hand vertical map
is a map of
R[U]
-modules up to homotopy, so the only thing
we have to verify is that
\operatorname{Cone}(f,1)
is a map of
R[U]
-modules
up to homotopy.

We claim that
v\colon\operatorname{Cone}(h)\rightarrow\operatorname{Cone}(g)
given by
v(z,x)=(0,sx)

is the required homotopy. Indeed, using matrix notation for the various
maps,

\displaystyle C(f,1)U-UC(f,1)

\displaystyle=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}1&0\\
0&-f\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}U&t\\
0&U\end{array}\right)-\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}U&0\\
0&U\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}1&0\\
0&-f\end{array}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}0&t\\
0&Uf-fU\end{array}\right)

\displaystyle\partial_{\operatorname{Cone}(g)}v+v\partial_{\operatorname{Cone}(h)}

\displaystyle=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\partial_{Z}&g\\
0&-\partial_{Y}\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}0&0\\
0&s\end{array}\right)+\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}0&0\\
0&s\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\partial_{Z}&-h\\
0&\partial_{X}\end{array}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}0&g\circ s\\
0&s\partial_{X}-\partial_{Y}s\end{array}\right),

where
C(f,1)
is shorthand for
\operatorname{Cone}(f,1)
. Therefore,
as
t=g\circ s
and
\partial_{Y}s-s\partial_{X}s=fU-Uf
, we deduce that

\operatorname{Cone}(f,1)
is
R[U]
-linear up to homotopy.
∎

Given a sequence of
R
-modules
\{D_{s}\}_{s\in\mathbb{Z}}
we write

\prod_{s\to-\infty}D_{s}
for the submodule of
\prod_{s}D_{s}
consisting
of the sequences
(x_{s})_{s\in\mathbb{Z}}
for which there exists
s_{0}
such that
x_{s}=0
for all
s\geq s_{0}
. Similarly,
\prod_{s\to\infty}D_{s}
is
the submodule of
(x_{s})_{s\in\mathbb{Z}}
with
x_{s}=0
for all sufficiently
small
s
.

Definition 5.27
.

Given a double complex
E=(D_{*,*},\partial^{\prime},\partial^{\prime\prime})
, define

\operatorname{Tot}^{\Pi,-\infty}(E)
and
\operatorname{Tot}^{\Pi,+\infty}(E)
to be
the complexes that in degree
n
are given by

\prod_{s\to-\infty}D_{s,n-s}\;\mbox{ and }\;\prod_{s\to\infty}D_{s,n-s}

respectively, with differential
\partial=\partial^{\prime}+\partial^{\prime\prime}
.

Theorem 5.28
.

Let
A=\Lambda_{R}[u]
with
|u|=3
and let
M
be a right
DG
A
-module. Define
D^{\infty}_{p,q}=M_{q-3p}
for all
p,q\in\mathbb{Z}
. Set

\displaystyle\partial^{\prime}=(-1)^{p+q+1}u\colon D^{\infty}_{p,q}

\displaystyle=M_{q-3p}\rightarrow M_{q-3p+3}=D^{\infty}_{p-1,q}

\displaystyle\partial^{\prime\prime}=\partial_{M}\colon D^{\infty}_{p,q}

\displaystyle=M_{q-3p}\rightarrow M_{q-3p-1}=D^{\infty}_{p,q-1}

and let
U\colon D^{\infty}_{p,q}=M_{q-3p}\rightarrow M_{q-3p}=D^{\infty}_{p-1,q-3}
be
the identity for all
p,q
. Then there is a quasi-isomorphism, natural in
M
,

\operatorname{Tot}^{\Pi,-\infty}(D_{*,*},\partial^{\prime},\partial^{\prime\prime})\rightarrow C^{\infty}_{A}(M),

which is a homomorphism of
R[U]
-modules up to homotopy.

Proof.

By Lemma
5.24
there is a quasi-isomorphism

f=f_{M}\colon C^{+}_{A}(M)\rightarrow C^{+,tw}_{A}(M)
and a homotopy
s=s_{M}\colon C^{+}_{A}(M)\rightarrow C^{+,tw}_{A}(M)
such that
fU-Uf=\partial s-s\partial
. We apply Lemma
5.26
for

f\colon C^{+}_{A}(M)\rightarrow C^{+,tw}_{A}(M)
and the norm map

N_{M}\colon C^{+,tw}_{A}(M)\rightarrow C^{-}_{A}(M)
to obtain the commutative
diagram with exact rows

(5.8)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{-}_{A}(M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Cone}(N_{M}\circ f)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{+}_{A}(M)[4]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{-}_{A}(M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{\infty}_{A}(M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{+,tw}_{A}(M)[1]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Cone}(f,1)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f[1]}

where we recall that
C^{\infty}_{A}(M)=\operatorname{Cone}(N_{M})
. By the same result

\operatorname{Cone}(f,1)
is a map of
R[U]
-modules up to homotopy. As both the
outer vertical maps are quasi-isomorphisms, it follows by passage to the long exact sequences in homology and the
5
-lemma that
\operatorname{Cone}(f,1)

is a quasi-isomorphism. Thus,
\operatorname{Cone}(f,1)
is the required map.
This quasi-isomorphism is natural in
M
, since both
f
and
N_{M}
are
natural transformations in
M
and the cone construction is functorial.

The remaining task is to establish an isomorphism

\operatorname{Cone}(N_{M}\circ f)\cong\operatorname{Tot}^{\Pi,-\infty}(D^{\infty}_{*,*},\partial^{\prime},\partial^{\prime\prime})
.
Using the identifications of Proposition
5.8
we obtain

\operatorname{Cone}(N_{M}\circ f)_{n}=C^{-}_{A}(M)_{n}\oplus C^{+}_{A}(M)_{n-4}\cong\left(\prod_{q\geq 0}M_{n+4q}\right)\oplus\left(\bigoplus_{p\geq 0}M_{n-4(p+1)}\right)

for each
n\in\mathbb{Z}
. Moreover, by Lemma
5.24

h\coloneqq N_{M}\circ f
and
t\coloneqq N_{M}\circ s
are given on
(x_{p})_{p\geq 0}\in C^{+}_{A}(M)_{n}
by

h((x_{p})_{p})=((-1)^{|x_{0}|}x_{0}u,0,\cdots)
and
t((x_{p})_{p\geq 0})=(x_{0},0,0,\cdots)
. Therefore, using the formulas

\partial_{\operatorname{Cone}(h)}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\partial_{C^{-}_{A}(M)}&-h\\
0&\partial_{C^{+}_{A}(M)}\end{array}\right)\;\mbox{ and }\;U=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}U&t\\
0&U\end{array}\right),

as well as the description of the differentials and the
U
-action in terms
of the models for
C^{\pm}_{A}(M)
, we deduce that

\operatorname{Cone}(h)\cong\operatorname{Tot}^{\Pi,-\infty}(D^{\infty}_{*,*},\partial^{\prime},\partial^{\prime\prime})
.
∎

A part of the double complex
(D^{\infty}_{*,*},\partial^{\prime},\partial^{\prime\prime})
is shown
below.

{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-87.17493pt}{32.55841pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}

This is indeed the natural concatenation of the two double complexes

D^{+}_{*,*}
and
D^{-}_{*,*}
.

Corollary 5.29
.

Let
M
be a right
A=\Lambda_{R}[u]
-module. Then

U\colon H^{\infty}_{A}(M)\rightarrow H^{\infty}_{A}(M)
is an isomorphism.

Proof.

U\colon\operatorname{Tot}^{\Pi,-\infty}(D^{\infty}_{*,*},\partial^{\prime},\partial^{\prime\prime})\rightarrow\operatorname{Tot}^{\Pi,-\infty}(D^{\infty}_{*,*},\partial^{\prime},\partial^{\prime\prime})
is an isomorphism. ∎

As in the cases
C^{\pm}_{A}
we will need calculations for

M=R
,
M=R\oplus R[2]
and
M=\Lambda_{R}[u]
. This is easily achieved with
the help of the above explicit model.

Corollary 5.30
.

We have

H^{\infty}_{A}(A)=0,\;\;H^{\infty}_{A}(R)=R[T,T^{-1}]\;\mbox{ and }\;H^{\infty}_{A}(R\oplus R[2])=R[S,S^{-1}],

where
|T|=-4
,
|S|=-2
and the
R[U]
-action is given by
U\cdot T^{i}=T^{i+1}
and
U\cdot S^{i}=S^{i+2}
for all
i\in\mathbb{Z}
.

We note once again that our notation should not be interpreted to mean that
there is any product structure in
H^{\infty}_{A}(R\oplus R[2])
or
H^{\infty}_{A}(R)
.

5.4.
The Equivariant Instanton Floer Groups

In this section we will give the definition of the various flavors
of equivariant instanton Floer homology. Before we can get to this we
have to recall a few definitions concerning filtered DG modules.
Let
M
be a
DG
module equipped with an increasing filtration

\cdots\subset F_{p-1}M\subset F_{p}M\subset F_{p+1}M\subset\cdots\subset M

by DG submodules. The minimal filtration quotients
F_{p}M/F_{p-1}M

enter many times in the following discussion, so we introduce the
simpler notation
\overline{F}_{p}M\coloneqq F_{p}M/F_{p-1}M
.
The filtration gives rise to a homological spectral sequence with

(5.9)

E^{1}_{p,q}=H_{p+q}(\overline{F}_{p}M)\implies H_{p+q}(M)

(we will elaborate on this in the next section). However, without any additional information, this spectral sequence may fail to converge in any sense to the desired target
H(M)
.

The following properties of a filtration are highly desirable in order to achieve any reasonable convergence properties of the above spectral sequence.
The filtration
\{F_{p}M\}_{p}
of
M
is said to be
[
Boa99
, Definition 2.1]

(i)

exhaustive if
\operatorname{colim}_{p}F_{p}M=\bigcup_{p}F_{p}M=M
,

(ii)

Hausdorff if
\operatorname{lim}_{p}F_{p}M=\bigcap_{p}F_{p}M=0
and

(iii)

complete if
\operatorname{Rlim}_{p}F_{p}M=0
.

Here
\operatorname{Rlim}
is the first (and only nonzero) right derived functor of

\operatorname{lim}
(see
[
Boa99
, Section 1]
for additional details).
It is important to note that these limits are calculated degreewise.
In particular, if the filtration is degreewise bounded below, that is,
for each degree
n
there exists
p_{0}(n)
such that
F_{p}M_{n}=0
for
all
p\leq p_{0}
, then the filtration is automatically complete Hausdorff.
Similarly, if the filtration is degreewise bounded above then the filtration
is automatically exhaustive.

Definition 5.31
.

Let
M
be a DG module
equipped with an increasing filtration
\{F_{p}M\}_{p}
by DG submodules.
Then the full completion of
(M,\{F_{p}M\}_{p})
is defined to be the complex

\hat{M}\coloneqq\operatorname{lim}_{q}\operatorname{colim}_{p}F_{p}M/F_{q}M

equipped with the filtration
F_{p}\hat{M}\coloneqq\operatorname{lim}_{q}F_{p}M/F_{q}M
.

Remark 5.32
.

The standard completion of a filtered module
M
is
typically defined by
\operatorname{lim}_{q}M/F_{q}M
(see for instance
[
Boa99
, 2.7]
) and is naturally filtered by
\{\operatorname{lim}_{q}F_{p}M/F_{q}M\}_{p}
. We have therefore chosen to use the terminology full
completion to distinguish it from this notion. The lemma below demonstrates
that the two notions coincide if the filtration of
M
is exhaustive.

A map
f\colon M\rightarrow M^{\prime}
between filtered DG modules is filtered if

f(F_{p}M)\subset F_{p}M^{\prime}
for all
p\in\mathbb{Z}
. There is therefore
a category of filtered DG modules and filtered maps. A sequence

M^{\prime}\rightarrow M\rightarrow M^{\prime\prime}
of filtered DG modules and filtered maps is short
exact if
F_{p}M^{\prime}\rightarrow F_{p}M\rightarrow F_{p}M^{\prime\prime}
is short exact for each
p\in\mathbb{Z}
.
The relevant properties of the full completion are summarized in the
following lemma.

Lemma 5.33
.

Let
M
be a filtered DG module. Then the following holds true.

(i)

The filtration
F_{p}\hat{M}=\operatorname{lim}_{q<p}F_{p}M/F_{q}M
of
\hat{M}
is exhaustive
and complete Hausdorff.

(ii)

For each
q<p
there is a natural isomorphism
F_{p}M/F_{q}M\rightarrow F_{p}\hat{M}/F_{q}\hat{M}
.

(iii)

If the filtration of
M
is exhaustive, then

\hat{M}=\operatorname{lim}_{q}M/F_{q}M
.

(iv)

If the filtration of
M
is complete Hausdorff, then

\hat{M}=\operatorname{colim}_{p}F_{p}M=\bigcup_{p}F_{p}M
.

The full completion
M\mapsto\hat{M}
defines a functor from the
category of filtered modules to itself, which preserves short exact
sequences.

Proof.

Let
M
be a filtered DG module and set
N=\operatorname{colim}_{p}F_{p}M=\bigcup_{p}F_{p}M
filtered by
F_{p}N=F_{p}M
. Then the full
completion of
M
coincides with the standard completion of
N
, i.e.,

\hat{M}=\operatorname{lim}_{q}N/F_{q}N
. Therefore part (i) and (ii) follow from
the corresponding properties of the standard completion (see
[
Boa99
, Proposition 2.8]
). If the filtration of
M
is exhaustive,
then
\operatorname{colim}_{p}F_{p}M/F_{q}M=M/F_{q}M
for each
q
and part (iii) easily follows. For part (iv)

\hat{M}=\operatorname{lim}_{q}\operatorname{colim}_{p}F_{p}M/F_{q}M=\operatorname{lim}_{q}N/F_{q}N=N=\operatorname{colim}_{p}F_{p}M.

It is clear that
M\mapsto\hat{M}
is functorial with respect to
filtered maps. The exactness of
\hat{M}
follows from the exactness
of sequential colimits and exactness of the standard completion
in the category of
R
-modules (see for instance
[
AM69
, Corollary 10.3]
).
∎

Remark 5.34
.

If
B
is a DG algebra and
M
is a left or right
B
-module equipped
with a filtration by
B
-submodules the definition of the full completion
and the above lemma apply without change. The reason for this
is that quotients, limits and colimits are formed in the category
of graded modules, with the
B
-action and differentials carried along.

Recall from the previous section that for a degreewise
R
-free
augmented DG algebra
A
, four functors
C^{\bullet}_{A}
,
\bullet\in\{+,-,(+,tw),\infty\}
, from the category of right
A
-modules
to the category of left
C^{-}_{A}(R)
-modules were defined. By Theorem

5.6
and Theorem

5.18
these are all exact and preserve quasi-isomorphisms. Therefore, if
M
is a right
A
-module equipped with
a filtration
F_{p}M
by
A
-submodules,
C^{\bullet}_{A}(M)
carries
a natural filtration
F_{p}C^{\bullet}_{A}(M)\coloneqq C^{\bullet}_{A}(F_{p}M)
by

C^{-}_{A}(R)
-submodules. Now, even if the initial filtration of

M
is exhaustive and complete Hausdorff, the resulting filtration
of
C^{\bullet}_{A}(M)
may fail to satisfy these properties. This
motivates the following definition.

Definition 5.35
.

Let
A
be a degreewise
R
-free augmented DG algebra.
For
\bullet\in\{+,-,(+,tw),\infty\}
define a functor

\hat{C}^{\bullet}_{A}
from the category of filtered right
A
-modules
to the category of filtered left
C^{-}_{A}(R)
-modules by

\displaystyle\hat{C}^{\bullet}_{A}(M)\coloneqq

\displaystyle\operatorname{lim}_{q}\operatorname{colim}_{p}C^{\bullet}_{A}(F_{p}M/F_{q}M)\;\;\mbox{ filtered by }\;

\displaystyle F_{p}\hat{C}^{\bullet}_{A}(M)\coloneqq

\displaystyle\operatorname{lim}_{q<p}C^{\bullet}_{A}(F_{p}M/F_{q}M)\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\mbox{ for all}\;p\in\mathbb{Z}.

In other words,
\hat{C}^{\bullet}_{A}
is the composition of the natural
extension of
C^{\bullet}_{A}
to filtered
A
-modules followed by the
full completion functor. We denote the homology of
\hat{C}^{\bullet}_{A}(M)

by
\hat{H}^{\bullet}_{A}(M)
.

The various flavors of equivariant instanton Floer homology are
obtained by applying this construction to the framed Floer
complex.

Definition 5.36
.

Let
A=C^{gm}_{*}(\operatorname{SO}(3))
and let
M=\widetilde{CI}(Y,E)

be the framed Floer complex associated with an
\operatorname{SO}(3)
-bundle
E
over a
closed oriented three-manifold
Y
, equipped with the index filtration.
For
\bullet\in\{+,-,(+,tw),\infty\}
define

CI^{\bullet}(Y,E)\coloneqq\hat{C}^{\bullet}_{A}(M)\;\;\mbox{ and }\;\;I^{\bullet}(Y,E)\coloneqq H(CI^{\bullet}(Y,E)).

The groups
I^{\bullet}(Y,E)
, for
\bullet\in\{+,-,(+,tw),\infty\}
, are called the positive, negative, positive twisted and Tate equivariant
instanton Floer homologies of
(Y,E)
, respectively. These homology groups are modules over
H^{-}_{A}(R)
.

For
A=\Lambda_{R}[u]
and
M=DCI(Y,E)
we similarly define the complexes

DCI^{\bullet}(Y,E)=\hat{C}^{\bullet}_{A}(M).

Remark 5.37
.

For
A=C_{*}(\operatorname{SO}(3);R)
and
M=R
it holds true that

H^{-}_{A}(R)\cong H^{-*}(\operatorname{BSO}(3);R)
(see
[
Eis19
, Lemma A.21]
).

The norm map
N_{M}\colon C^{+,tw}_{A}(M)\rightarrow C^{-}_{A}(M)
is a natural in
M
and
therefore extends to a natural map
\hat{N}_{M}\colon\hat{C}^{+,tw}_{A}(M)\rightarrow\hat{C}^{-}_{A}(M)
for any filtered module
M
. The following simple lemma
ensures that
\hat{C}^{\infty}_{A}(M)
is naturally isomorphic to

\operatorname{Cone}(\hat{N}_{M})
.

Lemma 5.38
.

Let
A
be a degreewise
R
-free augmented DG algebra
and let
M
be a filtered right
A
-module. Then there is a natural
(filtered) isomorphism of
C^{-}_{A}(R)
-modules

\operatorname{Cone}(\hat{N}_{M})\cong\hat{C}^{\infty}_{A}(M).

In particular, we have the following exact triangle of
H^{-}_{A}(R)
-modules.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{H}^{+,tw}_{A}(M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{H}^{-}_{A}(M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{H}^{\infty}_{A}(M)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(\hat{N}_{M})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces[0]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces[-1]}

Proof.

For
q<p
and
n
there is a natural identification

\frac{F_{p}C^{\infty}_{A}(M)}{F_{q}C^{\infty}_{A}(M)}_{n}\cong C^{\infty}_{A}\left(\frac{F_{p}M}{F_{q}M}\right)_{n}=C^{-}_{A}\left(\frac{F_{p}M}{F_{q}M}\right)_{n}\oplus C^{+,tw}_{A}\left(\frac{F_{p}M}{F_{q}M}\right)_{n-1}.

In terms of the right hand side the differential is given by

\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\partial^{-}_{p,q}&N_{p,q}\\
0&-\partial^{+,tw}_{p,q}\end{array}\right),

where
\partial^{-}_{p,q}
and
\partial^{+,tw}_{p,q}
denote the differential
in the first and second summand respectively and
N_{p,q}
denotes the
evident norm map. Applying
\operatorname{lim}_{q}\operatorname{colim}_{p}
to this identification,
taking into account that this is calculated degreewise with differentials
and
A
-module structure carried along, we obtain

\hat{C}^{\infty}_{A}(M)_{n}\cong\hat{C}^{-}_{A}(M)_{n}\oplus\hat{C}^{+,tw}_{A}(M)_{n-1}

in each degree
n
. The differential on the right hand side is
given by

\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\operatorname{lim}_{q}\operatorname{colim}_{p}\partial^{-}_{p,q}&\operatorname{lim}_{q}\operatorname{colim}_{p}N_{p,q}\\
0&\operatorname{lim}_{q}\operatorname{colim}_{p}\partial^{+,tw}_{p,q}\end{array}\right).

Therefore, the right hand side is precisely
\operatorname{Cone}(\hat{N}_{M})
, since
by definition
\hat{N}_{M}=\operatorname{lim}_{q}\operatorname{colim}_{p}N_{p,q}
.
∎

To verify that
DCI^{\bullet}(Y,E)
also calculates
I^{\bullet}(Y,E)
when

\frac{1}{2}\in R
we will establish the necessary invariance results for the functors
\hat{C}^{\bullet}_{A}
. The appropriate tool is the following lemma, which is a simple consequence of the Eilenberg-Moore comparison theorem of
[
Wei94
, Theorem 5.5.11]
. For a filtered map
f\colon M\rightarrow N
between
filtered DG modules we use the notation
\overline{f}_{p}\colon\overline{F}_{p}M\rightarrow\overline{F}_{p}N
for the induced maps between the the minimal
filtration quotients.

Lemma 5.39
.

Let
M
and
N
be DG modules equipped with
exhaustive and complete Hausdorff filtrations. Then if
f\colon M\rightarrow N

is a filtered map such that the induced map

\overline{f}_{p}\colon\overline{F}_{p}M\rightarrow\overline{F}_{p}N
is a quasi-isomorphism for each
p\in\mathbb{Z}
, then
f
is a quasi-isomorphism.

Proposition 5.40
.

Let
A
be a degreewise
R
-free augmented DG algebra.
Suppose
f\colon M\rightarrow N
is a filtered map of filtered right

A
-modules such that

\overline{f}_{p}\colon\overline{F}_{p}M\rightarrow\overline{F}_{p}N
is a quasi-isomorphism for each
p\in\mathbb{Z}
. Then for
\bullet\in\{+,-,(+,tw),\infty\}
the
induced map

\hat{f}^{\bullet}\colon\hat{C}^{\bullet}_{A}(M)\rightarrow\hat{C}^{\bullet}_{A}(N)

is a (filtered) quasi-isomorphism of
C^{-}_{A}(R)
-modules. Furthermore, upon
passage to homology, these isomorphisms are compatible with the
exact triangle of Lemma
5.38
.

Proof.

Let
\bullet\in\{+,-,(+,tw),\infty\}
and note that
the functors
C^{\bullet}_{A}
and
\hat{C}^{\bullet}_{A}
are covariant
in
M
. For simplicity of notation let
g=\hat{f}^{\bullet}\colon\hat{C}^{\bullet}_{A}(M)\rightarrow\hat{C}^{\bullet}_{A}(N)
denote the induced filtered map of
C^{-}_{A}(R)
-modules. Then by Lemma
5.33
part (ii) and the exactness of
C^{\bullet}_{A}
, there is for each
p
a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{\bullet}_{A}(\overline{F}_{p}M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{\bullet}_{A}(\overline{F}_{p}N)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{F}_{p}\hat{C}^{\bullet}_{A}(M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{F}_{p}\hat{C}^{\bullet}_{A}(N).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\overline{f}_{p})^{\bullet}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{g}_{p}}

By assumption
\overline{f}_{p}
is a quasi-isomorphism, so since the functor

C^{\bullet}_{A}
preserves quasi-isomorphisms it follows that
\overline{f}_{p}^{\bullet}
is a quasi-isomorphism. As the vertical maps are isomorphisms,
we conclude that
\overline{g}_{p}
is a quasi-isomorphism. By Lemma

5.33
part (i) the filtrations of
\hat{C}^{\bullet}_{A}(M)

and
\hat{C}^{\bullet}_{A}(N)
are exhaustive and complete Hausdorff. We
conclude that
g=\hat{f}^{\bullet}
is a quasi-isomorphism using the comparison result; Lemma
5.39
.
The lemma applies equally well to the filtered subcomplexes. Consequently,
the restrictions
g_{p}\colon F_{p}\hat{C}^{\bullet}_{A}(M)\rightarrow F_{p}\hat{C}^{\bullet}(N)

are also quasi-isomorphisms for all
p
. This shows that

g
is in fact a filtered quasi-isomorphism.
∎

Next we have to establish a similar invariance result for variations in
the DG algebra.

Proposition 5.41
.

Let
f\colon A\rightarrow B
be a quasi-isomorphism of degreewise

R
-free augmented algebras and let
M
be a filtered
B
-module.
Then there is for each
\bullet\in\{+,-,(+,tw),\infty\}
a natural
isomorphism of
H^{-}_{A}(R)\cong H^{-}_{B}(R)
-modules

\hat{H}^{\bullet}_{A}(f^{-1}M)\cong\hat{H}^{\bullet}_{B}(M),

compatible with the exact triangles of Lemma
5.38
.

Proof.

We first consider the case
\bullet=+
. For each pair

q<p
there is by Proposition
5.5
a natural quasi-isomorphism

f_{*}\colon C^{+}_{A}(F_{p}f^{-1}M/F_{q}f^{-1}M)\rightarrow C^{+}_{B}(F_{p}M/F_{q}M)

linear along
f^{*}\colon C^{-}_{B}(R)\rightarrow C^{-}_{A}(R)
. By applying
\operatorname{lim}_{q}\operatorname{colim}_{p}

we obtain a filtered map
g=\hat{f}_{*}\colon\hat{C}^{+}_{A}(f^{-1}M)\rightarrow\hat{C}^{+}_{B}(M)

linear along
f^{*}:C^{-}_{B}(R)\rightarrow C^{-}_{A}(R)
.
By construction, the following diagram commutes for each
p
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{+}_{A}(\overline{F}_{p}f^{-1}M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{+}_{B}(\overline{F}_{p}M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{F}_{p}\hat{C}^{+}_{A}(f^{-1}M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{F}_{p}\hat{C}^{+}_{B}(M)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{(f_{*})}_{p}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{g}_{p}}

As the upper horizontal map is a quasi-isomorphism, so is the lower
horizontal map and we conclude that
g=\hat{f}_{*}
is a filtered
quasi-isomorphism by Lemma
5.39
.
For
\bullet=-
there is for each pair
q<p
a natural quasi-isomorphism

f^{*}\colon C^{-}_{B}(F_{p}M/F_{q}M)\rightarrow C^{-}_{A}(F_{p}f^{-1}M/F_{q}f^{-1}M).

By passing to the colimit and limit we obtain a filtered map

\hat{f}^{*}\colon\hat{C}^{-}_{B}(M)\rightarrow\hat{C}^{-}_{A}(f^{-1}M)
, linear along

f^{*}\colon C^{-}_{B}(R)\rightarrow C^{-}_{A}(R)
. The same argument as above shows that
this is a filtered quasi-isomorphism.

For the two remaining cases recall that there is a relative functor

C^{+,tw}_{f}
, see Definition
5.19
, from the category of right
B
-modules to the category of left
C^{-}_{A}(R)
-modules. By Lemma

5.20
this functor is exact and preserves quasi-isomorphism, so we may promote it to a functor
\hat{C}^{+,tw}_{f}

between the corresponding filtered categories as we did with the others.
By passing to the colimit and then the limit in the commutative diagram
of Proposition
5.21
applied to
F_{p}M/F_{q}M

for all
q<p
, we obtain a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{C}^{+,tw}_{B}(M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{C}^{+,tw}_{f}(M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{C}^{+,tw}_{A}(f^{-1}M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{C}^{-}_{B}(M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{C}^{-}_{A}(f^{-1}M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{C}^{-}_{A}(f^{-1}M)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{\beta}_{M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{N}_{M}^{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{N}^{f}_{M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{N}^{A}_{M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{\alpha}_{M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{f}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}

of filtered DG modules over
C^{-}_{A}(R)
or
C^{-}_{B}(R)
as appropriate.
Furthermore, all the maps are filtered and
C^{-}_{A}(R)
-linear,
C^{-}_{B}(R)
-linear
or linear along
f^{*}\colon C^{-}_{B}(R)\rightarrow C^{-}_{A}(R)
as appropriate.
By the same proposition, as
f\colon A\rightarrow B
is a quasi-isomorphism of degreewise free DG algebras, all the horizontal arrows in the diagram applied
to
F_{p}M/F_{q}M
are quasi-isomorphisms. This implies, by the now familiar
argument, that all the horizontal arrows in the above diagram are
filtered quasi-isomorphisms. In particular, there is a natural isomorphism

\hat{H}^{+,tw}_{A}(f^{-1}M)\cong\hat{H}^{+,tw}_{B}(M)
. Moreover, we
obtain a zigzag of quasi-isomorphisms between the cones of the vertical maps

\hbox to248.08pt{\vbox to19.97pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 124.03864pt\lower-9.98308pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

{

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {{
