In view of Lemma
5.38
, we obtain an
isomorphism
\hat{H}^{\infty}_{A}(f^{-1}M)\cong\hat{H}^{\infty}_{B}(M)
linear along
H^{-}_{A}(R)\cong H^{-}_{B}(R)
as well. The above
diagram also ensures that these isomorphisms are compatible with the corresponding exact triangles.
∎

The key application of these invariance results is the following.

Corollary 5.42
.

Suppose that
\frac{1}{2}\in R
. Then for each
\bullet\in\{+,-,(+,tw),\infty\}
there is an isomorphism

H(DCI^{\bullet}(Y,E))\cong I^{\bullet}(Y,E)
of
H^{-}_{A}(R)\cong R[U]
-modules.
These isomorphisms are compatible with the corresponding exact triangles.

Proof.

As
\frac{1}{2}\in R
there are, according to
Proposition
2.9
, a quasi-isomorphism
i\colon\Lambda_{R}[u]\rightarrow C^{gm}_{*}(\operatorname{SO}(3))
of DG algebras and a zigzag

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{CI}(Y,E)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces DCI(Y,E)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

of filtered objects and filtered
\Lambda_{R}[u]
-homomorphisms
such that
\overline{f}_{p}
and
\overline{g}_{p}
are quasi-isomorphism for each
p\in\mathbb{Z}
. The required result now follows from Propositions

5.40
and
5.41
.
∎

For
A=\Lambda_{R}[u]
we can extend a number of the results from
the previous section.

Proposition 5.43
.

Let
A=\Lambda_{R}[u]
and let
M
be a filtered

A
-module. Then there is a filtered quasi-isomorphism

\hat{f}_{M}\colon\hat{C}^{+}_{A}(M)\rightarrow\hat{C}^{+,tw}_{A}(M)
of degree
3

and a (filtered) homotopy
\hat{s}_{M}\colon\hat{C}^{+}_{A}(M)\rightarrow\hat{C}^{+,tw}_{A}(M)

such that
\hat{f}_{M}U-U\hat{f}_{M}=\partial\hat{s}_{M}-\hat{s}_{M}\partial
for

U\in R[U]\cong C^{-}_{A}(R)
. Moreover, there is a (filtered) quasi-isomorphism

\operatorname{Cone}(\hat{N}_{M}\circ\hat{f})\rightarrow\operatorname{Cone}(\hat{N}_{M})\cong\hat{C}^{\infty}_{A}(M),

which is
R[U]
-linear up to homotopy, where the source is given the

R[U]
-module structure determined by the homotopy
\hat{t}_{M}=\hat{N}_{M}\circ\hat{s}_{M}
as in Lemma
5.25
. Furthermore, the resulting isomorphism in homology is compatible with the associated exact triangles.

Proof.

Recall from Lemma
5.24
that there
is for each right
A
-module
N
a quasi-isomorphism

f_{N}\colon C^{+}_{A}(N)\rightarrow C^{+,tw}_{A}(N)
of degree
3

and a homotopy
s_{N}\colon C^{+}_{A}(N)\rightarrow C^{+,tw}_{A}(N)
such that

f_{N}U-Uf_{N}=\partial s_{N}-s_{N}\partial
. Let
f_{p,q}
and
s_{p,q}
denote the
maps obtained for
N=F_{p}M/F_{q}M
for
q<p
. By naturality we
may pass to the colimit over
p
and then to the limit over
q
to
obtain maps
\hat{f}_{M}\colon\hat{C}^{+}_{A}(M)\rightarrow\hat{C}^{+,tw}_{A}(M)

and
\hat{s}_{M}\colon\hat{C}^{+}_{A}(M)\rightarrow\hat{C}^{+,tw}_{A}(M)
. As
f_{p,q}U-Uf_{p,q}=s_{p,q}\partial-\partial s_{p,q}
is valid for each pair
q<p
,
it follows that

\hat{f}_{M}U-U\hat{f}_{M}=\partial\hat{s}_{M}-\hat{s}_{M}\partial
. The fact that

\hat{f}_{M}
is a quasi-isomorphism is proved as before using
Lemma
5.39
.

The final statement follows by applying
\operatorname{lim}_{q}\operatorname{colim}_{p}

to the diagram (
5.8
) in the proof of
Theorem
5.28
for
F_{p}M/F_{q}M
and another
application of Lemma
5.39
.
It is clear from this construction that one obtains an isomorphism
between the corresponding exact triangles in homology.
∎

Combining the above proposition with Corollary
5.42
we obtain the following.

Corollary 5.44
.

Suppose that
\frac{1}{2}\in R
. Then there is a degree
3
isomorphism
I^{+}(Y,E)\cong I^{+,tw}(Y,E)
of
R[U]
-modules and there is an exact triangle of
R[U]
-modules

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I^{+}(Y,E)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I^{-}(Y,E)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I^{\infty}(Y,E)}
,
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces[3]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces[0]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces[-4]}

where the numbers specify the degrees of the maps.

We will now restrict ourselves to the cases of interest, namely,

(A,M)=(C^{gm}_{*}(\operatorname{SO}(3)),\widetilde{CI}(Y,E))\;\;\mbox{ or }\;\;(A,M)=(\Lambda_{R}[u],DCI(Y,E)),

where
M
is equipped with the index filtration. Recall from
Definition
2.8
and Definition
2.4
that
M

was obtained from a multicomplex
(M_{*,*},\{\partial^{r}\}_{r=0}^{5})
, and that the index filtration was defined to be the column filtration, i.e.,
F_{p}M_{n}=\bigoplus_{s\leq p}M_{s,n-s}
. In both cases it holds true that
M_{s,t}=0
for all
t>4
and
t<0
(for
M=DCI(Y,E)
this is true for
t\geq 4
). This implies that the filtration is degreewise finite and therefore exhaustive and
complete Hausdorff. Explicitly,

(5.10)

F_{p}M_{n}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}M_{n}&\mbox{ for all }p\geq n\\
0&\mbox{ for all }p\leq n-5\end{array}\right.

The following lemma indicates why it is desirable to introduce the full completions of the various functors.

Lemma 5.45
.

The filtration of
C^{+}_{A}(M)
,
F_{p}C^{+}_{A}(M)=C^{+}_{A}(F_{p}M)
, is degreewise bounded above, exhaustive and Hausdorff.
The filtration of
C^{-}_{A}(M)
is degreewise bounded below and complete
Hausdorff. If
M\neq 0
the filtration of
C^{+}_{A}(M)
fails to be complete
and the filtration of
C^{-}_{A}(M)
fails to be exhaustive. Therefore,

\hat{C}^{+}_{A}(M)=\operatorname{lim}_{q}C^{+}_{A}(M/F_{q}M)\;\;\mbox{ and }\;\;\hat{C}^{-}_{A}(M)=\operatorname{colim}_{p}C^{-}_{A}(F_{p}M).

Proof.

We will use the underlying bigrading of the multicomplex

M_{*,*}
. As a graded module (not DG) we have
C^{+}_{A}(M)=B(M,A,R)\cong M\otimes BA
. Therefore, in degree
n

(5.11)

C^{+}_{A}(M)_{n}=\bigoplus_{u+r=n}M_{u}\otimes BA_{r}=\bigoplus_{s+t+r=n}M_{s,t}\otimes BA_{r},

and
F_{p}C^{+}_{A}(M)
is obtained by imposing the condition
s\leq p
in the
above right hand direct sum. First, notice that
A
and hence
BA
are
supported in nonnegative degrees. Then, as
M_{s,t}=0
for
t<0
,
it follows
F_{p}C^{+}_{A}(M)_{n}=C^{+}_{A}(M)_{n}
for
p\geq n
. This shows
that the filtration is degreewise bounded above and hence exhaustive.
It also follows from the above description that the filtration is Hausdorff.
On the other hand

(5.12)

\lim_{p}C^{+}_{A}(M/F_{p}M)_{n}\cong\lim_{p}\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}s+t+r=n\\
s>p\end{subarray}}M_{s,t}\otimes BA_{r}\cong\prod_{s+t+r=n}M_{s,t}\otimes BA_{r}.

This does not coincide with the direct sum in equation (
5.11
)
if
M\neq 0
, due to the periodicity
M_{s,t}\cong M_{s+8,t}
for all
s,t
.
Thus,
C^{+}_{A}(M)
fails to be complete if
M\neq 0
.

Next, there is an isomorphism
C^{-}_{A}(M)=\operatorname{Hom}_{A}(EA,M)\cong\operatorname{Hom}(BA,M)
of graded modules. In a fixed degree
n
, we find

(5.13)

C^{-}_{A}(M)_{n}=\operatorname{Hom}(BA,M)_{n}=\prod_{s+t-r=n}\operatorname{Hom}(BA_{r},M_{s,t})

and as above
F_{p}C^{-}_{A}(M)
is obtained by imposing the condition

s\leq p
in the right hand product. For the factor
\operatorname{Hom}(BA_{r},M_{s,t})

to be nonzero it is necessary that
r\geq 0
and
0\leq t\leq 4
, and therefore

s=n+r-t\geq n-4
. Hence,
F_{p}C^{-}_{A}(M)_{n}=0
for
p<n-4
showing
that the filtration is degreewise bounded below, and thus complete
Hausdorff. Moreover, the above
also implies that the right hand product in (
5.13
) subject
to the condition
s\leq p
is finite and therefore coincides with the
direct sum. Consequently,
\operatorname{colim}_{p}F_{p}C^{-}_{A}(M)
is given by

(5.14)

\operatorname{colim}_{p}\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}s+t-r=n\\
s\leq p\end{subarray}}\operatorname{Hom}(BA_{r},M_{s,t})=\bigoplus_{s+t-r=n}\operatorname{Hom}(BA_{r},M_{s,t}),

which does not coincide with the product in (
5.13
) as
long as
M\neq 0
. This shows that the filtration fails to be exhaustive
if
M\neq 0
.

The description of
\hat{C}^{+}_{A}(M)
and
\hat{C}^{-}_{A}(M)
given in the
statement is now a consequence of Lemma
5.33
part
(iii) and (iv).
∎

Remark 5.46
.

Miller
[
Eis19
, Appendix A]
defines completed versions
of
C^{\pm}_{A}
using what he names the completed bar construction and
the finitely supported cobar construction. He defines the completed bar construction,
\hat{B}(M,A,N)
, to be the completion of
B(M,A,N)
with respect to the filtration by internal degree, while the finitely supported cobar
construction
c\hat{B}(N,A,M)\subset\operatorname{Hom}_{A}(B(N,A,A),M)
is the
subcomplex consisting of those functionals
that vanish on
B_{p,q}(N,A,A)
for all sufficiently large
p
. The above lemma, in particular equations (
5.12
) and
(
5.14
), verifies that our
\hat{C}^{+}_{A}(M)
and
\hat{C}^{-}_{A}(M)

coincide with Miller’s
\hat{B}(M,A,R)
and
c\hat{B}(R,A,M)

for
M=\widetilde{CI}(Y,E)
and
M=DCI(Y,E)
with
A
as appropriate.

The final part of this section is devoted to extending the concrete
models of Proposition
5.8
and Theorem
5.28
to the complex
DCI^{\bullet}(Y,E)
for
\bullet\in\{+,-,\infty\}
. We will therefore assume that
\frac{1}{2}\in R
throughout.

Theorem 5.47
.

Let
A=\Lambda_{R}[u]
and let
(D^{\pm}_{*,*},\partial^{\prime},\partial^{\prime\prime})

be the double complexes associated with
M=DCI(Y,E)
in Proposition

5.8
.
Then there are isomorphisms of DG
R[U]
-modules

DCI^{+}(Y,E)\cong\operatorname{Tot}^{\Pi}(D^{+}_{*,*},\partial^{\prime},\partial^{\prime\prime})\;\;\mbox{ and }\;\;DCI^{-}(Y,E)\cong\operatorname{Tot}^{\oplus}(D^{-}_{*,*},\partial^{\prime},\partial^{\prime\prime}).

In particular, there are identifications

DCI^{+}(Y)_{n}\cong\prod_{p\geq 0}M_{n-4p}\;\;\mbox{ and }\;\;DCI^{-}(Y)_{n}\cong\bigoplus_{p\geq 0}M_{n+4p}

for each
n\in\mathbb{Z}
. An element
x\in DCI^{\pm}(Y)_{n}
can therefore be expressed
by a sequence
x=(m_{n+4p})_{p}
where we require
p\leq 0
in the
+

case and
p\geq 0
and almost all
m_{n+4p}=0
in the
-
case. The
differential is in both cases given by

\partial x=(\partial_{M}m_{n+4p}-(-1)^{n}m_{n+4(p-1)}u)_{p}

and the action of
R[U]
is determined by
U\cdot x=(m_{n+4(p+1)})_{p}
.

Proof.

The isomorphism

C^{+}_{A}(M)\cong\operatorname{Tot}^{\oplus}(D^{+}_{*,*},\partial^{\prime},\partial^{\prime\prime})

of Proposition
5.8
yields in degree
n

C^{+}_{A}(M)_{n}\cong\bigoplus_{s\geq 0}M_{n-4s}=\bigoplus_{s\geq 0,t}M_{t,n-4s-t}

and
F_{p}C^{+}_{A}(M)
is obtained by imposing the condition
t\leq p
in the
right hand sum. Now the calculation of the limit
\operatorname{lim}_{p}C^{+}_{A}(M)_{n}/F_{p}C^{+}_{A}(M)_{n}
goes through as in equation (
5.12
) of the
previous proof to show that
\hat{C}^{+}_{A}(M)_{n}\cong\prod_{s\geq 0}M_{n-4s}
.

The other case is analogous and the calculation of the relevant
colimit proceeds as in equation (
5.14
) of the previous proof.
∎

Note that the only difference between the
above theorem and Proposition
5.8
is that we have interchanged the type of totalization we apply to the double complexes.

We also obtain the following explicit model computing
I^{\infty}(Y,E)
.

Theorem 5.48
.

Let
A=\Lambda_{R}[u]
, let

(D^{\pm}_{*,*},\partial^{\prime},\partial^{\prime\prime})
and
(D^{\infty}_{*,*},\partial^{\prime},\partial^{\prime\prime})
be the double
complexes associated with
M=DCI(Y,E)
in the
above theorem and Theorem
5.28
, respectively. Let

\nu,\psi\colon\operatorname{Tot}^{\Pi}(D^{+},\partial^{\prime},\partial^{\prime\prime})\rightarrow\operatorname{Tot}^{\oplus}(D^{-},\partial^{\prime},\partial^{\prime\prime})

of degree
3
and
0
respectively, be given degreewise by the compositions

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{s\geq 0}M_{n-4s}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{n+3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigoplus_{t\geq 0}M_{n+3+4t}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{s\geq 0}M_{n-4s}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigoplus_{t\geq 0}M_{n+4t},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(-1)^{n}u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}

where
\pi
and
\iota
denote the projection and inclusion, respectively.
Then
\nu
is a chain map satisfying
\nu U-U\nu=\partial\psi-\psi\partial
. Define

\operatorname{Cone}(\nu)
with the
R[U]
-module structure adjusted by the homotopy

\psi
as in Lemma
5.25
. Then there is an isomorphism

R[U]
-modules
\operatorname{Tot}^{\Pi,\infty}(D^{\infty}_{*,*},\partial^{\prime},\partial^{\prime\prime})\cong\operatorname{Cone}(\nu)
and a filtered quasi-isomorphism between this complex and

DCI^{\infty}(Y,E)
, which is
R[U]
-linear up to homotopy. Moreover, this quasi-isomorphism is compatible with the corresponding exact triangles
in homology.

Proof.

By Proposition
5.43

there is a filtered quasi-isomorphism
\hat{f}_{M}\colon\hat{C}^{+}_{A}(M)\rightarrow\hat{C}^{+,tw}_{A}(M)
of degree
3
and a filtered homotopy

\hat{s}_{M}:\hat{C}^{+}_{A}(M)\rightarrow\hat{C}^{+,tw}_{A}(M)
such that

\hat{f}_{M}U-U\hat{f}_{M}=\partial\hat{s}_{M}-\hat{s}_{M}\partial
. Furthermore, there is
an induced filtered quasi-isomorphism
\operatorname{Cone}(\hat{N}_{M}\circ\hat{f}_{M})\rightarrow\hat{C}^{\infty}_{A}(M)
, which is
R[U]
-linear up to homotopy when the cone
is given the adjusted
R[U]
-module structure determined by the homotopy

\hat{t}_{M}=\hat{N}_{M}\circ\hat{s}_{M}
. In terms of the explicit models
of the above theorem
\hat{N}_{M}\circ\hat{f}_{M}

and
\hat{N}_{M}\circ\hat{s}_{M}
are precisely
\nu
and
\psi
as defined
in the statement. The verification of this is done just as in Lemma

5.24
. The fact that

\operatorname{Cone}(\nu)\cong\operatorname{Tot}^{\Pi,\infty}(D^{\infty}_{*,*},\partial^{\prime},\partial^{\prime\prime})

is proved just as in Theorem
5.28
.
∎

Remark 5.49
.

The proofs of Theorem
5.47
and
Theorem
5.48
go through without any essential
change with
M=\widetilde{CI}(Y,E)
, regarded as a
\Lambda_{R}[u]
-module, in
place of
M=DCI(Y,E)
.

We may extract the following noteworthy consequences, showing in particular
that the framed homology groups
\widetilde{I}(Y,E)\coloneqq H(DCI(Y,E))\cong H(\widetilde{CI}(Y,E))
may be recovered from the
R[U]
-action on
I^{\pm}(Y,E)

up to extension.

Corollary 5.50
.

There are long exact sequences

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{I}_{n}(Y,E)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I^{+}(Y,E)_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I^{+}(Y,E)_{n-4}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{I}(Y,E)_{n-1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{I}(Y,E)_{n-3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I^{-}(Y,E)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I^{-}(Y,E)_{n-4}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{I}(Y,E)_{n-4}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}

Moreover,
U:I^{\infty}(Y,E)\rightarrow I^{\infty}(Y,E)
is
an isomorphism.

Proof.

Let
M=DCI(Y,E)
and
A=\Lambda_{R}[u]
. In terms of the
explicit models for
DCI^{\pm}(Y,E)=\hat{C}^{\pm}_{A}(M)
the action of

U\in R[U]
are in degree
n
given by the natural shifts

U\colon\prod_{s\geq 0}M_{n-4s}\rightarrow\prod_{s\geq 0}M_{n-4-4s}\;\;\mbox{ and }\;\;U\colon\bigoplus_{s\geq 0}M_{n+4s}\rightarrow\bigoplus_{s\geq 0}M_{n-4+4s},

respectively. Explicitly,
(x_{s})_{s\geq 0}\mapsto(x_{s+1})_{s\geq 0}
in the first case and

(y_{s})_{s\geq 0}\mapsto(0,y_{0},y_{1},\cdots)
in the second. From this we
deduce that the first map is surjective with kernel
D^{+}_{0,*}\cong M

and the second is injective with cokernel isomorphic to
D^{-}_{0,*}\cong M
.
There are therefore short exact sequences of chain complexes

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D^{+}_{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D^{+}_{*}[4]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D^{-}_{*}[-4]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D^{-}_{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}

giving rise to the stated long exact sequences in homology.
The final statement is a consequence of the fact that the shift

U\colon D^{\infty}_{n}=\prod_{s\to\infty}M_{n-4s}\rightarrow\prod_{s\to\infty}M_{n-4-4s}=D^{\infty}_{n-4}

is an isomorphism in each degree
n
and therefore also an isomorphism
upon passage to homology.
∎

Remark 5.51
.

The fact that
U\colon I^{\infty}(Y,E)\rightarrow I^{\infty}(Y,E)
is an isomorphism when
\frac{1}{2}\in R
was first proved using a spectral sequence argument in
[
Eis19
]
.

5.5.
The Index Spectral Sequences

For
\bullet\in\{+,-,(+,tw),\infty\}
the complexes
CI^{\bullet}(Y,E)
and
DCI^{\bullet}(Y,E)
introduced in Definition
5.36
come equipped with filtrations and therefore give rise to spectral sequences.
These will be called index spectral sequences
as the filtrations are induced by the index filtrations of
\widetilde{CI}(Y,E)

and
DCI(Y,E)
. The purpose of this section is to recall
some theory on spectral sequences
and then to establish the basic properties of the index spectral sequences.
We will mainly follow Boardman’s paper
[
Boa99
]
, but we will
stick to homological notation.
All of this will be very useful for our calculations in the next section.

Recall that an (unrolled) exact couple
(A,E,i,j,k)
consists of two sequences
of graded modules
(A_{s})_{s}
and
(E_{s})_{s}
, indexed over
s\in\mathbb{Z}
,
and homomorphisms
i=(i_{s}:A_{s-1}\rightarrow A_{s})_{s}
,
j=(j_{s}:A_{s}\rightarrow E_{s})_{s}

and
k=(k_{s}:E_{s}\rightarrow A_{s-1})_{s}
such that the sequence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{s-1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{s}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E_{s}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{s-1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{s}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{s}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{s}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k_{s}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{s}}

is exact for each
s
. We will generally omit the lower index on the maps,
letting the source and target specify the map in question.
In our situation the degrees of
(i,j,k)
will
be
(0,0,-1)
. For each
r\geq 1
and
s
set

\displaystyle Z^{r}_{s}

\displaystyle\coloneqq k^{-1}\operatorname{Im}[i^{(r-1)}:A_{s-r}\rightarrow A_{s-1}]

\displaystyle B^{r}_{s}

\displaystyle\coloneqq j\operatorname{Ker}[i^{(r-1)}:A_{s}\rightarrow A_{s+r-1}]

\displaystyle E^{r}_{s}

\displaystyle\coloneqq Z^{r}_{s}/B^{r}_{s},

where
i^{(r)}
denotes the
r
-fold composition
i\circ\cdots\circ i

for
r\geq 1
and
i^{(0)}
is the identity.
Furthermore, put
Z^{\infty}_{s}\coloneqq\bigcap_{r}Z^{r}_{s}
,
B^{\infty}_{s}\coloneqq\bigcup_{r}B^{r}_{s}

and
E^{\infty}_{s}\coloneqq Z^{\infty}_{s}/B^{\infty}_{s}
. For the later convergence theory it is also important to introduce the derived limit

RE^{\infty}_{s}\coloneqq\operatorname{Rlim}_{s}Z^{r}_{s}
. There are differentials

d^{r}\colon E^{r}_{s}\rightarrow E^{r}_{s-r}
for each
s
and
r\geq 1
, defined in the following way. Let
x\in Z^{r}_{s}
represent the class
[x]\in E^{r}_{s}
. Choose
y\in A_{s-r}

such that
i^{(r-1)}(y)=k(x)
. Then
d^{r}([x])=[j(y)]\in E^{r}_{s-r}
.
The situation is illustrated in the following diagram

(5.15)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y\in A_{s-r}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k(x)\in A_{s-1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j(y)\in E_{s-r}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x\in E_{s}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i^{(r-1)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}

One may then show that
d^{r}
is well-defined, that

d^{r}\circ d^{r}=0
and that

E^{r+1}_{s}\cong\operatorname{Ker}(d^{r}\colon E_{s}^{r}\rightarrow E^{r}_{s-r})/\operatorname{Im}(d^{r}\colon E^{r}_{s+r}\rightarrow E^{r}_{s})

for each
r\geq 1
and
s
. In other words,
(E^{r},d^{r})_{r\geq 1}

is a spectral sequence. We call it the spectral sequence associated with
the exact couple
(A,E,i,j,k)
. Every exact couple we will meet
is covered by the following example (see
[
Boa99
, Section 9]
).

Example 5.52
.

Let
C
be a DG module equipped with an increasing filtration
\{F_{s}C\}_{s}
by
DG submodules. For each
s\in\mathbb{Z}
let

A_{s}=H(F_{s}C)
and
E_{s}=H(F_{s}C/F_{s-1}C)
. Define
i\colon A_{s-1}\rightarrow A_{s}
,
j\colon A_{s}\rightarrow E_{s}
and
k\colon E_{s}\rightarrow A_{s-1}
to be the maps induced
by the short exact sequence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{s-1}C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{s}C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{s}C/F_{s-1}C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}

upon passage to homology. In particular,
k
is the connecting homomorphism
of degree
-1
. Then
(A,E,i,j,k)
is an exact couple.
The spectral sequence associated with this exact couple is the spectral
sequence mentioned in (
5.9
). If
B
is a
DG algebra and
C
is a
B
-module filtered by
B
-submodules, then

A_{s}
,
E_{s}
carry the structure of graded
H(B)
-modules, and the maps

i,j,k
are
H(B)
-linear. Moreover, this structure carries in a natural
way over to the whole spectral sequence.

Returning to the situation of an exact couple
(A,E,i,j,k)
put

(5.16)

A_{\infty}\coloneqq\operatorname{colim}_{s}A_{s},\;\;A_{-\infty}\coloneqq\operatorname{lim}_{s}A_{s}\;\;\mbox{ and }\;\;RA_{-\infty}\coloneqq\operatorname{Rlim}_{s}A_{s}.

For us the relevant target for the spectral sequence is the colimit

A_{\infty}
, filtered by
F_{s}A_{\infty}\coloneqq\operatorname{Im}(A_{s}\rightarrow A_{\infty})
.
For each
s\in\mathbb{Z}
there is a natural inclusion
\phi_{s}\colon F_{s}A_{\infty}/F_{s-1}A_{\infty}\rightarrow E^{\infty}_{s}
given by the composition

(5.17)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{s}A_{\infty}/F_{s-1}A_{\infty}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Im}j/B^{\infty}_{s}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z^{\infty}_{s}/B^{\infty}_{s}=E^{\infty}_{s}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

Here, the first isomorphism is given by sending
[x]\in F_{s}A_{\infty}/F_{s-1}A_{\infty}
, represented by
x\in F_{s}A_{\infty}
, to
[j(y)]\in\operatorname{Im}j/B^{\infty}_{s}
, where
y\in A_{s}
is a lift of
x
along the surjection
A_{s}\rightarrow F_{s}A_{\infty}
,
and the second map is induced by the inclusion
\operatorname{Im}j=\operatorname{Ker}k\subset Z^{\infty}_{s}
(see
[
Boa99
, Lemma 5.6]
and its proof).

Definition 5.53
.

The spectral sequence
(E^{r},d^{r})_{r\geq 1}
associated
with the exact couple
(A,E,i,j,k)
converges strongly to the
colimit
A_{\infty}
if the filtration
F_{s}A_{\infty}=\operatorname{Im}(A_{s}\rightarrow A_{\infty})
of
A_{\infty}
is exhaustive and complete Hausdorff and the map

\phi_{s}\colon F_{s}A_{\infty}/F_{s-1}A_{\infty}\rightarrow E^{\infty}_{s}
is an isomorphisms for each
s\in\mathbb{Z}
.

Strong convergence is the ideal form of convergence. In theory,
if we can can calculate

E^{\infty}
we would know the subquotients
F_{s}A_{\infty}/F_{s-1}A_{\infty}

and provided we can solve the extension problems to determine

F_{s}A_{\infty}/F_{t}A_{\infty}
for each pair
s>t
, we can recover the target group
as

A_{\infty}\cong\operatorname{lim}_{s}\operatorname{colim}_{t}F_{t}A_{\infty}/F_{s}A_{\infty}.

Definition 5.54
.

[
Boa99
, Definition 5.10]

Let
(A,E,i,j,k)
be an exact couple with associated
spectral sequence
(E^{r},d^{r})_{r\geq 1}
. If

RA_{-\infty}=0
and
A_{-\infty}=0
(see (
5.16
)), then the spectral sequence is said to converge conditionally to the colimit
A_{\infty}

In our situation of a filtered DG module and the corresponding exact couple described in Example
5.52

we have the following result.

Theorem 5.55
.

[
Boa99
, Theorem 9.2]

Let
C
be a DG module
equipped with an increasing filtration
F_{s}C
by DG submodules.
Let
(A_{s},E_{s},i,j,k)
be the associated
exact couple with corresponding spectral sequence
(E^{r},d^{r})_{r\geq 1}
.
If the filtration of
C
is exhaustive and complete Hausdorff, then the
spectral sequence
(E^{r},d^{r})_{r\geq 1}
converges conditionally to the
colimit
A_{\infty}=H(C)
.

In the presence of conditional convergence there are two remaining obstructions
to achieving strong convergence; namely, the vanishing of the group

RE^{\infty}
and another group
W
. We refer to
[
Boa99
, Lemma 8.5]

for the general definition of
W
, but we note that if the exact
couple is constructed from a filtered complex, then
W
is isomorphic to the kernel of the canonical interchange map (see
[
HR19
]
)

\kappa\colon\operatorname{colim}_{p}\operatorname{lim}_{q}H(F_{p}C/F_{q}C)\rightarrow\operatorname{lim}_{q}\operatorname{colim}_{p}H(F_{p}C/F_{q}C).

We then have the following convergence theorem.

Theorem 5.56
.

[
Boa99
, Theorem 8.2]

Let
(A,E,i,j,k)

be an exact couple and assume that the associated spectral sequence

(E^{r},d^{r})_{r\geq 1}
converges conditionally to the colimit
A_{\infty}
. Then
if
W=0
and
RE^{\infty}=0
the spectral converges strongly to the
colimit
A_{\infty}
.

The following result gives a few standard criteria for the vanishing of

RE^{\infty}
and
W
in the situation of a filtered complex.

Proposition 5.57
.

Let
C
be a filtered complex with associated
exact couple
(A,E,i,j,k)
and spectral sequence
(E^{r},d^{r})_{r\geq 1}
.

(i)

If the filtration of
C
is degreewise bounded below, then

RE^{\infty}=0
and
W=0
.

(ii)

If the filtration of
C
is degreewise bounded above then
W=0
.

(iii)

If the spectral sequence degenerates at some finite stage; that is,

E^{r_{0}}=E^{r_{0}+1}=\cdots=E^{\infty}
for some finite
r_{0}\geq 1
, then

RE^{\infty}=0
and
W=0
.

We may now apply this theory to the filtered complexes

CI^{\bullet}(Y,E)
for
\bullet\in\{+,-,(+,tw),\infty\}
. This is
a good point to explain that the mod
8
periodicity of
\widetilde{CI}(Y,E)

carries over to the complexes
CI^{\bullet}(Y,E)
. Let
M=\widetilde{CI}(Y,E)

and let
A=C^{gm}_{*}(\operatorname{SO}(3))
or
A=\Lambda_{R}[u]
. Then the periodicity
may be expressed as an isomorphism
M\cong M[8]
of
A
-modules. Moreover,
this isomorphism is compatible with the filtration in the sense that

F_{p+8}M\cong(F_{p}M)[8]
for all
p\in\mathbb{Z}
. There is therefore an
induced isomorphism

\displaystyle CI^{\bullet}(Y,E)

\displaystyle=\;\operatorname{lim}_{q}\operatorname{colim}_{p}C^{\bullet}_{A}(F_{p+8}M/F_{q+8}M)

\displaystyle\cong\;\operatorname{lim}_{q}\operatorname{colim}_{p}C^{\bullet}_{A}(F_{p}M/F_{q}M)[8]=CI^{\bullet}(Y,E)[8].

This isomorphism is seen to be filtered in the same sense:

F_{p}CI^{\bullet}(Y,E)_{n}\cong F_{p+8}CI^{\bullet}(Y,E)_{n+8}
for all
p
and

n
.

Theorem 5.58
.

Let
A=C_{*}^{gm}(\operatorname{SO}(3))
. There is a conditionally convergent spectral sequence of
H^{-}_{A}(R)
-modules

E^{1}_{s,t}=\bigoplus_{j(\alpha)\equiv s}H^{\bullet}_{\operatorname{SO}(3)}(\alpha)_{t}\implies I^{\bullet}(Y,E)_{s+t}

for each
\bullet\in\{+,-,(+,tw),\infty\}
, where

H^{\bullet}_{\operatorname{SO}(3)}(\alpha)\coloneqq H^{\bullet}_{A}(C^{gm}_{*}(\alpha))

for each critical orbit
\alpha\in\mathcal{C}
. Moreover, the spectral sequences
are periodic in the sense that there are isomorphisms

E_{s,t}^{r}\cong E_{s+8,t}^{r}
for all
s,t,r
. These isomorphisms commute
with the differentials and the
H^{-}_{A}(R)
-action and they are compatible
with the target in the sense that

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{F}_{s}I^{\bullet}_{s+t}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{F}_{s+8}I^{\bullet}_{s+t+8}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E^{\infty}_{s,t}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E^{\infty}_{s+8,t}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

commutes for all
s,t
.

The spectral sequence for
I^{-}(Y,E)
converges strongly, while the
spectral sequence for
I^{+}(Y,E)
converges strongly provided
RE^{\infty}=0
.

Proof.

Let
M=\widetilde{CI}(Y,E)
equipped with the index filtration.
For each
\bullet\in\{+,-,(+,tw),\infty\}
the complex
CI^{\bullet}(Y,E)=\hat{C}^{\bullet}_{A}(M)
carries
by construction an exhaustive and complete Hausdorff filtration with
minimal filtration quotients

\overline{F}_{p}\hat{C}^{\bullet}_{A}(M)\cong C^{\bullet}_{A}(\overline{F}_{p}M).

There is therefore by Theorem

5.55
a conditionally convergent spectral sequence

E^{1}_{s,t}=H^{\bullet}_{A}(\overline{F}_{s}M)_{s+t}\implies\hat{H}^{\bullet}_{A}(M)_{s+t}=I^{\bullet}(Y,E)_{s+t}.

From the definition of
\widetilde{CI}(Y,E)
(Definition
2.4
) we may identify

\overline{F}_{s}M_{s+t}=\bigoplus_{j(\alpha)\equiv s}C^{gm}_{t}(\alpha)

so that
E^{1}_{s,t}\cong\bigoplus_{j(\alpha)\equiv s}H_{\operatorname{SO}(3)}(\alpha)_{t}

in the notation of the statement.

Next, the periodicity isomorphisms

F_{p}\hat{C}^{\bullet}_{A}(M)_{n}\cong F_{p+8}\hat{C}^{\bullet}_{A}(M)_{n+8}
,
compatible with the differentials and the
C^{-}_{A}(R)
-structure, induce a morphism of the associated exact couple. That is, there are isomorphisms

A_{s}\cong A_{s+8}
,
E_{s}\cong E_{s+8}
each
s
, compatible with the structure maps. It is then a straightforward exercise to verify
that this gives rise to the periodicity in the spectral sequence, compatible
with the target, as stated.

Finally, for the convergence we know by Lemma
5.45

that the filtration of
\hat{C}^{+}_{A}(M)
is degreewise bounded above and that the filtration of
\hat{C}^{-}_{A}(M)
is degreewise bounded below. This
implies by Proposition
5.57
and Theorem

5.56
that the spectral sequence associated
with
I^{-}(Y,E)
converges strongly and that the spectral sequence
associated with
I^{+}(Y,E)
converges strongly provided
RE^{\infty}=0
.
∎

Provided
\frac{1}{2}\in R
, these spectral sequences can equally well be constructed from
M=DCI(Y,E)
equipped with the index filtration and
A=\Lambda_{R}[u]
. This is what we will assume when we calculate
these groups for binary polyhedral spaces in the next section. Furthermore,
in this setting the calculation of the groups

H^{\bullet}_{\operatorname{SO}(3)}(C^{gm}_{*}(\alpha))\cong H^{\bullet}_{\Lambda_{R}[u]}(H_{*}(\alpha))

for each
\bullet\in\{+,-,(+,tw),\infty\}
, are contained in Lemma

5.7
, Corollary
5.9
and Corollary
5.30
. From these calculations it becomes
clear that the spectral sequences for
I^{+}
and
I^{-}
are contained
in the upper and lower half-planes respectively, while the spectral
sequence for
I^{\infty}
is a whole-plane spectral sequence.

6.
Calculations for Binary Polyhedral Spaces

We will from this point require that
2\in R
is invertible. The universal
example to have in mind is
R=\mathbb{Z}[\frac{1}{2}]
. Our main aim is to give
explicit calculations of the
H^{-}_{\Lambda_{R}[u]}(R)=R[U]
-modules

I^{+}(\overline{Y}_{\Gamma},E),\;\;I^{-}(\overline{Y}_{\Gamma},E)\;\;\mbox{ and }\;\;I^{\infty}(\overline{Y}_{\Gamma},E)

for each finite subgroup
\Gamma\subset\operatorname{SU}(2)
, where
E\rightarrow\overline{Y}_{\Gamma}
is the trivial
\operatorname{SU}(2)
-bundle.
To simplify the notation slightly we will omit the reference to the bundle in the notation from this point. In the final part we also include
the calculations of
I^{\bullet}(Y_{\Gamma})
for
\bullet\in\{+,-,\infty\}
,
omitting some details.

The two results Theorem
4.21
and Proposition
4.25
give a concrete description of the Donaldson model
\operatorname{DCI}(\overline{Y}_{\Gamma})
in all cases. The key tools needed in the calculations are the index spectral sequence in combination with the explicit models for
DCI^{\pm}
of Theorem
5.8
. In the
I^{+}
case, we will see that the index spectral sequence immediately
degenerates, and we are left with solving an extension problem in the
category of
R[U]
-modules. In the
I^{-}
case there are a number of
differentials in the index spectral sequence, but we will in each
case verify that the spectral sequence degenerates on some finite
page
E^{r_{0}}
. The extension problems in this case are neatly solved
by observing that
E^{\infty}_{s}
is a free
R[U]
-module for all
s
.

In
[
Eis19
, Corollary 8.7]
Miller calculates
I^{\infty}(Y)
for all rational homology spheres. This, of course, includes
\overline{Y}_{\Gamma}
,
so our calculation of this group should only be regarded as a verification
of his result. In addition, we observe that the norm map vanishes in
homology so that the exact triangle relating the three groups splits up
into short exact sequences.

Throughout this section we let
A=\Lambda_{R}[u]
with
|u|=3
. Given
a finite subgroup
\Gamma\subset\operatorname{SU}(2)
we will always write

\mathcal{C}
for the set of critical orbits, or equivalently, the set of
1
-dimensional quaternionic representations of
\Gamma
. Recall that
for each
\alpha\in\mathcal{C}
we have fixed generators

b_{\alpha}\in H_{0}(\alpha)
,
t_{\alpha}=b_{\alpha}\cdot u\in H_{3}(\alpha)

if
\alpha
is irreducible and
t_{\alpha}\in H_{2}(\alpha)
if
\alpha

is reducible.

6.1.
The Case
I^{+}

In this section we will calculate
I^{+}(\overline{Y}_{\Gamma})
as an

R[U]
-module for all finite subgroups
\Gamma\subset\operatorname{SU}(2)
.
Even though our arguments do not explicitly rely on the index spectral
sequence, we note that the motivation for the upcoming arguments
is the following observation.

Lemma 6.1
.

Let
M=DCI(\overline{Y}_{\Gamma})
for a
finite subgroup
\Gamma\subset\operatorname{SU}(2)
. Then the index spectral sequence

E^{1}_{s,t}=\bigoplus_{j(\alpha)\equiv s}H_{A}^{+}(\alpha)_{t}\implies I^{+}(\overline{Y}_{\Gamma})_{s+t}

degenerates at the
E^{1}
page, that is, all differentials are trivial and

E^{1}=E^{\infty}
.

Proof.

From the calculations in Lemma
5.7
and Corollary
5.9

we see that
H^{+}_{A}(\alpha)
vanishes in odd degrees for all types of
orbits
\alpha
. Then, as
j(\alpha)\equiv 0\ (\mathrm{mod}\ 4)
for all
\alpha\in\mathcal{C}
, it follows that
E^{1}_{s,t}=0
if
s
or
t
is odd. Since the differential

d^{r}
has bidegree
(-r,r-1)
we conclude that
d^{r}=0
for all
r\geq 1

as required.
∎

From this one may determine
I^{+}(\overline{Y}_{\Gamma})
as an
R
-module.
However, the structure as an
R[U]
-module is more subtle and this is
the reason we spell out our argument more directly in terms of the
filtration of the chain complex
DCI^{+}(\overline{Y}_{\Gamma})
.

Let
\Gamma\subset\operatorname{SU}(2)
be a finite subgroup and
let
M=DCI(\overline{Y}_{\Gamma})
. Let
\mathcal{C}^{irr}\subset\mathcal{C}
denote the
subset of free critical orbits or equivalently irreducible flat connections.
Define
M^{irr}\subset M
to be the submodule generated by
\mathcal{C}^{irr}
, i.e.,

M^{irr}_{s,t}=\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\in\mathcal{C}^{irr}\\
j(\alpha)\equiv s\end{subarray}}H_{t}(\alpha)\;\;\mbox{ and }\;\;M^{irr}_{n}=\bigoplus_{s+t=n}M^{irr}_{s,t}.

The following lemma is the key tool in resolving our extension problems in
the category of
R[U]
-modules.

Lemma 6.2
.

There is an
R
-linear map
\psi\colon M\rightarrow M
of degree
-4
satisfying

(i)

\psi(x)\cdot u=(-1)^{|x|}\partial_{M}x
for all
x\in M
,

(ii)

\operatorname{Im}(\psi)\subset M^{irr}
,

(iii)

\psi(\operatorname{Im}(u))=0
and

(iv)

\psi(\operatorname{Im}\partial_{M})=0
.

Proof.

By Theorem
4.21
the only possibly nontrivial
differentials in
M
are
\partial_{M}\colon M_{4s}\rightarrow M_{4s-1}
for
s\in\mathbb{Z}
. The same result also shows that
u\colon M_{4s-4}^{irr}\rightarrow M_{4s-1}^{irr}=M_{4s-1}
is an isomorphism for each
s
.
We may therefore define
\psi\colon M_{4s}\rightarrow M_{4s-4}^{irr}\subset M_{4s-4}
by
u^{-1}\circ\partial_{M}
and set
\psi=0\colon M_{t}\rightarrow M_{t-4}
in all other degrees. The relation
\psi(x)\cdot u=(-1)^{|x|}\partial_{M}x
is then satisfied for
all
x\in M
and by construction
\operatorname{Im}(\psi)\subset M^{irr}
.
For (iii) and (iv) it is then sufficient to note that
\operatorname{Im}(u)
and

\operatorname{Im}(\partial_{M})\subset M^{irr}
are supported in the degrees
M_{4s-1}
for
s\in\mathbb{Z}
.
∎

Remark 6.3
.

It is not difficult to show that the conditions
(i) and (ii) determine the map
\psi
uniquely, but we will not use
this explicitly.

Recall from Corollary
5.47
that for
M=DCI(\overline{Y}_{\Gamma})
there is an identification
DCI^{+}(\overline{Y}_{\Gamma})_{n}\cong\prod_{p\geq 0}M_{n-4p}
for each
n
.
Moreover, the differential is given by

\partial(m_{n-4p})_{p\geq 0}=(\partial_{M}m_{n-4p}-(-1)^{n}m_{n-4(p+1)}u)_{p\geq 0}.

The point of the map
\psi
should now be clear; given
m\in M_{n}

the element
(\psi^{p}(x))_{p\geq 0}\in\prod_{p\geq 0}M_{n-4p}

gives an explicit extension of
m
to a cycle in
DCI^{+}(Y)
.

Using the description of the differentials in Proposition

4.21
we note that
\psi
is given on a generator

b_{\alpha}\in H_{0}(\alpha)\subset M_{4p}
by

(6.1)

\psi(b_{\alpha})=u^{-1}\left(\sum_{\beta\in\mathcal{C}^{irr}}n_{\beta\alpha}t_{\beta}\right)=\sum_{\beta\in\mathcal{C}^{irr}}n_{\beta\alpha}b_{\beta}

(the integers
n_{\beta\alpha}
were defined in Definition
4.20
).

Recall from Lemma
5.45
that

DCI^{+}(\overline{Y}_{\Gamma})=\hat{C}_{A}^{+}(M)=\operatorname{lim}_{p}C^{+}_{A}(M/F_{p}M),

filtered by
F_{p}\hat{C}_{A}^{+}(M)=\hat{C}_{A}^{+}(F_{p}M)=\operatorname{lim}_{q<p}C^{+}_{A}(F_{p}M/F_{q}M)
. The concrete model given in Corollary
5.47
applies equally well to these subcomplexes. Recall that we use the notation

\overline{F}_{p}M=F_{p}M/F_{p-1}M
.

Lemma 6.4
.

Let
M=DCI(\overline{Y}_{\Gamma})
be equipped with the index
filtration. Then
F_{4p}M=F_{4p+t}M
for all
p\in\mathbb{Z}
and
0\leq t\leq 3
.
Moreover, there are identifications for
s\in\mathbb{Z}
and
0\leq t\leq 3

\displaystyle C^{+}_{A}(\overline{F}_{4p}M)_{4s+t}

\displaystyle\cong\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}M_{4p+t}&\mbox{ for }s\geq p\\
0&\mbox{ for }s<p\end{array}\right.

\displaystyle\hat{C}^{+}_{A}(F_{4p}M)_{4s+t}

\displaystyle\cong\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}\prod_{q\geq 0}M_{4(p-q)+t}&\mbox{ for }s\geq p\\
\prod_{q\geq 0}M_{4(s-q)+t}&\mbox{ for }s<p.\end{array}\right.

Define
\zeta\colon C^{+}_{A}(\overline{F}_{4p}M)\rightarrow\hat{C}^{+}_{A}(F_{4p}M)

for
x\in M_{4p+t}\cong C^{+}(\overline{F}_{4p}M)_{4s+t}
,
s\geq p
, by

\zeta(x)=(\psi^{q}(x))_{q\geq 0}\in\prod_{q\geq 0}M_{4(p-q)+t}=\hat{C}^{+}_{A}(F_{4p}M),

where
\psi
is the map of Lemma
6.2
, and otherwise to be zero. Then
\zeta
is a chain map splitting the
exact sequence of DG
R
-modules (i.e.,
\pi\circ\zeta=1
)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{C}^{+}_{A}(F_{4p-1}M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{C}^{+}_{A}(F_{4p}M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{+}_{A}(\overline{F}_{4p}M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\zeta}

Proof.

The fact that
F_{4p+t}M=F_{4p}M
for
0\leq t\leq 3

follows from the structure theorem
4.21
. From the same
result one concludes that
F_{4p}M_{n}=M_{n}
for
n\leq 4p+3
and

F_{4p}M_{n}=0
for
n\geq 4(p+1)
. Therefore,

\overline{F}_{4p}M_{n}
coincides with
M_{n}
for
4p\leq n\leq 4p+3
and
vanishes otherwise. Using this, the given degreewise formulas for
C^{+}_{A}(\overline{F}_{4p}M)
and
\hat{C}^{+}_{A}(F_{4p}M)
follow from Proposition
5.8
and Corollary
5.47
, respectively.

Next we have to verify that
\zeta
is a chain map. The differential
in
C^{+}_{A}(\overline{F}_{4p}M)
is given by
u\colon M_{4p}\rightarrow M_{4p+3}
in
degree
4s
for
s>p
and is otherwise
0
. The differential in

\hat{C}^{+}_{A}(F_{4p}M)
is given in degree
4s+t
by

\partial((m_{4(s-q)+t})_{q\geq 0})=(\partial_{M}m_{4(s-q)+t}-(-1)^{t}m_{4(s-q-1)+t}u)_{q\geq 0},

where if
s>p
it is assumed that
m_{4(s-q)+t}=0
for
q\leq s-p
. In view
of this formula and the fact that
\psi(x)\cdot u=(-1)^{|x|}\partial_{M}x

for all
x\in M
by Lemma
6.2
, we see that for

x\in M_{4p+t}=C^{+}_{A}(\overline{F}_{4p}M)_{4s+t}
,
s\geq p
,

\partial\circ\zeta(x)=\partial(\psi^{q}x)_{q\geq 0}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}0&\mbox{ if }(s,t)=(p,0)\mbox{ or }1\leq t\leq 3\\
(x\cdot u,0,0,\cdots)&\mbox{ if }s>p,t=0\end{array}\right.

In the first case we also have
\partial x=0
and hence
\zeta\circ\partial(x)=0
,
while in the second case we have

\partial x=x\cdot u
so that
\zeta\circ\partial(x)=(\psi^{q}(x\cdot u))_{q\geq 0}=(x\cdot u,0,0,\cdots)
as
\psi
vanishes on
\operatorname{Im}u
by
Lemma
6.2
. In all other degrees
\zeta
vanishes, so we
may conclude from the above that
\zeta
is a chain map.

Finally, the projection
\pi\colon\hat{C}^{+}_{A}(F_{4p}M)\rightarrow C^{+}_{A}(\overline{F}_{4p}M)
is in terms of the formulas of (
6.4
) given by the
projection
\prod_{q\geq 0}M_{4(p-q)+t}\rightarrow M_{4p+t}
in degree

4s+t
for
s\geq p
and otherwise
0
. From this description it is
clear that
\pi\circ\zeta=1
and the proof is complete.
∎

We need one final lemma describing the interaction between the map

\zeta
and the action of
R[U]
.

Lemma 6.5
.

In the situation of the above lemma the map

\zeta\colon C^{+}_{A}(\overline{F}_{4p}M)\rightarrow\hat{C}^{+}_{A}(F_{4p}M)
satisfies

\zeta(Ux)=U\zeta(x)
for each
x
with
|x|\neq 4p
. Moreover, in degree

4p
we have the following commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{+}_{A}(\overline{F}_{4p}M)_{4p}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{C}^{+}_{A}(F_{4p}M)_{4p}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{C}^{+}_{A}(F_{4p}M)_{4(p-1)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{+}_{A}(\overline{F}_{4(p-1)}M)_{4(p-1)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{C}^{+}_{A}(F_{4(p-1)}M)_{4(p-1)}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\zeta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\zeta}

Proof.

In degree
n
for
n<4p
the map
\zeta
vanishes, so the first
assertion is clear in this case. In degree
4s+t
with
s>p
and
0\leq t\leq 3
we have, using the formulas of Lemma
6.4
,

C^{+}_{A}(\overline{F}_{4p}M)_{4s+t}=M_{4p+t}\;\;\mbox{ and }\;\;\hat{C}^{+}_{A}(F_{4p}M)_{4s+t}=\prod_{q\geq 0}M_{4(p-q)+t}.

It then follows from Proposition
5.8
and
Corollary
5.47
that the
U
action is given by
the identity in these degrees. Therefore,
U\circ\zeta=\zeta\circ U
in this case as well.

For
x\in M_{4p+t}=C^{+}_{A}(\overline{F}_{4p}M)_{4p+t}
, where
0\leq t\leq 3
,
we have
Ux=0
, while

U(\zeta(x))=U((\psi^{q}x)_{q\geq 0})=(\psi^{q+1}x)_{q\geq 0}=(\psi^{q}(\psi x))_{q\geq 0}=\zeta(\psi(x)),

where we regard
\psi x\in M_{4(p-1)+t}=C^{+}_{A}(\overline{F}_{4(p-1)}M)_{4(p-1)+t}
.
This shows that the given diagram commutes in degree
4p+t
for

0\leq t\leq 3
. However, in the proof of Lemma
6.2

it was shown that
\psi\colon M_{4p+t}\rightarrow M_{4(p-1)+t}
vanishes for

1\leq t\leq 3
. Therefore
\zeta\circ U=U\circ\zeta
in degree

4p+t
for
1\leq t\leq 3
as well. This completes the proof.
∎

To describe
I^{+}(\overline{Y}_{\Gamma})
and later
I^{-}(\overline{Y}_{\Gamma})
, it is convenient to introduce the following definition.

Definition 6.6
.

For an
R[U]
-module
X
define the mod
8
periodic

R[U]
-modules
X^{\Pi,8}
and
X^{\oplus,8}
degreewise by

X^{\Pi,8}_{n}=\prod_{s\in\mathbb{Z}}X_{n+8s}\;\;\mbox{ and }\;\;X^{\oplus,8}_{n}=\bigoplus_{s\in\mathbb{Z}}X_{n+8s}.

The maps
U\colon X^{\Pi,8}_{n}\rightarrow X^{\Pi,8}_{n-4}
and
U\colon X^{\oplus,8}_{n}\rightarrow X^{\oplus,8}_{n-4}
are defined to be the product and direct sum over
the maps
U:X_{n+8s}\rightarrow X_{n-4+8s}
for
s\in\mathbb{Z}
, respectively.

Let
\Gamma\subset\operatorname{SU}(2)
be a finite subgroup. Given
\alpha\in\mathcal{C}

we will use the shorthand notation

H^{+}_{A}(\alpha)\coloneqq H^{+}_{A}(H_{*}(\alpha))
.
Write
\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{C}^{irr}\cup\mathcal{C}^{red}\cup\mathcal{C}^{f.red}
for the decomposition of the critical orbits into irreducible, reducibles and fully reducibles.
For each
\eta\in\mathcal{C}^{f.red}
,
\lambda\in\mathcal{C}^{red}
and
\alpha\in\mathcal{C}^{irr}
introduce variables
V_{\eta}
,
W_{\lambda}
and a generator
g_{\alpha}
so that

H^{+}_{A}(\eta)=R[V_{\eta}],\;\;\;\;H^{+}_{A}(\lambda)=R[W_{\lambda}]\;\;\mbox{ and }\;\;H^{+}_{A}(\alpha)=R\cdot g_{\alpha},

where
|V_{\eta}|=4
,
|W_{\lambda}|=2
and
|g_{\alpha}|=0
. This is justified by the calculations of Lemma
5.7
and Corollary

5.9
. In the following we regard the relative grading

j:\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}/8
as taking values in
\{0,4\}
.

Theorem 6.7
.

The positive equivariant instanton Floer homology
I^{+}(\overline{Y}_{\Gamma})
associated with the trivial
\operatorname{SU}(2)
-bundle over
\overline{Y}_{\Gamma}
is given by

\left[\left(\bigoplus_{\alpha\in\mathcal{C}^{irr}}R\cdot g_{\alpha}[j(\alpha)]\right)\oplus\left(\bigoplus_{\lambda\in\mathcal{C}^{red}}R[W_{\lambda}][j(\lambda)]\right)\oplus\left(\bigoplus_{\eta\in\mathcal{C}^{f.red}}R[V_{\eta}][j(\eta)]\right)\right]^{\Pi,8}.

The
R[U]
-module structure is determined by

\displaystyle U\cdot V^{p}_{\eta}

\displaystyle=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}V^{p-1}_{\eta}&\mbox{ for }p>0\\
\sum_{\rho\in C^{irr}}n_{\rho\eta}g_{\rho}&\mbox{ for }p=0\end{array}\right.

\displaystyle U\cdot W_{\lambda}^{p}

\displaystyle=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}W^{p-2}_{\lambda}&\mbox{ for }p>0\\
\sum_{\rho\in C^{irr}}n_{\rho\lambda}g_{\rho}&\mbox{ for }p=0\end{array}\right.

\displaystyle U\cdot g_{\alpha}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\rho\in C^{irr}}n_{\rho\alpha}g_{\rho},

where we interpret
W_{\beta}^{-1}=0
and the integers
n_{\rho_{1}\rho_{2}}

are given in Proposition
4.25
for each pair
(\rho_{1},\rho_{2})\in\mathcal{C}
.

Proof.

Let as usual
M=DCI(\overline{Y}_{\Gamma})
be equipped with
the index filtration. By Lemma
6.4
we have
F_{4p}M=F_{4p+t}M
for
0\leq t\leq 3
, and the map

\zeta\colon C^{+}_{A}(\overline{F}_{4p}M)\rightarrow\hat{C}^{+}_{A}(F_{4p}M)

along with the inclusion
i\colon\hat{C}^{+}_{A}(F_{4(p-1)}M)\rightarrow\hat{C}^{+}_{A}(F_{4p}M)
define a chain isomorphism

i+\zeta:\hat{C}^{+}_{A}(F_{4(p-1)}M)\oplus C^{+}_{A}(\overline{F}_{4p}M)\cong\hat{C}^{+}_{A}(F_{4p}M)

of
DG

R
-modules for each
p
. By induction we obtain isomorphisms

\hat{C}^{+}_{A}(F_{4p}M)\cong\hat{C}^{+}_{A}(F_{4(p-r-1)}M)\oplus\left(\bigoplus_{q=0}^{r}C^{+}_{A}(\overline{F}_{4(p-q)}M)\right)

for each
p
and
r\geq 1
. Since the filtration
F_{p}\hat{C}^{+}_{A}(M)=\hat{C}^{+}_{A}(F_{p}M)
is degreewise bounded above and complete Hausdorff, it follows
by first passing to the limit over
r
and then to the colimit over
p

that we obtain an isomorphism of DG
R
-modules

\hat{C}^{+}_{A}(M)\cong\prod_{p\in\mathbb{Z}}C^{+}_{A}(\overline{F}_{4p}M).

Hence, as products commutes with homology,

(6.6)

I^{+}(\overline{Y}_{\Gamma})=\hat{H}^{+}_{A}(M)\cong\prod_{p\in\mathbb{Z}}H^{+}_{A}(\overline{F}_{4p}M).

Write
\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{C}^{0}\cup\mathcal{C}^{1}
where

\mathcal{C}^{i}=\{\alpha\in\mathcal{C}:j(\alpha)\equiv 4i\ (\mathrm{mod}\ 8)\}
for
i=0,1
.
Then

H^{+}_{A}(\overline{F}_{4p}M)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}\bigoplus_{\alpha\in\mathcal{C}^{0}}H^{+}_{A}(\alpha)[4p]&\mbox{ if }p\equiv 0\ (\mathrm{mod}\ 2)\\
\bigoplus_{\alpha\in\mathcal{C}^{1}}H^{+}_{A}(\alpha)[4p]&\mbox{ if }p\equiv 1\ (\mathrm{mod}\ 2),\end{array}\right.

and we may simplify the expression in (
6.6
)

(6.7)

\prod_{p\in\mathbb{Z}}H^{+}_{A}(\overline{F}_{4p}M)=\prod_{p\in\mathbb{Z}}\bigoplus_{\alpha\in\mathcal{C}}H^{+}_{A}(\alpha)[8p+j(\alpha)]=\left(\bigoplus_{\alpha\in\mathcal{C}}H^{+}_{A}(\alpha)[j(\alpha)]\right)^{\Pi,8}.

By replacing each
H^{+}_{A}(\alpha)
with
R[V_{\alpha}]
,
R[W_{\alpha}]

or
R\cdot g_{\alpha}
according to whether
\alpha
is fully reducible,
reducible or irreducible we obtain the additive statement of the theorem.

To finish the proof we need to determine the action of
U
. Returning
to the notation of equation (
6.6
), for a fixed degree

n
, we have
I^{+}(\overline{Y}_{\Gamma})_{n}=\hat{H}^{+}_{A}(F_{4p}M)_{n}

for each
p
with
4p>n
. This is a consequence of the formula (
6.4
) given in Lemma
6.4
. Fix the minimal
p
with
4p>n
and consider the commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I^{+}(\overline{Y}_{\Gamma})_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{H}^{+}_{A}(F_{4p}M)_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{q:4q\leq n}H^{+}_{A}(\overline{F}_{4q}M)_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I^{+}(\overline{Y}_{\Gamma})_{n-4}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{H}^{+}_{A}(F_{4p}M)_{n-4}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{q:4q\leq n}H^{+}_{A}(\overline{F}_{4(q-1)}M)_{n-4}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}

Here,
\phi
is the isomorphism obtained by taking the limit, as

j\to\infty
, over the maps

([x_{q}])_{j\leq q\leq n/4}\mapsto\sum_{j\leq q\leq n/4}[\zeta(x_{q})],

where
x_{q}\in C^{+}_{A}(\overline{F}_{4q}M)
represents
[x_{q}]\in H^{+}_{A}(\overline{F}_{4q}M)
and
U^{\prime}
is the map that forces the right
rectangle to commute. Our task is to determine
U^{\prime}
. By Lemma
6.5

we have for
y\in H^{+}_{A}(\overline{F}_{4q}M)_{n}
that

H(\zeta)\circ U(y)=U\circ H(\zeta)(y)
provided
n>4q
, while if

n=4q
, and
y=[x]
then
U\circ H(\zeta)(y)=[\psi x]
. It follows
that
U^{\prime}=\prod U
if
n
is not divisible by
4
. Otherwise, if

4|n
so that
n=4(p-1)
, then
U^{\prime}=\tau+\prod U
, where

\tau=([x_{q}])_{q\leq p-1}=[\psi(x_{p-1})]
. Therefore, in the description

I^{+}(\overline{Y}_{\Gamma})=\prod_{p\in\mathbb{Z}}\bigoplus_{\alpha\in\mathcal{C}}H^{+}_{A}(\alpha)[8p+j(\alpha)]

of (
6.7
), the action of
U
is given by taking the product over the internal
R[U]
-module structure of each factor
H^{+}_{A}(\alpha)
and adding the correction
term
\tau
. For each
p
,
\tau
only affects the terms

H^{+}_{A}(\alpha)[8p+j(\alpha)]_{8p+j(\alpha)}=H^{+}_{A}(\alpha)_{0}
. In terms of
our generators this correspond to
V^{0}_{\alpha}
if
\alpha
is fully reducible,

W_{\alpha}^{0}
if
\alpha
is reducible and
g_{\alpha}
if
\alpha
is
irreducible. Now, we may require that these generators
correspond to
b_{\alpha}\in H_{0}(\alpha)\subset M_{4q}\cong C^{+}_{A}(\overline{F}_{4q}M)=H^{+}_{A}(\overline{F}_{4q}M)_{4q}
under the identifications of
Lemma
6.4
. Therefore, using the explicit
formula (
6.1
) for
\psi
, we obtain

U\cdot V^{0}_{\eta}=\sum_{\rho\in\mathcal{C}^{irr}}n_{\rho\eta}g_{\rho}

and similarly for the other types of generators. This completes the
proof.
∎

Example 6.8
.

For
Y=\overline{Y}_{O^{*}}
there are two irreducibles

\alpha
,
\beta
, the trivial connection
\theta
and a fully
reducible
\eta
. The grading is given by
j(\theta)=j(\beta)=0

and
j(\alpha)=j(\eta)=4
. Furthermore,
n_{\alpha\beta}=n_{\beta\alpha}=3

and
n_{\alpha\theta}=n_{\beta\eta}=1
. This is obtained from
Proposition
4.25
. The above theorem gives

I^{+}(Y)=\left(R[V_{\theta}]\oplus R\cdot g_{\beta}\oplus R[V_{\eta}][4]\oplus R\cdot g_{\alpha}[4]\right)^{\Pi,8}.

This means that

I^{+}(Y)_{n}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}R\{g_{\beta}\}\oplus\prod_{i\geq 0}R\{V_{\theta}^{2i},V_{\eta}^{2i-1}\}&n\equiv 0\ (\mathrm{mod}\ 8)\\
R\{g_{\alpha}\}\oplus\prod_{i\geq 0}R\{V_{\theta}^{2i+1},V_{\eta}^{2i}\}&n\equiv 4\ (\mathrm{mod}\ 8)\\
0&\mbox{ otherwise. }\end{array}\right.

For
x=(r_{2i}V^{2i}_{\eta})_{i\geq 0}\in I^{+}(Y)_{4}
,
r_{2i}\in R
, we have

U\cdot x=r_{0}g_{\alpha}+(r_{2i}V^{2i-1}_{\eta})_{i\geq 1}\in I^{+}(Y)_{0}
.
For
x=rg_{\alpha}
,
r\in R
, we have
U\cdot x=3rg_{\beta}
.

6.2.
The Case
I^{-}

In this section we will calculate
I^{-}(\overline{Y}_{\Gamma})
for all
finite subgroups
\Gamma\subset\operatorname{SU}(2)
. In contrast to the
calculations in the previous section, there will be a number of nontrivial
differentials in the index spectral sequence. Nevertheless, the spectral sequence will still stabilize after a finite number of steps in each
case.

Lemma 6.9
.

Let
\Gamma\subset\operatorname{SU}(2)
be a finite subgroup. Then
the only possibly nontrivial differentials in the index spectral sequence

E^{1}_{s,t}=\bigoplus_{j(\alpha)\equiv s}H^{-}_{A}(\alpha)_{t}\implies I^{-}(\overline{Y}_{\Gamma})_{s+t}

are
d^{4r}\colon E^{4r}_{4(s+r),-4(r-1)}\rightarrow E^{4r}_{4s,3}
for
s
and
r\geq 1
.

Proof.

As
j(\alpha)\equiv 0\mod{4}
for each
\alpha\in\mathcal{C}
,
we have
E^{1}_{s,*}=0
unless
4|s
. By Lemma
5.7
and
Corollary
5.9
the
R[U]
-module
H^{-}_{A}(\alpha)

is given by
R[U]
with
|U|=-4
,
R[Z][2]
with
|Z|=-2
or
R[3]
depending on whether
\alpha
is fully reducible, reducible or irreducible, respectively. This means that
E^{1}_{4s,t}=0
for
t\geq 4
and
odd
t\leq 2
. Since the differential
d^{r}
has bidegree
(-r,r-1)

we deduce that it can only be nonzero if it lands in the bidegree

(4s,3)
for some
s
. The only such differentials that also begin
in a possibly nonzero group are
d^{4r}\colon E^{4r}_{4(s-r),-4(r-1)}\rightarrow E^{4r}_{4s,3}
. ∎

Our first task will be to give an explicit formula for the nontrivial
differentials.
Fix a finite subgroup
\Gamma\subset\operatorname{SU}(2)
and let

M=DCI(\overline{Y}_{\Gamma})
be equipped with the index filtration.
Recall that by Lemma
5.45

DCI^{-}(\overline{Y}_{\Gamma})=\operatorname{colim}_{p}C^{-}_{A}(F_{p}M)=\bigcup_{p}C^{-}_{A}(F_{p}M).

In the following we will use the fact from Lemma
6.4
that
F_{4p+t}M=F_{4p}M
for all
p\in\mathbb{Z}
and
0\leq t\leq 3

without further mention.

Lemma 6.10
.

For all
p,s\in\mathbb{Z}
and
0\leq t\leq 3
we have

\displaystyle C^{-}_{A}(F_{4p}M)_{4s+t}

\displaystyle\cong\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}\bigoplus_{0\leq q\leq p-s}M_{4(s+q)+t}&\mbox{ if }s\leq p\\
0&\mbox{ if }s>p\end{array}\right.

\displaystyle C^{-}_{A}(\overline{F}_{4p}M)_{4s+t}

\displaystyle\cong\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}M_{4p+t}&\mbox{ if }s\leq p\\
0&\mbox{ if }s>p\end{array}\right.

The differential in
C^{-}_{A}(\overline{F}_{4p}M)
is given by

u\colon M_{4p}\rightarrow M_{4p+3}
in degree
4s
with
s\leq p
, and vanishes otherwise.
The differential in
C^{-}_{A}(F_{4p}M)
is given by

\partial((x_{q})_{0\leq q\leq p-s})=(\partial_{M}x_{q}-(-1)^{t}x_{q-1}u)_{0\leq q\leq p-s+1}

for
(x_{q})_{0\leq q\leq p-s}\in\bigoplus_{0\leq q\leq p-s}M_{4(s+q)+t}=C^{-}_{A}(F_{4p}M)_{4s+t}
where
s\leq p
, and we interpret
x_{-1}=0=x_{p-s+1}
.

Proof.

This is a simple consequence of Proposition
5.8
using the fact that
F_{4p}M_{n}=M_{n}
for
n\leq 4p+3
and

F_{4p}M_{n}=0
otherwise. ∎

For each
\eta\in\mathcal{C}^{f.red}
,
\lambda\in\mathcal{C}^{red}
and
\alpha\in\mathcal{C}^{irr}
we introduce variables
U_{\eta},Z_{\lambda}
and a generator

h_{\alpha}
. Then, according to Lemma
5.7
and
Corollary
5.9
,

H^{-}_{A}(\eta)=R[U_{\eta}],\;\;\;\;H^{-}_{A}(\lambda)=R[Z_{\lambda}][2]\;\;\mbox{ and }\;\;H^{-}_{A}(\alpha)=R\cdot h_{\alpha}

where
|U_{\eta}|=-4
,
|Z_{\lambda}|=-2
and
|h_{\alpha}|=3
.

Lemma 6.11
.

Let
\{E^{r},d^{r}\}_{r\geq 1}
be the index spectral sequence with

E^{1}_{s,t}=\bigoplus_{j(\alpha)\equiv s}H^{-}_{A}(\alpha)_{t}\implies I^{-}_{s+t}(\overline{Y}_{\Gamma}).

Then, for each
r\geq 1
and
s
,
E^{4r}_{4s,-4(r-1)}=E^{1}_{4s,-4(r-1)}

is a free
R
-module with generators

\{U^{r-1}_{\eta}:\eta\in\mathcal{C}^{f.red}\ni j(\eta)\equiv 4s\}\cup\{Z^{2r-1}_{\lambda}:\lambda\in\mathcal{C}^{red}\ni j(\lambda)\equiv 4s\}

and
E^{1}_{4s,3}
is a free
R
-module with generators

\{h_{\alpha}:\alpha\in\mathcal{C}^{irr}\ni j(\alpha)\equiv 4s\}
.
Let
p_{r}:E^{1}_{4s,3}\rightarrow E^{4r}_{4s,3}
denote the natural surjection. Then in terms of these generators the differential
d^{r}\colon E^{4r}_{4(s+r),-4(r-1)}\rightarrow E^{4r}_{4s,3}
is determined by

\displaystyle d^{4r}(U^{r-1}_{\eta})

\displaystyle=p_{r}\left(\sum_{(\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{r})}n_{\alpha_{1}\eta}\left(\prod_{i=1}^{r-1}n_{\alpha_{i+1}\alpha_{i}}\right)\cdot h_{\alpha_{r}}\right)

\displaystyle d^{4r}(W^{2r-1}_{\lambda})

\displaystyle=p_{r}\left(\sum_{(\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{r})}n_{\alpha_{1}\lambda}\left(\prod_{i=1}^{r-1}n_{\alpha_{i+1}\alpha_{i}}\right)\cdot h_{\alpha_{r}}\right),

where the sums are taken over tuples
(\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{r})\in(\mathcal{C}^{irr})^{r}
for which
(\eta,\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{r})
, respectively

(\lambda,\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{r})
, form a path in the
graph
\mathcal{S}_{\Gamma}
(see Definition
3.9
).

Proof.

Before we introduce the explicit generators we will work
out a formula for the differentials in terms of the complex
M
.
By Lemma
6.9
we have

E^{4r}_{4(s+r),-4(r-1)}=E^{1}_{4(s+r),-4(r-1)}=H^{-}_{A}(\overline{F}_{4(s+r)}M)_{4(s+1)}
and by Lemma
6.10
the latter group may be identified with

\operatorname{Ker}(u)\subset M_{4(s+r)}
.
Recall from diagram (
5.15
) that the differential
d^{4r}

is defined by first applying the connecting homomorphism

\delta\colon H^{-}_{A}(\overline{F}_{4(s+r)}M)\rightarrow H^{-}_{A}(F_{4(s+r)-1}M)
, then lifting along the map

H^{-}_{A}(F_{4s}M)\rightarrow H^{-}_{A}(F_{4(s+r)-1}M)
and then pushing down along

H^{-}_{A}(F_{4s}M)\rightarrow H^{-}_{A}(\overline{F}_{4s}M)
. On the chain level
we have the following diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{-}_{A}(\overline{F}_{4(s+r)}M)_{4(s+1)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{-}_{A}(F_{4(s+r)-1}M)_{4s+3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{-}_{A}(F_{4s}M)_{4s+3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{4(s+r)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigoplus_{q=0}^{r-1}M_{4(s+q)+3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{4s+3}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}

The arrows correspond to the connecting homomorphism
\delta
are
dashed as they are not well-defined before passing to homology.
Given
x\in\operatorname{Ker}u\subset M_{4(s+r)}
the
element
\delta(x)
is represented on the chain level by

(0,0,\cdots,0,\partial_{M}x)
. As
\iota
is the inclusion of the first summand,
this element does not lift along
\iota
. Let
\psi:M\rightarrow M
be the map
of Lemma
6.2
and let

y\coloneqq(\psi^{r-q}x)_{q=1}^{r-1}\in\bigoplus_{q=1}^{r-1}M_{4(s+q)}\cong C^{-}_{A}(F_{4(s+r-1)}M)_{4(s+1)}.

Then
\partial(y)=(\partial_{M}\psi^{r-1}x,0,0,\cdots,0,-\partial_{M}x)
, which implies
that
\iota[\partial_{M}\psi^{r-1}x]=[(0,\cdots,0,\partial_{M}x)]
in homology. By
Lemma
6.10
we have the following sequence of
isomorphisms

M_{4s+3}\cong C^{-}_{A}(F_{4s}M)_{4s+3}\cong C^{-}_{A}(\overline{F}_{4s}M)_{4s+3}\cong H^{-}_{A}(\overline{F}_{4s}M)_{4s+3}\cong E^{1}_{4s,3}.

Moreover, according to Lemma
6.9
, there
are no outgoing differentials from
E^{r}_{4s,3}
for
r\geq 1
. We conclude that

d^{4r}(x)
is given by the image of
\partial_{M}\psi^{r-1}x
along
the surjection
E^{1}_{4s,3}\rightarrow E^{4r}_{4s,3}
for each

x\in E^{4r}_{4(s+r),-4(r-1)}\subset M_{4(s+r)}
. In other words, we have the
following commutative diagram

(6.8)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E^{4r}_{4(s+r),-4(r-1)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E^{1}_{4(s+r),-4(r-1)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Ker}(u)\subset M_{4(s+r)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E^{4r}_{4s,3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E^{1}_{4s,3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{4s+3}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d^{4r}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial_{M}\circ\psi^{(r-1)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{r}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

Now,
\operatorname{Ker}u\subset M_{4(s+r)}
is freely generated by

b_{\eta}\in H_{0}(\eta)
,
b_{\lambda}\in H_{0}(\lambda)
for
\eta\in\mathcal{C}^{f.red}
and
\lambda\in\mathcal{C}^{red}
with
j(\eta)\equiv j(\lambda)\equiv 4(s+r)
. It is important to note that under the top horizontal identification in
(
6.8
) we may, and will, require that these
correspond to the generators
U^{r-1}_{\eta}
and
W^{2r-1}_{\lambda}
in
E^{1}_{4(s+r),-4(r-1)}
. Similarly,
M_{4s+3}
is freely generated by
t_{\alpha}=b_{\alpha}\cdot u\in H_{3}(\alpha)

for
\alpha\in\mathcal{C}^{irr}
with
j(\alpha)\equiv 4s
,
and under the lower horizontal isomorphism we require that these
correspond to the generators
h_{\alpha}\in E^{1}_{4s,3}
.
Recall from equation (
6.1
) that

\psi(b_{\eta})=u^{-1}\partial_{M}(b_{\eta})=\sum_{\alpha\in\mathcal{C}^{irr}}u^{-1}(n_{\alpha\eta}t_{\alpha})=\sum_{\alpha\in\mathcal{C}^{irr}}n_{\alpha\eta}b_{\alpha}.

and similarly for
\psi(b_{\lambda})
. Taking into account the
fact that
n_{\alpha\beta}=0
whenever
\alpha
and
\beta
are not adjacent
in the graph
\mathcal{S}_{\Gamma}
, we obtain

\partial_{M}\psi^{r-1}(b_{\eta})=\sum_{(\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{r})}(n_{\alpha_{1}\eta}n_{\alpha_{2}\alpha_{2}}\cdots n_{\alpha_{r}\alpha_{r-1}})\cdot t_{\alpha_{r}},

where the sum is taken over
(\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{r})\in(\mathcal{C}^{irr})^{r}

for which
(\eta,\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{r})
forms an edge path in

\operatorname{S}_{\Gamma}
. An analogous formula holds for
b_{\lambda}
, so in view
of diagram (
6.8
) the proof is complete.
∎

By combining the above lemma with Theorem
4.25
we obtain
complete control over all the differentials in the spectral sequences.
The following result takes care of all the extension problems we will meet.

Lemma 6.12
.

Let
\Gamma\subset\operatorname{SU}(2)
be a finite subgroup and
let
(E^{r},d^{r})_{r\geq 1}
be the index spectral sequence with

E^{1}_{s,t}=\bigoplus_{j(\alpha)\equiv s}H^{-}_{A}(\alpha)_{t}\implies I^{-}(\overline{Y}_{\Gamma})_{s+t}.

Assume that
E^{\infty}_{s}
is a free
R[U]
-module for

s=0,4
. Then there is an isomorphism of
R[U]
-modules

I^{-}(\overline{Y}_{\Gamma})\cong(E^{\infty}_{0}\oplus E^{\infty}_{4})^{\oplus,8}.

Proof.

Recall that the index spectral sequence is periodic
in the sense that
E^{r}_{s,t}\cong E^{r}_{s+8,t}
, or equivalently
E^{r}_{s}[8]\cong E^{r}_{s+8}
, for all
s,t\in\mathbb{Z}
and
r\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}
. Therefore,
the assumption that
E_{0}^{\infty}
and
E_{4}^{\infty}
are free
R[U]
-modules
implies that
E^{\infty}_{s}
is a free
R[U]
-module for each
s\in\mathbb{Z}
, as
E^{1}_{s}=0
for all
s
not divisible by
4
.

Write
I=I^{-}(\overline{Y}_{\Gamma})
. By Theorem
5.58

(E^{r},d^{r})_{r\geq 1}
is a spectral sequence
of
R[U]
-modules that converges strongly to
I
. In other words,

I
carries an exhaustive and complete Hausdorff filtration

\{F_{s}I\}_{s}
of
R[U]
-submodules and there is an isomorphism
E^{\infty}_{s}\cong\overline{F}_{s}I
of
R[U]
-modules for each
s\in\mathbb{Z}
. In view
of the fact that
E^{\infty}_{s}
is free over
R[U]
, the short exact
sequence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{s-1}I}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{s}I}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{F}_{s}I\cong E^{\infty}_{s}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{s}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{s}}

splits in the category of
R[U]
-modules for each
s\in\mathbb{Z}
. Hence,
F_{s-1}I\oplus\overline{F}_{s}I\cong F_{s}I
and inductively

F_{s-1}I\oplus\left(\bigoplus_{t=0}^{r}\overline{F}_{s+t}I\right)\cong F_{s+r}I

for
s\in\mathbb{Z}
and
r\geq 1
. These are all isomorphisms of
R[U]
-modules.
By Lemma
6.10
we have
E^{1}_{s,t}=0
for all

s
and
t\geq 4
. Therefore, for fixed
n\in\mathbb{Z}
,
(E^{1}_{s})_{n}=E^{1}_{s,n-s}=0

for all
s\leq n-4=:s_{0}
. Hence,
(\overline{F}_{s}I)_{n}=0
for all
s\leq s_{0}
.
Since the filtration of
I
is complete Hausdorff it follows that

(F_{s}I)_{n}=0
for all
s\leq s_{0}
. Consequently, using the above isomorphisms,
we obtain

I_{n}=\operatorname{colim}_{s\geq s_{0}}(F_{s}I)_{n}\cong\operatorname{colim}_{s\geq s_{0}}\bigoplus_{i=s_{0}}^{s}(\overline{F}_{s}I)_{n}\cong\bigoplus_{s}(E^{\infty}_{s})_{n}

for each
n\in\mathbb{Z}
. These isomorphisms piece together to an isomorphism

I\cong\bigoplus_{s}E^{\infty}_{s}
of
R[U]
-modules. Finally, we may exploit the periodicity of
E^{\infty}

and the fact that
E^{\infty}_{s}=0
unless
4|s
to simplify:

I\cong\bigoplus_{s}E^{\infty}_{s}\cong\bigoplus_{s}(E^{\infty}_{8s}\oplus E^{\infty}_{8s+4})\cong\bigoplus_{s}(E^{\infty}_{0}[8s]\oplus E^{\infty}_{4}[8s])=(E^{\infty}_{0}\oplus E^{\infty}_{4})^{\oplus,8}

and the proof is complete.
∎

It is not possible to give a uniform result for the calculation of
I^{-}(\overline{Y}_{\Gamma})
for all the subgroups
\Gamma\subset\operatorname{SU}(2)
simultaneously.
We will therefore treat the simplest cases
\Gamma=I^{*},O^{*},T^{*},C_{m}
first and treat the more involved case of binary dihedral groups afterwards.

We have the following table over the flat connections extracted from
Appendix
A
. The notation is compatible with Proposition
4.25
.

\begin{array}[]{|c|c|c|c|}\hline\cr\Gamma&\mathcal{C}^{f.red}&\mathcal{C}^{irr}&\mathcal{C}^{red}\\
\hline\cr C_{2m}&\theta,\eta&&\lambda_{1},\cdots,\lambda_{m-1}\\
\hline\cr C_{2m+1}&\theta&&\lambda_{1},\cdots,\lambda_{m}\\
\hline\cr I^{*}&\theta&\alpha,\beta&\\
\hline\cr O^{*}&\theta,\eta&\alpha,\beta&\\
\hline\cr T^{*}&\theta&\alpha&\lambda\\
\hline\cr\end{array}

Theorem 6.13
.

The negative equivariant instanton Floer homology
associated with the trivial
\operatorname{SU}(2)
-bundle over
\overline{Y}_{\Gamma}

for
\Gamma=C_{2m},C_{2m+1}
is given by

\displaystyle I^{-}(\overline{Y}_{C_{2m}})

\displaystyle=\left((R[U_{\theta}]\oplus R[U_{\eta}][4m]\oplus\bigoplus_{i=1}^{m-1}R[Z_{\lambda_{i}}][4i+2]\right)^{\oplus,8}

\displaystyle I^{-}(\overline{Y}_{C_{2m+1}})

\displaystyle=\left(R[U_{\theta}]\oplus\bigoplus_{i=1}^{m}R[Z_{\lambda_{i}}][4i+2]\right)^{\oplus,8},

while for
\Gamma=I^{*},O^{*},T^{*}
we have
I^{-}(\overline{Y}_{\Gamma})=(X_{\Gamma})^{\oplus,8}
where
X_{\Gamma}
is the
R[U]
-submodule
of
P_{\Gamma}
generated by
G_{\Gamma}
specified in the following table.

\begin{array}[]{|c|c|c|}\hline\cr\Gamma&P_{\Gamma}&G_{\Gamma}\\
\hline\cr I^{*}&R[U_{\theta}]&\{U_{\theta}^{2}\}\\
\hline\cr O^{*}&R[U_{\theta}]\oplus R[U_{\eta}][4]&\{U_{\theta}^{1},U_{\eta}^{1}\}\\
\hline\cr T^{*}&R[U_{\theta}]\oplus R[Z_{\lambda}][2]&\{U_{\theta}^{1},Z_{\lambda}^{0},3U_{\theta}^{0}-Z^{1}_{\lambda}\}\\
\hline\cr\end{array}

Proof.

For
\Gamma=I^{*},O^{*},T^{*},C_{n}
we will write
\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{C}_{\Gamma}

for the set of flat connections and
(E^{r},d^{r})_{r\geq 1}
will denote the corresponding index spectral sequence with

E^{1}_{s,t}=\bigoplus_{j(\alpha)\equiv s}H^{-}_{A}(\alpha)_{t}\implies I^{-}(\overline{Y}_{\Gamma})_{s+t}.

In each case we will calculate
E^{\infty}
and observe that
E^{\infty}_{0}

and
E^{\infty}_{4}
are free
R[U]
-modules. By Lemma
6.12

this is enough to conclude that
I^{-}(\overline{Y}_{\Gamma})=(E_{0}^{\infty}\oplus E_{4}^{\infty})^{\oplus,8}
. In the following we will make consistent use
of Lemma
6.9
, Theorem
4.25
and Lemma
6.11
that together determine all the
differentials explicitly.

\mathbf{C_{n}}
. As
\mathcal{C}^{irr}=\emptyset
there are no nontrivial
differentials in the spectral sequence so that
E^{1}=E^{\infty}
. For
n=2m
we have
\mathcal{C}=\{\theta,\eta,\lambda_{1},\cdots,\lambda_{m-1}\}
with
\theta
,
\eta
fully reducible and the
\lambda_{i}
reducible. The grading is given by
j(\lambda_{i})\equiv 4i
and
j(\eta)\equiv 4m
. This gives

E^{\infty}_{0}\oplus E^{\infty}_{4}=R[U_{\theta}]\oplus R[U_{\eta}][j(\eta)]\oplus\left(\bigoplus_{i=1}^{m-1}R[Z_{\lambda_{i}}][j(\lambda_{i})+2]\right).

For
n=2m+1
we have
\mathcal{C}=\{\theta,\lambda_{1},\cdots,\lambda_{m}\}
with

\theta
fully reducible and the
\lambda_{i}
reducible. The grading is
given by
j(\lambda_{i})\equiv 4i
. Hence,

E^{\infty}_{0}\oplus E^{\infty}_{4}=R[U_{\theta}]\oplus\left(\bigoplus_{i=1}^{m}R[Z_{\lambda_{i}}][j(\lambda_{i})+2]\right).

In both cases these are free
R[U]
-modules (
R[Z_{\lambda}]
is freely generated by
\{Z_{\lambda}^{0},Z_{\lambda}^{1}\}
). The stated results are obtained by applying

X\mapsto X^{\oplus,8}
to the above formulas for
E_{0}^{\infty}\oplus E^{\infty}_{4}
.

\mathbf{I^{*}}
. In this case we have
\mathcal{C}=\{\theta,\alpha,\beta\}
with

\alpha
and
\beta
irreducible. The grading is given by
j(\theta)=j(\beta)=0
and
j(\alpha)=4
. The first nontrivial differential in the index spectral sequence is

d^{4}\colon E^{4}_{8s,0}=R\{U^{0}_{\theta}\}\rightarrow R\{h_{\alpha}\}=E^{4}_{8s-4,3}

and is given by
d^{4}(U^{0}_{\theta})=n_{\alpha\eta}h_{\alpha}=h_{\alpha}
. Therefore,
E^{\infty}_{8s+4,3}=0=E^{\infty}_{8s,0}
. The next differential is

d^{8}\colon E^{8}_{8s,-4}=R\{U_{\theta}^{1}\}\rightarrow R\{h_{\beta}\}=E^{8}_{8(s-1),3}

and is given by
d^{8}(U^{1}_{\theta})=n_{\beta\alpha}n_{\alpha\eta}h_{\beta}=4h_{\beta}
. As
2\in R
is invertible, this is an isomorphism and we conclude that
E^{\infty}_{8s,3}=0
and
E^{\infty}_{8s,-4}=0
. There are no more nontrivial
differentials in the spectral sequence. Therefore,
E^{\infty}_{4}=0
and
E^{\infty}_{0}\subset R[U_{\theta}]
is the free
R[U]
-submodule generated by
U_{\theta}^{2}
as required.

\mathbf{O^{*}}
. Here we have
\mathcal{C}=\{\theta,\eta,\alpha,\beta\}
with

\theta
,
\eta
fully reducible and
\alpha,\beta
irreducible. The grading
is
j(\theta)=j(\beta)=0
and
j(\alpha)=j(\eta)=4
. The first nontrivial
differentials are

\displaystyle d^{4}\colon

\displaystyle E^{4}_{8s+4,0}=R\{U^{0}_{\eta}\}\rightarrow R\{h_{\beta}\}=E^{4}_{8s,3}

\displaystyle d^{4}\colon

\displaystyle E^{4}_{8s,0}=R\{U^{0}_{\theta}\}\rightarrow R\{h_{\alpha}\}=E^{4}_{8s-4,3}.

These are both isomorphisms as
n_{\beta\eta}=1=n_{\alpha\theta}
. This implies
that
E^{\infty}_{8s,3}=E^{5}_{8s,3}=0
and
E^{\infty}_{8s-4,3}=E^{5}_{8s-4,3}=0
. There can therefore be no more nontrivial differentials.
Hence,
E^{\infty}_{0}\subset R[U_{\theta}]
and
E^{\infty}_{4}\subset R[U_{\eta}][4]

are the free
R[U]
-submodules generated by
U_{\theta}^{1}
and
U_{\eta}^{1}
,
respectively.

\mathbf{T^{*}}
. In this case
\mathcal{C}=\{\theta,\alpha,\lambda\}
with

\theta
fully reducible,
\alpha
irreducible and
\lambda
reducible.
The grading is
j(\theta)=j(\lambda)=0
and
j(\alpha)=4
. The first
nontrivial differential is
d^{4}\colon E^{4}_{8s,0}=R\{Z_{\lambda}^{1},U_{\theta}^{0}\}\rightarrow R\{h_{\alpha}\}=E^{4}_{8s-4,3}
and is determined by

d^{4}(U^{0}_{\theta})=n_{\alpha\theta}h_{\alpha}=h_{\alpha}
and

d^{4}(Z^{1}_{\lambda})=n_{\alpha\lambda}h_{\alpha}=3h_{\alpha}
. This map
is surjective with kernel
R\{3U^{0}_{\theta}-Z^{1}_{\lambda}\}
. Therefore,

E^{\infty}_{8s-4,3}=E^{4}_{8s-4,3}=0
and there are no more nontrivial differentials. We find
E^{\infty}_{4}=0
,
(E^{\infty}_{0})_{0}=R\{3U^{0}_{\theta}-Z^{1}_{\lambda}\}

and
(E^{\infty}_{0})_{n}=(R[U_{\theta}]\oplus R[Z_{\lambda}][2])_{n}
in all other
degrees
n
. It now suffices to observe that
E^{\infty}_{0}
is indeed the free
submodule of
R[U_{\theta}]\oplus R[Z_{\lambda}][2]
generated by

\{Z_{\lambda}^{0},3U^{0}_{\theta}-Z^{1}_{\lambda},U_{\theta}^{1}\}
. This completes
the final case and the proof. ∎

We will now consider the binary dihedral groups
D_{m}^{*}
. It is necessary to
partition the calculations into cases depending on the residue of
m

mod
4
. We have the following table over the flat connections

\begin{array}[]{|c|c|c|c|}\hline\cr&\mathcal{C}^{f.red}&\mathcal{C}^{irr}&\mathcal{C}^{red}\\
\hline\cr D_{4n}^{*}&\theta,\eta_{1},\eta_{2},\eta_{3}&\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{2n}&\\
\hline\cr D_{4n+1}^{*}&\theta,\eta&\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{2n}&\lambda\\
\hline\cr D_{4n+2}^{*}&\theta,\eta_{1},\eta_{2},\eta_{3}&\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{2n+1}&\\
\hline\cr D_{4n+3}^{*}&\theta,\eta&\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{2n+1}&\lambda\\
\hline\cr\end{array}

in agreement with Appendix
A
and Proposition
4.25
.
In all cases the grading of the irreducibles are given by

j(\alpha_{i})\equiv 4i\mod{8}
. For
D^{*}_{4n+2}
and
D^{*}_{4n+3}
all
the fully reducibles and reducibles satisfy
j(\rho)=0
. For

D^{*}_{4n}
we have
j(\theta)=j(\eta_{1})=0
,
j(\eta_{2})=j(\eta_{3})=4
, while
for
D^{*}_{4n+1}
we have
j(\theta)=j(\eta)=0
and
j(\lambda)=4
. For
the convenience of the reader we include the relevant diagrams for

S_{D^{*}_{m}}
from Proposition
4.25
.

\mathcal{S}_{D^{*}_{2m}}
\alpha_{1}
\alpha_{2}
\alpha_{m-1}
\alpha_{m}
\theta
\eta_{1}
\eta_{2}
\eta_{3}
(2|2)
(2|2)
1
